# Research Log (perishable facts and their sources)

Every perishable fact used in the curriculum is logged here with its source, the date it was checked, and a confidence note. Chapters cite these entries by ID (e.g., `[R-003]`) in their **Sources** section. When a fact is re-checked, add a new dated line instead of deleting the old one.

Confidence tags follow the curriculum convention: **[Established]** (stable, multiple primary sources), **[Industry estimate]** (credible but approximate or single-source), **[Contested]** (sources disagree, so the range is shown).

## R-001 · NCEES FE Electrical and Computer specification

- **Spec in force:** the CBT specification "effective beginning with the July 2020 examinations". It has 110 questions in 17 knowledge areas. Source: [NCEES FE E&C CBT specification (PDF)](https://ncees.org/wp-content/uploads/FE-Electrical-and-Computer-CBT-specs.pdf). NCEES's 2025 outreach deck also describes the spec as "Updated in 2020" ([FEOAT 2025 deck](https://ncees.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/05/FEOAT-PPT-2025.pdf)).
- **Question ranges:** Mathematics 11–17 · Probability & Statistics 4–6 · Ethics & Professional Practice 4–6 · Engineering Economics 5–8 · Properties of Electrical Materials 4–6 · Circuit Analysis 11–17 · Linear Systems 5–8 · Signal Processing 5–8 · Electronics 7–11 · Power Systems 8–12 · Electromagnetics 4–6 · Control Systems 6–9 · Communications 5–8 · Computer Networks 4–6 · Digital Systems 8–12 · Computer Systems 5–8 · Software Engineering 4–6.
- **Software Engineering (4–6 questions) subtopics,** quoted from the spec: "A. Algorithms (e.g., sorting, searching, complexity, big-O) B. Data structures (e.g., lists, trees, vectors, structures, arrays) C. Software implementation (e.g., iteration, conditionals, recursion, control flow, scripting, testing)"; Computer Systems (5–8): "A. Microprocessors B. Memory technology and systems C. Interfacing" (PDF read Nov 2026 via the NCEES FE exam page) [Established].
- **Appointment:** about 6 h in total, of which 5 h 20 min is exam time. The exam is closed-book with the electronic *NCEES FE Reference Handbook*. Errata confirm version 10.4 was in use from July 2024. Some prep sites say 10.6 applies from July 2026, but this was **not verified on ncees.org**.
- **Newer spec announced?** None found as of Nov 2026. The Nov 2026 NCEES Examinee Guide still describes the FE as 110 questions, year-round.
- **Confidence:** high for most areas. Medium for Linear Systems, Signal Processing, Electronics and Communications, which were confirmed via secondary sources citing the spec. **Open issue:** ncees.org was unreachable from the build environment, so the PDF should be re-checked directly.

## R-002 · IEEE 802.11 EVM requirements

- **802.11ax, 1024-QAM (MCS 10/11):** transmit relative constellation error (EVM) ≤ **−35 dB** [Established]. Some test-equipment notes cite −32 dB depending on amplitude-drift compensation; that footnote is ambiguous. Source: [LitePoint EVM application note (2021)](https://www.litepoint.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/01/EVM-App-Notes-010621-web.pdf).
- **802.11be, 4096-QAM (MCS 12/13):** ≤ **−38 dB** [Established]. Source: [Rohde & Schwarz application note 1EF114](https://cdn.rohde-schwarz.com.cn/pws/dl_downloads/dl_application/application_notes/1ef114/1EF114_1e_802_11be_EVM.pdf), which quotes the TGbe draft (clause 36.3.19.4.1).
- **802.11be:** maximum channel width **320 MHz**, in the 6 GHz band only. The standard was approved by the IEEE SA Board on 26 Sep 2024 and published as **IEEE 802.11be-2024** on 22 Jul 2025. Source: [IEEE SA 802.11be page](https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/802.11be/7516/).

## R-003 · 3GPP NR frequency ranges

- **Ranges:** FR1 = 410–7125 MHz; FR2-1 = 24 250–52 600 MHz; FR2-2 = 52 600–71 000 MHz [Established].
- **Source:** 3GPP TS 38.104 Table 5.1-1, [ETSI TS 138 104 V19.2.0 (2025-10)](https://www.etsi.org/deliver/etsi_ts/138100_138199/138104/19.02.00_60/ts_138104v190200p.pdf). The same values appear in V19.6.0 (2026-09). NTN bands (TS 38.101-5) use different ranges.

## R-004 · On-chip interconnect

- **Wire capacitance:** "Typical dense wires have ~0.2 fF/µm", compared with 1–2 fF/µm for gate capacitance [Established rule of thumb]. Source: D. Harris, *CMOS VLSI Design* 4th ed. lecture slides, [Lecture 14 "Wires"](https://pages.hmc.edu/harris/cmosvlsi/4e/lect/lect14.pdf).
- **Research-stage wire resistance:** imec reported semi-damascene Ru lines at **16 nm metal pitch** with average resistance **as low as 656 Ω/µm**, positioned for local metal at the A7 node and beyond [Industry estimate, research result, not production]. Source: [imec press release, 3 Jun 2025 (IITC 2025)](https://www.imec-int.com/en/press/imec-demonstrates-16nm-pitch-ru-lines-record-low-resistance-obtained-using-semi-damascene). No public Ω/µm figure was found for production M0 layers.

## R-005 · Synchrophasor standards

- **Measurement:** IEC/IEEE 60255-118-1:2018 (supersedes IEEE C37.118.1-2011/1a-2014; a revision project (PAR) was approved in Nov 2025, not yet a new edition). **Data transfer:** IEEE C37.118.2-2024. Sources: [IEEE SA 60255-118-1](https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/60255-118-1/5724/), [IEEE SA C37.118.2](https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/C37.118.2/7077/).
- **Reporting rates (C37.118.1-2011 Table 1):** 50 Hz systems use 10/25/50 frames/s; 60 Hz systems use 10/12/15/20/30/60 frames/s. The 20 frames/s rate in the 2018 edition is **unverified**.

## R-006 · Power-grid frequencies

- **Japan:** eastern Japan runs at 50 Hz and western Japan at 60 Hz, with the boundary near the Fuji River (Shizuoka). The split dates from early generator purchases (German machines for Tokyo, US machines for Osaka) [Established]. Sources: [J-POWER](https://www.jpower.co.jp/english/ir/library/pdf/2011/2011b.pdf); [IEEE Milestone: Sakuma Frequency Converter Station, 1965](https://ethw.org/Milestones:Sakuma_Frequency_Converter_Station,_1965).
- **Other countries:** see [IEC World Plugs](https://www.iec.ch/world-plugs). Most of the Americas use 60 Hz; most of Europe, Africa, Asia and Oceania use 50 Hz.

## R-007 · US 6 GHz unlicensed band

- **Allocation:** FCC 20-51 (adopted 23 Apr 2020) opened **5.925–7.125 GHz** (1200 MHz, U-NII-5 to U-NII-8) for unlicensed use [Established]. Sources: [FCC 20-51](https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-20-51A1.pdf), [FCC announcement](https://www.fcc.gov/document/fcc-opens-6-ghz-band-wi-fi-and-other-unlicensed-uses-0).
- **Later rules:** very-low-power devices were allowed in 2023 and extended to the whole band in 2025.
- **Power limits (access points):** low-power indoor (LPI) **30 dBm EIRP** maximum and **5 dBm/MHz** PSD, indoor only (clients 6 dB lower, 24 dBm and −1 dBm/MHz); standard power **36 dBm EIRP** and **23 dBm/MHz**, permitted only under Automated Frequency Coordination (AFC) control, in U-NII-5 and U-NII-7 (clients 30 dBm and 17 dBm/MHz) [Established]. Because the PSD limit is fixed, LPI EIRP grows with channel width: 5 dBm/MHz allows 27 dBm in a 160 MHz channel and reaches the 30 dBm cap only at 320 MHz. A 2020 further notice proposed raising the LPI PSD to 8 dBm/MHz; the FCC's 2025 tables still list 5 dBm/MHz. Sources: [FCC 20-51](https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-20-51A1.pdf) (§15.407 rules); [FCC DA 25-362](https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-25-362A1.pdf) (2025 table of device classes and limits); [Federal Register, 28 May 2020](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2020-05-28/pdf/2020-11320.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-008 · SAR ADC settling

- **Requirement:** settling a full-scale step to within ½ LSB of an N-bit converter needs ln(2^(N+1)) = (N+1)·ln 2 time constants, which is 11.78τ for 16 bits [Established]. Sources: [TI SBAA535A](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/sbaa535a/sbaa535a.pdf); A. Walsh, ["Front-End Amplifier and RC Filter Design for a Precision SAR ADC", *Analog Dialogue* 46-12 (2012)](https://www.analog.com/en/resources/analog-dialogue/articles/front-end-amp-and-rc-filter-design.html), which gives N_TC = ln(V_step / ½ LSB).

## R-009 · kT/C noise

- **Formula and value:** v_n,rms = √(kT/C), which gives ≈ 64 µV rms for 1 pF at 300 K (64.4 µV computed) [Established]. Source: UC Berkeley EECS 247 Lecture 9 (Khorramabadi), ["expected 64 µV" for 1 pF](https://hasler.ece.gatech.edu/Courses/ECE3400/Proj4/Berkeley_Switch_Cap_Filters.pdf).

## R-010 · Rise time and bandwidth

- **Relation:** t_r(10–90 %) ≈ 0.35 / BW [Established]. It is exact for a single-pole response (ln 9/(2π) = 0.3497). Higher-order "brick-wall-like" scope responses give 0.4–0.45. Source: [Tektronix, *ABCs of Probes*](https://download.tek.com/document/02_ABCs-of-Probes-Primer.pdf).

## R-011 · Complex multiplication with three real multiplications

- **Method:** (a + jb)(c + jd) can be computed from ac, bd and (a + b)(c + d), so only 3 real multiplications are needed; Winograd proved 3 is optimal [Established]. Source: N. J. Higham, "Stability of a method for multiplying complex matrices with three real matrix multiplications," *SIAM J. Matrix Anal. Appl.* 13(3):681–687, 1992, doi:10.1137/0613043. Higham cites Knuth, *TAOCP* Vol. 2, which attributes the method to P. Ungar (1963). The common "Gauss trick" attribution appears only in secondary sources.

## R-012 · Desktop CPU maximum boost clocks

- **Current flagships, as of Nov 2026:** AMD Ryzen 9 9950X3D up to 5.7 GHz (amd.com); Intel Core Ultra 9 285K max turbo 5.7 GHz (Intel ARK).
- **Highest rated stock boost on sale:** Intel Core i9-14900KS, 6.2 GHz (Intel ARK, launched Mar 2024) [Established].
- **Next generation:** reports of ~7 GHz parts are rumor only [Contested].

## R-013 · ABET curriculum minimums and semester-credit workload

- **ABET minimums:** the ABET *Criteria for Accrediting Engineering Programs* (2025–2026), Criterion 5 (Curriculum), require at least **30 semester credit hours** of college-level mathematics and basic sciences and at least **45 semester credit hours** of engineering topics [Established]. Source: [ABET criteria 2025–2026](https://www.abet.org/accreditation/accreditation-criteria/criteria-for-accrediting-engineering-programs-2025-2026/).
- **Credit-hour workload:** traditionally, one semester credit = one hour of class plus about two hours of outside work per week for about 15 weeks, ≈ 45 hours. Source: e.g., [Washington and Lee credit definition](https://www.wlu.edu/university-registrar/policies-and-procedures/undergraduate-credit-definition), based on the long-standing federal definition in 34 CFR 600.2. That federal definition was amended in 2025 and its current wording was not verified, so this is used only as an approximate convention [Industry estimate].

## R-014 · Nominal mains voltages

- **IEC 60038 (50 Hz systems):** the preferred low-voltage values are 230 V single-phase and 230/400 V three-phase (phase-to-neutral / phase-to-phase), with supply at the connection point expected within ±10%. These replaced the older 220/380 V and 240/415 V systems [Established]. Sources: [IEC 60038 summary (Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IEC_60038); [VDE preview of IEC 60038 ed. 7.0](https://www.vde-verlag.de/iec-normen/preview-pdf/info_iec60038%7Bed7.0%7Db.pdf).
- **North America (60 Hz):** the nominal residential voltage is 120 V single-phase (120/240 V split-phase service), under ANSI C84.1 [Established]. The ANSI tolerance bands were not verified against the standard text. Source: [IEC 60038 summary (Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IEC_60038), which also lists the 60 Hz values.
- Checked Nov 2026 via secondary sources; neither standard's text was consulted directly.

## R-015 · Lithography exposure field (the "reticle limit")

- **Standard scanners (DUV and 0.33-NA EUV):** the maximum exposure field is **26 mm × 33 mm ≈ 858 mm²**, which caps the size of a single die printed in one exposure [Established].
- **High-NA EUV (0.55 NA):** anamorphic 4×/8× optics halve the field to **26 mm × 16.5 mm ≈ 429 mm²**. Larger dies must be stitched from two exposures or split into chiplets. Larger (6 × 12 inch) masks are being pursued by ASML and chipmakers to restore the full field [Established for the field size; mask transition timing is an industry plan].
- **Largest single dies:** NVIDIA's GH100 (H100) die is widely reported at about 814 mm² with about 80 billion transistors on TSMC 4N, close to the field limit; its predecessor GA100 was about 826 mm² [Industry estimate: from NVIDIA's Hopper launch materials as reproduced by trade press; the primary whitepaper was not fetched from the build environment]. Sources: [TechPowerUp GH100 database](https://www.techpowerup.com/gpu-specs/nvidia-gh100.g1011); [Notebookcheck, H100 launch](https://www.notebookcheck.net/Nvidia-unveils-H100-Hopper-compute-GPU-and-Grace-superchip-architectures.609352.0.html).
- **Sources:** [IEEE Spectrum, "This Machine Could Keep Moore's Law on Track"](https://spectrum.ieee.org/high-na-euv); [Tom's Hardware on 6×12-inch High-NA photomasks](https://www.tomshardware.com/tech-industry/semiconductors/tsmc-samsung-and-intel-shore-up-support-with-asml-to-deploy-larger-high-na-euv-photomasks-6-12-inch-photomask-transition-may-take-years-despite-unified-effort). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-018 · Functional verification study (Wilson Research Group / Siemens EDA)

- **IC/ASIC first-silicon success:** 5% in the 2026 study (released 8 Sep 2026; the recruitment method changed, which limits comparison) and 14% in 2024. It was about 30–33% from 2010–2020 and 24% in 2022 [Established as reported by the study].
- **Share of project time in verification:** 49% (IC/ASIC, 2026); earlier studies 53–57%.
- **Schedule:** about 75% of IC/ASIC projects were behind schedule (2024 and 2026).
- **Verification vs design engineers:** about a 1:1 mean peak headcount ratio (2022 study), with verification headcount growing faster (6.2% vs 2.7% CAGR, 2007–2022).
- **Respins:** "logic or functional flaws" are the #1 cause of respins. Exact 2024 and 2026 percentages are UNVERIFIED.
- **Sources:** [Siemens 2026 study blog](https://blogs.sw.siemens.com/verificationhorizons/2026/09/08/the-2026-functional-verification-study/); [2024 report PDF](https://static.sw.cdn.siemens.com/siemens-disw-assets/public/1NyfMZJ3nuPhMGQCGFDITG/en-US/ic-asic-functional-verification-trend-report-wp-86424-d3.pdf); [SemiEngineering, "First-Time Silicon Success Plummets"](https://semiengineering.com/first-time-silicon-success-plummets/); [2022 study, part 8](https://blogs.sw.siemens.com/verificationhorizons/2022/12/12/part-8-the-2022-wilson-research-group-functional-verification-study/).

## R-019 · Advanced-node design and mask cost estimates [Contested]

- **IBS design-cost estimates** (from-scratch designs with no IP reuse):
  - c. 2021: 28 nm ≈ \$40 M, 7 nm ≈ \$217 M, 5 nm ≈ \$416 M, 3 nm up to ≈ \$590 M.
  - Arm's 2023 F-1 prospectus, citing IBS (Jul 2022): 7 nm ≈ \$249 M, 2 nm ≈ \$725 M, of which software, verification and IP qualification are about 71%.
  - Earlier 2018 estimates were higher, e.g. 5 nm ≈ \$542 M.
- **Critique:** industry critics (SemiAnalysis, 2022, second-hand) argue IBS figures run 50–66% above typical real-world costs.
- **Mask sets:** reported at roughly \$10–30 M at 3 nm, from secondary sources only (UNVERIFIED).
- **Source:** [Arm F-1](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1973239/000119312523216983/d393891df1.htm).

## R-020 · Fab cycle time and mask/EUV layer counts

- **Days per mask layer:** fabs average about 1–1.5 days per mask layer (best about 0.8). 28 nm used about 40–50 mask layers and 7 nm about 80–85 (SemiEngineering, ["Battling Fab Cycle Times"](https://semiengineering.com/battling-fab-cycle-times), 2017) [Industry estimate].
- **Wafer cycle time:** about 12 weeks on average and 14–20 weeks for advanced processes, across 400–1,400 process steps ([BCG × SIA, Apr 2021](https://www.semiconductors.org/wp-content/uploads/2021/05/BCG-x-SIA-Strengthening-the-Global-Semiconductor-Value-Chain-April-2021_1.pdf)) [Industry estimate].
- **EUV layers (secondary reports):** about 14 at TSMC N5 and about 19–25 at N3 variants. Total mask counts at N3/N2 are UNVERIFIED.

## R-021 · Learning-science evidence

- **Testing effect:** Roediger & Karpicke (2006), "Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention," *Psychological Science* 17(3), 249–255. Restudy wins at 5 minutes, but testing wins at 2 days and 1 week. One-week recall in Experiment 2 was 61% (STTT) vs 40% (SSSS) [Established].
- **Spacing effect:** Cepeda, Pashler, Vul, Wixted & Rohrer (2006), "Distributed practice in verbal recall tasks: A review and quantitative synthesis," *Psychological Bulletin* 132(3), 354–380. Across 839 assessments, spaced practice beats massed practice, and the optimal gap grows with the retention interval [Established].
- **Interleaving:** Rohrer & Taylor (2007), "The shuffling of mathematics problems improves learning," *Instructional Science* 35, 481–498. One week later, mixed practice scored 63% vs blocked 20%, despite worse performance during practice (60% vs 89%) [Established].
- **Technique review:** Dunlosky, Rawson, Marsh, Nathan & Willingham (2013), *Psychological Science in the Public Interest* 14(1), 4–58. **High utility:** practice testing and distributed practice. **Moderate:** elaborative interrogation, self-explanation and interleaved practice. **Low:** summarization, highlighting, keyword mnemonic, imagery and rereading [Established].

- **First-time takers from ABET programs within 12 months of graduation:** 70% (Jan–Jun 2026 cohort; NCEES table updated July 2026, n = 1,782) and 64% (Jul–Dec 2025 cohort, via PPI).

- **LM358 / LM358A:** typical slew rate 0.3 V/µs and gain–bandwidth 0.7 MHz. **LM358B / LM358BA:** 0.5 V/µs and 1.2 MHz (revision history of the TI datasheet) **[Established]**.

The lower HGX rating is attributed to air-cooling limits in some reports **[Industry estimate: OEM and analyst documents rather than NVIDIA's own datasheet]**.

## R-027 · Subthreshold and near-threshold operation

- **Minimum-energy point.** In subthreshold CMOS, energy per operation has a minimum at a supply of a few hundred millivolts, where falling switching energy (∝ V²) is balanced by rising leakage energy (leakage acts over a longer, exponentially slower cycle). See B. H. Calhoun, A. Wang and A. Chandrakasan, "Modeling and sizing for minimum energy operation in subthreshold circuits," *IEEE JSSC*, vol. 40, no. 9, pp. 1778–1786, 2005 **[Established]**.
- **Commercial use.** Ambiq's SPOT (Subthreshold Power Optimized Technology) platform runs digital logic below 0.5 V. Its first Apollo MCUs (Cortex-M4F, 24 MHz, launched 2015) were quoted by the company at 30 µA/MHz active and about 100 nA standby, with claims of 5–10× lower energy than competing MCUs **[vendor claim; treat comparisons as Contested]**. Later generations add a higher-frequency "turboSPOT" mode.
- **Sources:** [Ambiq SPOT platform page](https://www.ambiq.com/spot); [Electronic Design, "Q&A: Salas on the SPOT in Low-Power Design"](https://www.electronicdesign.com/technologies/power/article/21800639/qa-salas-on-the-spot-in-low-power-design); [eeNews Europe, Apollo launch (2015)](https://www.eenewseurope.com/en/arm-mcu-built-in-sub-threshold-technology-for-10-fold-power-reduction/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-028 · Electricity-meter accuracy classes (IEC 62053-21)

- **Standard:** IEC 62053-21:2020 (edition 2.0, published June 2020) covers static meters for AC active energy in 50 Hz and 60 Hz networks, in accuracy classes **0.5, 1 and 2** (nominally 0.5%, 1% and 2%). It replaced the 2003 edition and its 2016 amendment and moved the safety requirements to IEC 62052-31. Europe adopted it as EN IEC 62053-21:2021. A third edition was reported to be in development [Established].
- **Sources:** [IEC webstore, IEC 62053-21:2020](https://webstore.iec.ch/publication/28660); [IECEE listing](https://www.iecee.org/certification/iec-standards/iec-62053-212020). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-029 · JEDEC thermal test environments (JESD51-2A)

- **Standard:** JEDEC JESD51-2A, *Integrated Circuits Thermal Test Method Environmental Conditions — Natural Convection (Still Air)*, January 2007. The junction-to-ambient thermal resistance θJA is measured in a sealed enclosure of low-conductivity, high-emissivity walls with a nominal interior of 305 mm × 305 mm × 305 mm, with the package mounted on standardized test boards (JESD51-3 and JESD51-7 for surface-mount packages) [Established].
- **Caveat:** vendors themselves note that θJA describes this test environment, not an end product, and that it is a poor predictor of junction temperature in a real system whose heat paths differ [Established].
- **Sources:** [Richtek AN061, "Application and Definition of Thermal Resistances on Datasheet"](https://www.richtek.com/m/Design%20Support/Technical%20Document/AN061?sc_lang=en); [Diodes Incorporated, "Understanding thermal resistance and characterization parameters in datasheets"](https://www.diodes.com/zh/design/support/perspective/understanding-thermal-resistance-and-characterization-parameters-in-datasheets); JESD51-2A listing at a standards reseller. The JEDEC document itself was not fetched. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-030 · 3DMark stress tests and frame-rate stability

- **Method:** UL's 3DMark stress tests run a benchmark scene in a loop and report **frame-rate stability**, the average frame rate of the worst loop divided by that of the best loop, as a percentage. A run passes at **97% or more** with all loops completed. Loop counts and durations differ between test editions (for example about 20 loops, or about 20 minutes for the Wild Life Extreme stress test) [Established].
- **Sources:** UL support article ["Stress Test result screen"](https://support.benchmarks.ul.com/support/solutions/articles/44002134931-stress-test-result-screen) and UL news ["Using 3DMark to measure sustained performance"](https://benchmarks.ul.com/news/using-3dmark-to-measure-sustained-performance). UL's sites did not resolve from the build environment, so these details were taken from search-result excerpts of those pages. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-031 · Thermal runaway in integrated circuits

- **Paper:** A. Vassighi and M. Sachdev, "Thermal runaway in integrated circuits," *IEEE Transactions on Device and Materials Reliability*, vol. 6, no. 2, pp. 300–305, June 2006, DOI 10.1109/TDMR.2006.876577. Rising standby leakage raises junction temperature, which raises leakage further; under burn-in conditions the loop can run away. The paper describes the triggering conditions and the thermal management needed to avoid them. The same material appears in the authors' book *Thermal and Power Management of Integrated Circuits* (Springer, 2006) [Established].
- **Sources:** [IEEE record (via document index)](https://manuscript.isc.ac/Inventory/49/1125305.htm); [Springer book page](https://link.springer.com/book/10.1007/0-387-29749-9). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-032 · PDN target impedance method

- **Paper:** L. D. Smith, R. E. Anderson, D. W. Forehand, T. J. Pelc and T. Roy (Sun Microsystems), "Power distribution system design methodology and capacitor selection for modern CMOS technology," *IEEE Transactions on Advanced Packaging*, vol. 22, no. 3, pp. 284–291, Aug. 1999, DOI 10.1109/6040.784476. The method identifies a target impedance over a broad frequency range and chooses the regulator, bulk and ceramic capacitors, modeled in SPICE, so that the network stays below it [Established].
- **Formula in common use:** Z_target = V_DD × allowed ripple fraction / transient current step, as restated in vendor application notes [Established].
- **Sources:** [Scilit record](https://scilit.com/publications/0ab4248b497aba5db9060887e1451436); [MPS application note, "Meeting the impedance mask: strategies for PDN design and optimization"](https://media.monolithicpower.cn/mps_cms_document/a/i/aip_meeting-the-impedance-mask_strategies-for-pdn-design-and-optimization_r1.0.pdf). The IEEE full text was not fetched. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-033 · Quartz crystal resonator Q

- **Values:** a high-stability 5 MHz quartz crystal unit has a Q of about 2–3 million; for AT- and SC-cut resonators the maximum Q × f is about 16 million with f in MHz, so Q falls with frequency. Quartz's low intrinsic loss is why its Q is so high [Established].
- **Source:** J. R. Vig, *Quartz Crystal Resonators and Oscillators for Frequency Control and Timing Applications — A Tutorial* (U.S. Army CECOM; several revisions, e.g. 2008), [copy hosted by Pletronics](https://www.pletronics.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/Vig_tutorial.pdf) and [DTIC report ADA254892](https://apps.dtic.mil/sti/pdfs/ADA254892.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-034 · NFC and ISO/IEC 14443: carrier and data rates

- **Values:** ISO/IEC 14443 proximity cards (Types A and B) and readers use a **13.56 MHz** carrier. The base bit rate is **106 kbit/s** in each direction; the card answers by load modulation on a **847.5 kHz subcarrier (f_c/16)**. Higher rates are defined as divisions of the carrier: f_c/64 ≈ 212 kbit/s, f_c/32 ≈ 424 kbit/s, f_c/16 ≈ 848 kbit/s [Established].
- **Sources:** [ISO/IEC 14443 summary (Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO/IEC_14443); [ISO/IEC JTC1/SC17/WG8 ballot draft, 14443-2 PDAM 5.2](https://wg8.de/wg8n1897_17n4509_Ballot_2ndCD_14443-2_PDAM5.2_VHBR_PSK.pdf) (bit rates as carrier divisions); [NXP MFRC522 reader datasheet](https://datasheetq.com/pdf-html/903893-2009/NXP/10page/MFRC522_.html) (106–848 kbit/s with a 13.56 MHz/16 subcarrier). The published standard itself was not fetched; drafts and a datasheet were. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-035 · 32.768 kHz tuning-fork crystals: Q and start-up

- **Values:** typical tuning-fork crystal Q of **40,000–70,000** (Maxim/Dallas Application Note 58); some SMD parts are specified at a minimum Q of 20,000. A properly designed oscillator with a suitable crystal **usually starts within one second**; high series resistance (ESR) and excessive load capacitance are the main causes of slow start-up (AN58), and Microchip's AN2648 also lists high Q and weak amplifier drive [Industry estimate: vendor design guidance, two vendors].
- **Sources:** [Maxim/Dallas Application Note 58, "Crystal Considerations for Dallas Real-Time Clocks"](https://www.ercankoclar.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/Application-Note-58-Crystal-Considerations-for-Dallas-Real-Time-Clocks.pdf); [Analog Devices design note, "Crystal Considerations with Analog Devices Real-Time Clocks"](https://www.analog.com/en/resources/design-notes/crystal-considerations-with-analog-devices-realtime-clocks-rtcs.html); [Microchip AN2648, "Selecting and Testing 32 kHz Crystal Oscillators for AVR MCUs"](https://www.microchip.com/content/dam/mchp/documents/MCU08/ApplicationNotes/ApplicationNotes/AN2648-Selecting_Testing-32KHz-Crystal-Osc-for-AVR-MCUs-00002648.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-036 · S-domain blocks in design tools: Verilog-AMS and ngspice

- **Verilog-AMS:** the Accellera *Verilog-AMS Language Reference Manual*, version 2.4.0 (30 May 2014), §4.5.11 "Laplace transform filters", defines analog operators that "implement lumped linear continuous-time filters": `laplace_zp` (zero-pole form; zeros and poles given as real/imaginary pairs), `laplace_zd` (zero-denominator), `laplace_np` (numerator-pole) and `laplace_nd` (numerator-denominator; polynomial coefficients listed from the constant term upward), each with an optional tolerance argument [Established].
- **ngspice:** the XSPICE code-model library shipped with ngspice includes `s_xfer`, an "s-domain transfer function block" whose parameters are numerator and denominator polynomial coefficients (`num_coeff`, `den_coeff`) plus a frequency-scaling parameter (`denormalized_freq`), so it represents rational transfer functions only. Confirmed from the ngspice-42 code-model library (`analog.cm`) and by running the 02-05 lab netlist [Established].
- **Sources:** [Verilog-AMS LRM 2.4.0 (copy hosted by the Designer's Guide Community)](https://designers-guide.org/verilog-ams/VlogAMS-2.4.0.pdf), also available from Accellera; ngspice-42 as packaged for Ubuntu (`analog.cm`, model `s_xfer`). The ngspice website (ngspice.sourceforge.io) blocked the build environment, so its manual chapter on XSPICE code models was not fetched. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-037 · KLU and sparse solvers in circuit simulation

- **KLU:** T. A. Davis and E. Palamadai Natarajan, "Algorithm 907: KLU, a direct sparse solver for circuit simulation problems," *ACM Transactions on Mathematical Software*, vol. 37, no. 3, article 36, pp. 36:1–36:17, Sept. 2010, DOI 10.1145/1824801.1824814. KLU permutes the matrix to block triangular form, applies a fill-reducing ordering to each block, and factors with a Gilbert–Peierls left-looking sparse LU. The KLU user guide explains why: circuit matrices are typically permutable into block triangular form with many 1-by-1 blocks, plus a handful of dense rows and columns (from voltage and current sources) that fall outside the diagonal blocks and cause no fill-in. Fill-in is so low that solvers built on dense BLAS kernels (SuperLU, UMFPACK) are slower on these matrices [Established].
- **ngspice:**
  - ngspice-42 (24 Dec 2023) introduced a "new optional matrix solver KLU (select by 'option klu')".
  - From ngspice-43 (14 Jul 2024), `klu` is among the standard `./configure` options, so it is compiled in by default.
  - In the current source, the default task settings set the KLU mode to off (`TSKkluMODE = CKTkluOFF`), so the long-standing Sparse 1.3 solver stays the default unless KLU is selected.
  - The ngspice-42 build used for this curriculum reports "Compiled with KLU Direct Linear Solver" [Established].
- **Sources:** [Crossref record](https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1145/1824801.1824814); [KLU user guide source and bibliography (SuiteSparse repository)](https://github.com/DrTimothyAldenDavis/SuiteSparse/tree/dev/KLU/Doc); [ngspice NEWS](https://sourceforge.net/p/ngspice/ngspice/ci/master/tree/NEWS?format=raw) and source file `src/spicelib/analysis/cktntask.c`. The ACM full text returned HTTP 403, so the KLU rationale comes from the authors' user guide rather than the paper body. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-038 · HPL and the TOP500 list

- **What HPL solves:** "a (random) dense linear system in double precision (64 bits) arithmetic", using a right-looking LU factorization with row partial pivoting (netlib HPL page). TOP500 rules require the algorithm to "conform to LU factorization with partial pivoting" with an operation count of 2/3 n³ + O(n²), which excludes fast matrix-multiplication algorithms such as Strassen's. HPL's reference test code counts 2/3 N³ − 1/2 N² flops for the factorization plus 2N² for the solve [Established].
- **List of November 2026:** the 68th TOP500 list was published 17 Nov 2026 in Chicago. No. 1 is **LineShine** at the National Supercomputing Centre in Shenzhen (NSCS), China: 13,789,440 cores, **Rmax 2,198.40 PFlop/s**, **Rpeak 2,735.82 PFlop/s**. It entered the list at No. 1 in June 2026, displacing El Capitan, now No. 2 at 1,809.00 PFlop/s [Established].
- **Sources:** [HPL (netlib)](https://www.netlib.org/benchmark/hpl/); [TOP500 Linpack rules](https://top500.org/project/linpack/); [HPL_pdtest.c](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/icl-utk-edu/hpl/main/testing/ptest/HPL_pdtest.c); [TOP500 November 2026](https://top500.org/lists/top500/2026/11/) and [the full list](https://top500.org/lists/top500/list/2026/11/). The November 2026 figures postdate the author's training data and were read directly from top500.org. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-039 · Google TPU v1 matrix unit

- **Values:** the first-generation TPU's matrix unit "contains 256x256 MACs that can perform 8-bit multiply-and-adds" (65,536 in all), uses systolic execution "to save energy", runs at a 700 MHz clock with 28 MiB of on-chip memory, and "offers a peak throughput of 92 TeraOps/second (TOPS)" [Established].
- **Source:** N. P. Jouppi, C. Young, N. Patil, D. Patterson et al., "In-Datacenter Performance Analysis of a Tensor Processing Unit," *Proc. 44th International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA '17)*, Toronto, June 2017, DOI 10.1145/3079856.3080246; [arXiv:1704.04760](https://arxiv.org/abs/1704.04760). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-040 · NVIDIA H100 and Rubin: arithmetic and memory figures

- **H100 SXM** (NVIDIA product page): FP64 34 TFLOPS; FP64 Tensor Core 67 TFLOPS; FP16 and BF16 Tensor Core **1,979 TFLOPS "with sparsity"**; GPU memory bandwidth **3.35 TB/s** (80 GB). NVIDIA's Hopper architecture article says structured sparsity doubles the throughput of standard Tensor Core operations, so the dense FP16 figure is about **989.5 TFLOPS**. That is this curriculum's halving; NVIDIA's page does not print a dense number [Established].
- **Rubin GPU** (NVIDIA Vera Rubin NVL72 page, per GPU): FP16/BF16 **4 PFLOPS**, footnoted "Dense specification"; **288 GB HBM4 at 19.2 TB/s**; FP64 33 TFLOPS. NVIDIA labels these figures as projected and subject to change, and press reports (seen in search snippets only) cite an earlier 22 TB/s bandwidth figure [Established as published; vendor-projected].
- **Sources:** [NVIDIA H100](https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/h100/); [NVIDIA Hopper architecture in depth](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-hopper-architecture-in-depth/); [NVIDIA Vera Rubin NVL72](https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/vera-rubin-nvl72/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-041 · 5G NR MIMO layers

- **Values:** in 3GPP NR Release 15, a PDSCH (downlink data channel) can carry up to **8 layers**, using two codewords for 5–8 layers (TS 38.211 clause 7.3.1.3, Table 7.3.1.3-1). A PUSCH (uplink data channel) carries a single codeword mapped onto up to **4 layers** (TS 38.211 clause 6.3.1.3). Release 18 extends the PUSCH maximum to **8 layers**, with new 8-port codebooks and two codewords for more than four layers [Established].
- **Sources:**
  - Ericsson authors, ["5G NR: The Next Generation Wireless Access Technology" overview, arXiv:1806.06898](https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.06898), based on TS 38.211 V15.1.0: "A PDSCH can have two codewords to support up to 8-layer transmission"; "PUSCH supports a single codeword that can be mapped up to 4 layers".
  - [NTT DOCOMO Technical Journal Vol. 26 No. 2 (Release 18)](https://www.docomo.ne.jp/english/corporate/technology/rd/technical_journal/bn/vol26_2/004.html): "Up to Rel-17, specifications were established for PDSCH transmission with a maximum of 8 MIMO layers and PUSCH transmission with a maximum of four MIMO layers. In Rel-18 … the maximum number of layers for PUSCH transmission is extended to 8."
  - TS 38.211 clause text was read on a third-party mirror (itecspec.com). 3gpp.org and etsi.org blocked the build environment, so the official specification files were not opened.
- Checked Nov 2026.

## R-042 · Wi-Fi spatial streams: 802.11ax and 802.11be

- **Values:** IEEE 802.11ax (Wi-Fi 6) supports up to **8 spatial streams**. IEEE 802.11be-2024 (Wi-Fi 7) also supports up to **8**: "802.11be and 802.11bn-compliant devices shall support up to 8 streams" (survey by Geraci et al.). The 802.11be Specification Framework Document (11-19/1262r23, January 2021) had set "a maximum of 16 spatial streams for SU-MIMO", but that target was not carried into the approved amendment. A Cisco Meraki guide: "limited to 8, like the previous generation. The IEEE committee initially considered having support for 16 spatial streams, but there is no provision for that…". The IEEE SA lists 802.11be Board Approval on **26 Sep 2024** and publication on **22 Jul 2025** [Established].
- **Sources:** [Geraci et al., arXiv:2507.09613](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.09613); [IEEE SA, 802.11be-2024](https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/802.11be/7516/); IEEE 802.11 document 11-19/1262r23 (TGbe SFD); Cisco Meraki Wi-Fi 7 technical guide; MathWorks WLAN Toolbox documentation for 802.11ax (`wlanHESUConfig`: 1 to 8 space-time streams). The IEEE standard text itself was not opened (paywalled). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-043 · LoRA: low-rank adaptation

- **Values:** E. J. Hu, Y. Shen, P. Wallis, Z. Allen-Zhu, Y. Li, S. Wang, L. Wang and W. Chen, "LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models," ICLR 2022, arXiv:2106.09685. Abstract: "Compared to GPT-3 175B fine-tuned with Adam, LoRA can reduce the number of trainable parameters by 10,000 times and the GPU memory requirement by 3 times." The paper's GPT-3 experiments use ranks r = 1, 2, 4, 8 and 64 [Established].
- **Sources:** [arXiv:2106.09685](https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.09685); [ICLR 2022 poster page](https://iclr.cc/virtual/2022/poster/6319). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-044 · GPS: four satellites and least squares

- **Values:** "To estimate the three position coordinates and the receiver time offset, a GPS receiver needs to track at least 4 satellites", and since a receiver typically observes more than four, "GPS positioning … works on the principle of least squares estimation", after linearizing the observation model. The U.S. government's GPS site states that the 24-slot constellation "ensures users can view at least four satellites from virtually any point on the planet" [Established].
- **Sources:** [P. F. de Bakker, GPS course reader, TU Delft CTB3310, 2017](https://filelist.tudelft.nl/CiTG/Over%20faculteit/Afdelingen/Geoscience%20%26%20Remote%20sensing/Study/gpsreader.pdf); [gps.gov, Space Segment](https://www.gps.gov/space-segment). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-045 · 5G NR Type II CSI and eigenvector feedback

- **Values:** in 3GPP TS 38.214, clause 5.2.2.2.3 "Type II Codebook", the UE reports a precoder built as a weighted combination of L orthogonal DFT beams (the number of beams, `numberOfBeams`, and the phase alphabet, `phaseAlphabetSize`, are configured), with amplitude and phase coefficients. "The UE shall not report RI > 2" for Type II. For the Release 16 Enhanced Type II codebook (clause 5.2.2.2.5), "The UE shall not report υ>4". Vendor authors (Samsung and Huawei, in Jin et al.) describe Type II as intended "to facilitate refined resolution quantized channel eigenvector feedback, which is tailored for spatially multiplexing data to multiple UEs", "intentionally limited to a maximum of rank two in Release 15" and "later extended to rank four in Release 16" [Established].
- **Sources:**
  - TS 38.214 V17.2.0 text, read from a third-party copy (Clemson University project files); 3gpp.org and etsi.org blocked the build environment, and the Release 15 edition itself was not opened.
  - [Jin et al., "Massive MIMO Evolution Towards 3GPP Release 18," arXiv:2210.08218](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.08218); its journal publication was not confirmed.
  - [Ning et al., arXiv:2601.05092](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05092) (preprint): "Under ideal feedback, the beamforming vector would be the dominant eigenvector of the channel covariance."
- Checked Nov 2026.

## R-046 · Yield-curve factors: Litterman and Scheinkman

- **Finding:** R. Litterman and J. Scheinkman, "Common Factors Affecting Bond Returns," *Journal of Fixed Income*, 1(1), pp. 54–61, June 1991, DOI 10.3905/jfi.1991.692347. Most of the variation in Treasury returns is explained by three factors, interpreted as **level, steepness and curvature** [Established].
- **Method description is [Contested]:** Bliss (1997, Federal Reserve Bank of Atlanta *Economic Review*) describes the method as factor analysis; Pérignon and Villa (2006) and Hautsch and Ou (2009) describe it as principal components. The original article was not read, and a precise share of variance for the three factors was not verified, so the curriculum states only "most".
- **Sources:** [Crossref record](https://api.crossref.org/works/10.3905/jfi.1991.692347); [Bliss 1997 (Kellogg-hosted copy)](https://www.kellogg.northwestern.edu/faculty/mcdonald/ftp/d67/Bliss_termstructure.pdf); lecture slides (J. Pan, SAIF) for the factor names. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-047 · PageRank as an eigenvector

- **Values:** S. Brin and L. Page, "The anatomy of a large-scale hypertextual Web search engine," *Computer Networks and ISDN Systems*, 30(1–7), pp. 107–117, April 1998, DOI 10.1016/S0169-7552(98)00110-X: "PageRank or PR(A) can be calculated using a simple iterative algorithm, and corresponds to the principal eigenvector of the normalized link matrix of the web"; "We usually set d to 0.85" [Established, historical].
- **Note:** as printed, the paper's formula PR(A) = (1 − d) + d Σ … sums to the number of pages rather than 1, despite the paper's statement that the PageRanks form a probability distribution; normalized versions are standard in textbooks.
- **Sources:** [Google Research archive copy](https://research.google.com/pubs/archive/334.pdf); [Stanford SNAP course copy](https://snap.stanford.edu/class/cs224w-readings/Brin98Anatomy.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-048 · Eckart-Young theorem

- **Citation:** C. Eckart and G. Young, "The approximation of one matrix by another of lower rank," *Psychometrika*, 1(3), pp. 211–218, September 1936, DOI 10.1007/BF02288367. The truncated SVD is the best low-rank approximation; the 2-norm error statement used in 02-08 is the standard form given in Strang and in Trefethen and Bau [Established].
- **Source:** [Springer record](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF02288367). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-049 · Foster and Cauer thermal models

- **Values:** Infineon Application Note AN2015-10, "Transient thermal measurements and thermal equivalent circuit models," V1.2 (2020-04-14; replaces AN2008-03), §2.1: $Z_{th}(t) = \sum_{i=1}^n r_i(1 - e^{-t/\tau_i})$ with $\tau_i = r_ic_i$. On the Cauer (continued-fraction, ladder) model: it "reflects the real, physical setup of the semiconductor… The network nodes therefore allow access to the internal temperatures of the layer sequence." On the Foster (partial-fraction) model: "The network nodes do not have any physical significance. This illustration is used in datasheets, as the coefficients can be easily extracted from a measured cooling curve." Converting Foster to Cauer "is not unambiguous" [Established].
- **Source:** [Infineon AN2015-10 (Infineon community copy)](https://community.infineon.com/gfawx74859/attachments/gfawx74859/cnigbt/53/1/Infineon-Thermal_equivalent_circuit_models-ApplicationNotes-v01_02-EN.pdf). Other vendors' equivalent notes were not checked. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-050 · Matrix exponential algorithms

- **Values:** C. Moler and C. Van Loan, "Nineteen Dubious Ways to Compute the Exponential of a Matrix," *SIAM Review* 20(4):801–836, 1978, DOI 10.1137/1020098; and "…Twenty-Five Years Later," *SIAM Review* 45(1):3–49, 2003, DOI 10.1137/S00361445024180. Of scaling and squaring: "When properly implemented, the resulting algorithm is one of the most effective we know." The conclusions decline to name a single best method ("Answering that question is very risky"). The 2003 paper quotes MATLAB's help text, "EXPM is computed using a scaling and squaring algorithm with a Pade approximation". SciPy's `scipy.linalg.expm` documentation states that it implements the Al-Mohy and Higham (2009) scaling-and-squaring Padé algorithm [Established].
- **Sources:** Crossref records; [1978 paper (university-hosted copy)](https://msvlab.ntou.edu.tw/grades/Engineering-Mathematics-100-2/19ways-1978.pdf); [2003 paper (preprint copy)](https://www.cs.jhu.edu/~misha/ReadingSeminar/Papers/Moler03.pdf); SciPy 1.18 docstring, read locally. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-051 · Kalman filter and battery gauging models

- **Values:** R. E. Kalman, "A New Approach to Linear Filtering and Prediction Problems," *Transactions of the ASME, Journal of Basic Engineering* 82(1):35–45, March 1960, DOI 10.1115/1.3662552. From the abstract: "The filtering problem is shown to be the dual of the noise-free regulator problem." Texas Instruments SLUAB20A (2025), on battery fuel gauges: "Many battery gauge algorithms use an equivalent circuit model for the battery" [Established]. No vendor document stating that a specific commercial gauge uses a Kalman filter was found, so the curriculum does not claim one.
- **Sources:** Crossref record; [retyped copy (Yale)](https://www.cs.yale.edu/homes/yry/readings/general/Kalman1960.pdf); [TI SLUAB20A](https://www.ti.com/lit/pdf/sluab20). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-052 · State-space sequence models: S4 and Mamba

- **Values:**
  - **S4:** A. Gu, K. Goel and C. Ré, "Efficiently Modeling Long Sequences with Structured State Spaces," ICLR 2022 (arXiv:2111.00396). It uses the continuous model $x' = Ax + Bu$, $y = Cx + Du$, discretized with the bilinear method ("we follow prior work in using the bilinear method").
  - **Mamba:** A. Gu and T. Dao, "Mamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State Spaces," COLM 2024 (arXiv:2312.00752). §2 uses $h'(t) = \mathbf{A}h(t) + \mathbf{B}x(t)$, $y = \mathbf{C}h$, discretized by the "zero-order hold (ZOH)": $\bar{\mathbf{A}} = \exp(\Delta\mathbf{A})$, $\bar{\mathbf{B}} = (\Delta\mathbf{A})^{-1}(\exp(\Delta\mathbf{A}) - \mathbf{I})\cdot\Delta\mathbf{B}$. Its "selective" mechanism makes Δ, B and C depend on the input.
  - [Established]
- **Sources:** [arXiv:2111.00396](https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.00396); [arXiv:2312.00752](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.00752); the venue for Mamba is from the OpenReview record (COLM 2024). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-053 · ngspice numerical defaults and junction limiting

- **Values (ngspice master source code):**
  - `src/spicelib/analysis/cktntask.c` sets the default task options: `TSKabstol = 1e-12` (A), `TSKreltol = 1e-3`, `TSKvoltTol = 1e-6` (V), `TSKgmin = 1e-12` (S), `TSKdcMaxIter = 100`, `TSKtranMaxIter = 10`, `TSKintegrateMethod = TRAPEZOIDAL` with `TSKmaxOrder = 2`, `TSKxmu = 0.5` ("xmu=0: Backward Euler … xmu=0.5: trapezoidal (standard) … xmu=0.49: good damping of current ringing"), and a default temperature of 300.15 K.
  - `src/spicelib/devices/devsup.c` defines `DEVpnjlim`. If a new junction voltage exceeds `vcrit` and changes by more than $2V_T$, the step is replaced by $v_{old} + V_T(2 + \ln(\Delta v/V_T - 2))$ (or $V_T\ln(v_{new}/V_T)$ from a non-positive start).
  - `src/spicelib/devices/dio/diotemp.c` sets `vcrit = vte·log(vte/(√2·Is))`.
  - `dioload.c` adds GMIN in parallel with the junction.
  - The ngspice-42 build used here reported 5 iterations for the 02-10 diode operating point [Established].
- **Sources:** [ngspice git repository (SourceForge)](https://sourceforge.net/p/ngspice/ngspice/ci/master/tree/), files as named. The ngspice manual (ngspice.sourceforge.io) was unreachable from the build environment. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-054 · SPICE and SPICE2 origins

- **Values:**
  - L. W. Nagel and D. O. Pederson, "SPICE (Simulation Program with Integrated Circuit Emphasis)," UC Berkeley ERL Memorandum ERL-M382, 12 April 1973 (presented at the 16th Midwest Symposium on Circuit Theory).
  - L. W. Nagel, "SPICE2: A Computer Program to Simulate Semiconductor Circuits," ERL-M520 (PhD thesis), 9 May 1975.
  - The thesis conclusions state that "the Newton-Raphson algorithm appears to be the unanimous choice for a nonlinear equation solution method" (and that it needs modification, of which simple-limiting methods are discussed). They state that the Modified Nodal Analysis method required "substantially less computational effort" than the alternatives tested, and they describe trapezoidal integration with truncation-error time-step control as the method employed; Gear methods were evaluated [Established].
- **Sources:** [ERL-M382](https://www2.eecs.berkeley.edu/Pubs/TechRpts/1973/22871.html); [ERL-M520](https://www2.eecs.berkeley.edu/Pubs/TechRpts/1975/9602.html) (scanned thesis, text extracted by OCR). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-055 · HPL-MxP mixed-precision benchmark

- **Values:** HPL-MxP performs an "LU factorization and iterative refinement performed afterwards to bring the solution back to 64-bit accuracy", with low-precision (likely 16-bit) LU. GMRES may use the L and U factors as a preconditioner. The rules set 16-bit floating point as the lowest allowed precision, a scaled backward error ≤ 16 with ε = 2⁻⁵³, and a rate counted as (2/3 n³ + 3/2 n²)/time. FP8 results are "no longer admissible as of November 2025". **November 2026 list:** No. 1 El Capitan (LLNL), **16.680 EFlop/s**, against its HPL Rmax of **1.8090 EFlop/s** (TOP500 rank 2), a speed-up of **9.2**. LineShine, the TOP500 No. 1, ranks 4th on HPL-MxP at 7.922 EFlop/s [Established].
- **Sources:** [hpl-mxp.org](https://hpl-mxp.org/), [rules](https://hpl-mxp.org/rules.md), [results](https://hpl-mxp.org/results.md); [TOP500 November 2026](https://www.top500.org/lists/top500/2026/11/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-056 · Low-precision floating-point formats

- **Values:**
  - OCP "8-bit Floating Point Specification (OFP8)," Revision 1.0, approved 20 June 2023 (a 1 Dec 2023 correction fixed the stated biases). It defines E4M3 (bias 7, no infinities, maximum magnitude **448**) and E5M2 (bias 15, maximum magnitude **57,344**).
  - bfloat16: "one sign bit, eight exponent bits, and seven mantissa bits" (Google Cloud blog, 23 Aug 2019).
  - IEEE 754 binary16: a sign bit, 5 exponent bits and 10 explicitly stored fraction bits (from the Python and NumPy documentation; the IEEE 754-2019 text is paywalled and was not read).
  - [Established]
- **IEEE P3109:** an IEEE SA project, "Arithmetic Formats for Machine Learning", with its PAR approved 15 Feb 2023 and listed as an active PAR (not yet a published standard). Its working group's public interim report (draft 0.9.1, Oct 2024) and a June 2026 arXiv paper describe formats parameterized by width, precision, signedness and the presence of infinities, aligned with IEEE 754-2019 [Established that the project exists; final status unconfirmed]. Sources: [IEEE SA P3109 PAR](https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/3109/11165/); [P3109 working group](https://sagroups.ieee.org/p3109wgpublic); [arXiv 2606.04028](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04028). Checked Nov 2026.
- **Sources:** [OCP OFP8 Rev 1.0](https://opencompute.org/documents/ocp-8-bit-floating-point-specification-ofp8-revision-1-0-2023-06-20-pdf); [Google Cloud blog on bfloat16](https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/bfloat16-the-secret-to-high-performance-on-cloud-tpus); [Python struct docs](https://docs.python.org/3/library/struct.html). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-057 · Patriot missile timing error

- **Values:** U.S. GAO, "Patriot Missile Defense: Software Problem Led to System Failure at Dhahran, Saudi Arabia," GAO/IMTEC-92-26 (B-247094), letter dated 4 February 1992.
  - Time was kept "in tenths of seconds but is expressed as an integer", in registers "only 24 bits long".
  - The battery had been "in operation for over 100 consecutive hours"; 28 soldiers were killed.
  - Appendix II tabulates, for 100 hours, an inaccuracy of **0.3433 s** and a range-gate shift of **687 m**; at 20 hours, 0.0687 s and 137 m.
  - Skeel ("Roundoff Error and the Patriot Missile," *SIAM News* 25(4), 1992) explains the drift: 0.1 chopped to 23 binary places equals $0.1(1 - 2^{-20})$, so the clock was off by $2^{-20}$ of the elapsed time, 0.3433 s after 100 hours.
  - Skeel also explains why the drift mattered: tracking uses the elapsed time between radar pulses, and with consistent errors that difference "should be in error by only 0.0001%"; but the software, modified several times for the Gulf deployment, gained a subroutine converting clock time more accurately, which was not used everywhere, so "a less accurate truncated time" from one pulse was subtracted from a more accurate time from another and the error no longer cancelled (wording from search excerpts of the article).
  - The GAO's range-gate figure is its own tabulated value and is not simply speed × timing error.
  - [Established]
- **Sources:** [GAO report (Justia republication of the full scan)](https://gao.justia.com/department-of-defense/1992/2/patriot-missile-defense-imtec-92-26/IMTEC-92-26-full-report.pdf); [Skeel, SIAM News (mirror)](https://w3.ual.es/~plopez/docencia/itis/patriot.htm). gao.gov blocked the build environment. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-058 · Integrated voltage regulators and backside power delivery

- **FIVR:** E. A. Burton et al. (Intel), "FIVR — Fully integrated voltage regulators on 4th generation Intel Core SoCs," *Proc. IEEE APEC 2014*, pp. 432–439. The paper states that 4th-generation Core microprocessors are powered by fully integrated voltage regulators: switching converters on the die, with the inductors in the package substrate [Established]. The 4th-generation Core (Haswell) launched in 2013.
- **PowerVia:** Intel presented a backside power delivery test chip built on Intel 4 at the 2023 VLSI Symposium. Press coverage of the paper reports about 30% less voltage droop than a frontside-powered Intel 4 core and a 6.7% higher maximum frequency [Industry estimate: vendor-reported, from secondary coverage]. PowerVia is part of the Intel 18A process.
- **Sources:** [Burton et al. 2014 (PDF copy)](https://xdevs.com/doc/_Metrology/burton2014.pdf); [IEEE Spectrum on backside power](https://spectrum.ieee.org/backside-power-delivery-2661088734); [AnandTech, "Intel details PowerVia chipmaking tech" (Jun 2023)](https://www.anandtech.com/show/18894). The IEEE papers' full text was not fetched. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-059 · Serial-link bit-error-rate targets: PCIe and Ethernet

- **PCIe Gen 1–5 (NRZ):** a BER of $10^{-12}$. Synopsys (G. Ruggles, "Optimizing PCIe 6.0 Designs at 64GT/s," 19 Apr 2021): "the bit error rate (BER) of 1e-12 that we are used to for PCIe is not feasible … with the target being 1e-6 for the First Bit Error Rate (FBER)." A Tektronix/Anritsu PCIe Gen 5 receiver-test webinar (Mar 2021) checks "BER <1E(-12)" [Established; the PCI-SIG specification text was not read].
- **PCIe 6.0 (64 GT/s, PAM4, flit mode):** D. Das Sharma (Intel; PCI-SIG board), IEEE San Diego CAS/SSCS slides (27 Aug 2021) and OFA Workshop 2022 slides: FBER < $10^{-6}$; 256-byte flit of "236B TLP, 6B DLP, 8B CRC, 6B FEC"; FEC "3-way interleaved, single symbol correct"; "Retry probability per flit is 5 x 10-6"; reliability target "0 < FIT << 1 for a x16". Peer-reviewed summary: Das Sharma, *IEEE Micro* 41(1):23–29, 2021 (abstract only seen) [Established].
- **IEEE 802.3:** the 100G projects (802.3ba/bj/bm) adopted "a BER better than or equal to 10-12 at the MAC/PLS service interface" (Anslow, 400GbE study group, Sep 2013). The 802.3bs objectives (approved 17 Mar 2016) set "a BER of better than or equal to 10-13 at the MAC/PLS service interface (or the frame loss ratio equivalent)", and a May 2026 study-group contribution states the same $10^{-13}$ for 400GE, 800GE and 1.6TE, with a frame loss ratio below $6.2\times10^{-11}$ for 64-octet frames [Established].
- **Sources:** [Synopsys article](https://www.synopsys.com/articles/pcie-6-designs.html); [Das Sharma 2021 slides](https://ewh.ieee.org/r6/san_diego/sscs/events/slides/2021_08_27_PCIe6.0_IEEE_CAS_iDLP_Debendra.pdf); [Das Sharma OFA 2022](https://www.openfabrics.org/wp-content/uploads/2022-workshop/2022-workshop-presentations/206_DDasSharma.pdf); [Anslow 2013](https://www.ieee802.org/3/400GSG/public/13_09/anslow_400_01_0913.pdf); [802.3bs objectives](https://www.ieee802.org/3/bs/Objectives_16_0317.pdf); [Brown et al., May 2026](https://www.ieee802.org/3/400GPL/public/2605/brown_400GPL_01b_2605.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-060 · Zero-error BER tests and confidence

- **Formula:** with zero errors observed in $n$ bits, the confidence that the BER is below $p$ is $C = 1 - (1 - p)^n$, so $n = \ln(1 - C)/\ln(1 - p) \approx -\ln(1 - C)/p$. An IEEE 802.3 contribution (Anslow, Nov 2013) quotes this ITU-T G.Sup39 formula and its consequences at 400 Gb/s: 95% confidence that BER < $10^{-12}$ takes 7.5 s; at $10^{-15}$, 2 hours [Established].
- **Derived here:** $-\ln0.05 = 2.996$, so 95% needs $2.996/p$ bits: 93.62 s at 32 Gb/s and $10^{-12}$; 7.489 s at 400 Gb/s; 2.080 h at $10^{-15}$ and 400 Gb/s.
- **Source:** [Anslow, IEEE 802.3 400GbE SG, Nov 2013](https://www.ieee802.org/3/400GSG/public/13_11/anslow_400_02_1113.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-061 · Qualification sample sizes: AEC-Q100 and JEDEC JESD47

- **JEDEC JESD47G** (*Stress-Test-Driven Qualification of Integrated Circuits*, March 2009), Table 1: high-temperature operating life at $T_j \ge 125$ °C for 1000 h on "3 Lots / 77 units" with "0 Fail"; its Table A gives 230 samples for LTPD 1 with zero failures. A standards-store listing shows JESD47N (Aug 2026) as the current revision; whether its HTOL row is unchanged was not verified [Established for Rev G].
- **AEC-Q100:** a NASA NEPP workshop presentation (Lilani et al., "MIL-PRF-38535 vs. AEC-Q100," 2020): "No fails in 231 devices (77 devices from 3 lots) … LTPD = 1, meaning a maximum of 1% failures at 90% confidence level," and "This sample size is NOT sufficient or intended for process control or PPM evaluation." AEC-Q100 Rev J is dated 11 Aug 2023 (title of the AEC document, seen via search; aecouncil.com was unreachable) [Established].
- **Derived here:** $0.99^{231} = 0.098$, so zero failures in 231 gives 90.2% confidence that the defective fraction is below 1%.
- **Sources:** [JESD47G (hosted copy)](https://designers-guide.org/forum/Attachments/JESD47G.pdf); [NASA NEPP 2020](https://nepp.nasa.gov/workshops/etw2020/talks/15-JUN-MON/1630-Lilani-Yarbrough-Harzstark-Cozzolino-NEPP-ETW-AEC_pt2.pdf); [NSAI listing of JESD47N](https://shop.standards.ie/en-ie/standards/jedec-jesd47n-2026-1137371_saig_jedec_jedec_4039270/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-062 · Defect level and fault coverage

- **Paper:** T. W. Williams and N. C. Brown, "Defect Level as a Function of Fault Coverage," *IEEE Transactions on Computers*, vol. C-30, no. 12, pp. 987–988, Dec. 1981, DOI 10.1109/TC.1981.1675742 (citation confirmed via Crossref; full text paywalled).
- **Formula:** $\text{DL} = 1 - Y^{1 - T}$, as taught in university test-engineering courses (for example NYCU "Introduction to EDA," Spring 2026, lecture 9), with the table Y = 90%, T = 90% → about 10,000 DPM. Clustered (negative-binomial) refinements are used with fab data [Established].
- **Sources:** [Crossref record](https://doi.org/10.1109/TC.1981.1675742); [NYCU EDA lecture](https://tiger.iee.nycu.edu.tw/course/EDAIntro2026Spring/materials/lec9.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-063 · Jitter: the dual-Dirac model and the Q-scale

- **Model and convention:** R. Stephens, *Jitter Analysis: The dual-Dirac Model, RJ/DJ, and Q-Scale*, Keysight white paper 5989-3206EN (text dated Dec 2004; printing of 5 Apr 2023): "TJ(BER) ≡ 2QBERxσ + DJ(δδ)", with BER = ρT·erfc(Q/√2); "For transition density, ρT = 1/2, BER = 10–11 corresponds to Q = 6.7, for BER = 10–12, Q = 7.0". Composition rules from the same paper: random jitter adds in quadrature, and "DJTotal(δδ) ≈ DJ1(δδ) + DJ2(δδ) + …" [Established].
- **Multiplier table:** Renesas (IDT) AN-815, *Understanding Jitter Units*, Rev A (27 Mar 2014), Table 1: rms-to-peak-to-peak multiplier 11.996 at $10^{-9}$, 14.069 at $10^{-12}$ and 15.883 at $10^{-15}$ [Established].
- **Derived here:** $Q^{-1}(10^{-12}) = 7.0345$, so $2Q = 14.069$; with ρT = 1, $Q(Q_{\text{BER}}) = 5\times10^{-13}$ gives $2Q = 14.26$.
- **Sources:** [Keysight 5989-3206EN](https://www.keysight.com/zz/en/assets/7018-01309/white-papers/5989-3206.pdf); [Renesas AN-815](https://renesas.com/ja/document/apn/815-understanding-jitter-units). The T11 MJSQ document is members-only and was not read. Checked Nov 2026.

## R-064 · Soft-error rates and FIT

- **FIT:** "One FIT equals 1 failure per 1 billion device hours" (AMD UG116); Infineon/Cypress AN54908: "One FIT is a single failure in 10^9 device hours" [Established].
- **SRAM soft-error rates:** AMD *Device Reliability Report* (UG116), v10.22, released 13 Nov 2026, Table "Soft error rates (in FIT/Mb)", block RAM, real-time rate per event (Rosetta experiment; "includes upsets from neutron, proton, and thermal neutron secondaries"; "upsets from alpha particles are not included"), corrected to New York City per JESD89A/89-1A, with 90% confidence intervals: 484 (90 nm Virtex 4, ±11%), 373 (90 nm Spartan 3), 293 (90 nm Spartan 3E/3A), 692 (65 nm Virtex 5, −13%/+15%), 370 (45 nm Spartan 6), 213 (40 nm Virtex 6), 72 (28 nm Artix 7, Spartan 7, Zynq 7000, −8%/+9%), 42 (28 nm Kintex 7 and Virtex 7, −23%/+31%), 58 (20 nm UltraScale, −15%/+18%), 15 (16 nm UltraScale+, −12%/+14%; alpha 7), 11.4 (7 nm Versal, −32%/+50%; alpha 2.0, ±50%). "Mb = 1e6 memory bits." Re-read through the docs.amd.com content API in Nov 2026. The per-bit decline is not monotone across nodes. The 2018 edition (v10.9) listed 20 FIT/Mb for 16 nm, so values are revised as data accumulate [Established for the trend; values vendor-measured].
- **Sources:** [AMD UG116, SEU and soft-error-rate measurements](https://docs.amd.com/r/en-US/ug116/SEU-and-Soft-Error-Rate-Measurements); [Infineon AN54908](https://www.infineon.com/). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-065 · Six Sigma: 3.4 ppm and the 1.5σ shift

- **Claim and origin:** J. S. Ramberg, "Six Sigma: Fad or Fundamental?", *Quality Digest*, May 2000: "Should the process mean shift by 1.5 sigma, the defective rate would increase to 3.4 PPM defectives"; "The late Bill Smith, a reliability engineer at Motorola, is widely credited with originating Six Sigma"; "The 1.5-sigma deviation remains controversial." Motorola received the Baldrige award in 1988 (NIST recipients list) [Established for the arithmetic; the shift convention is Contested].
- **Derived here:** $Q(4.5) = 3.398\times10^{-6}$; a centered ±6σ process has $2Q(6) = 1.97\times10^{-9}$.
- **Sources:** [Quality Digest, May 2000](https://qualitydigest.com/may00/html/sixsigmapro.html); [NIST Baldrige recipients](https://www.nist.gov/baldrige/award-recipients). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-066 · PAPR, CCDF and crest factor of communication signals

- **CCDF method:** Keysight application note 5968-6875E, *Characterizing Digitally Modulated Signals with CCDF Curves* (published 19 Jul 2024): "A CCDF curve shows how much time the signal spends at or above a given power level. The power level is expressed in dB relative to the average power"; "as the number of tones increases, the CCDF curves begin to resemble the Additive White Gaussian Noise (AWGN) curve" [Established].
- **LTE measurements:** Rohde & Schwarz application note 1MA271_0e, *5G Waveform Candidates* (June 2016): "CCDF measurement of a 20 MHz LTE Downlink Signal (E-TM 1.1); Crest Factor = 11.65 dB"; 20 MHz LTE uplink (SC-FDMA, 100 RB): "QPSK (yellow; 5.15 dB Crest Factor), 16QAM (blue; 5.60 dB) and 64QAM (green; 6.03 dB)"; WCDMA "typical crest factor ~4 dB"; "The high PAPR in OFDM was the reason for introducing a slightly modified access technology for the LTE Uplink"; "Typically Crest Factor Reduction (CFR) techniques are applied to reduce PAPR and Digital Pre-Distortion (DPD) algorithms will then correct for" amplifier nonlinearity. R&S 1MA138_0E (Dec 2008): "The maximum PAPR for LTE is approximately 12 dB" [Established; measured crest factors depend on capture length].
- **5G NR uplink:** D. Darsena, G. Gelli and F. Verde, "Beamforming and precoding techniques", Wiley 5G Ref (arXiv:2004.05079, 2020): "The NR PHY is based on Orthogonal Frequency-Division Multiplexing (OFDM) with cyclic prefix (CP), with optional support for Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) precoded OFDM (DFT-s-OFDM) in the UL"; "if DFT-s-OFDM is employed, only single-layer transmission is allowed" (citing 3GPP TS 38.211). The 3GPP specification text itself was not read [Established].
- **Derived here:** for a complex Gaussian signal the instantaneous power is exponential, so the PAPR at CCDF probability $p$ is $10\log_{10}\ln(1/p)$: 6.632 dB at $10^{-2}$, 9.643 dB at $10^{-4}$, 11.40 dB at $10^{-6}$. The 11.65 dB measured LTE crest factor corresponds to an exceedance probability of about $4.5\times10^{-7}$ under that model, consistent with a long capture.
- **Sources:** [Keysight 5968-6875E](https://www.keysight.com/content/dam/keysight/en/doc/gate/application-notes-archived/5968-6875.pdf); [R&S 1MA271](https://scdn.rohde-schwarz.com/ur/pws/dl_downloads/dl_application/application_notes/1ma271/1MA271_0e_5G_waveform_candidates.pdf); [R&S 1MA138](https://scdn.rohde-schwarz.com/ur/pws/dl_downloads/dl_application/application_notes/1ma138/1MA138_0e_RF_chipset_verification_for_LTE.pdf); [arXiv:2004.05079](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.05079). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-067 · RS(544,514) "KP4" FEC and pre-FEC BER

- **Code and analysis method:** P. Anslow (Ciena), "RS(544,514) FEC performance including precoding", IEEE P802.3cd Task Force, July 2016: "The IEEE 802.3bs Task Force has adopted RS(544,514) FEC"; "RS(544,514) FEC (which has 10-bit symbols)". With random errors, the BER at the FEC input that gives a frame loss ratio of 6.2E-10 ("equivalent to BER = 1E-12 with random errors") is 3.8E-4, and 2.3E-4 for FLR 6.2E-13. With a burst-continuation probability a = 0.75 the requirement for FLR 6.2E-10 tightens to 2.3E-4 for the P802.3bs D1.5 configuration, 5.9E-5 with no multiplexing and 2.5E-5 with 2:1 bit multiplexing; these burst values include the extra errors from the bursts. The contribution allots "a BER of 2.4E-4 in the optical sub-link" [Established].
- **Formulas:** X. Wang, X. He and H. Ren, IEEE P802.3df contribution, 28 Sept 2022: SER_in = 1 − (1 − BER_in)^m; UCR = Σ_{i=t+1}^{n} C(n,i) SER^i (1 − SER)^{n−i}; BER_out ≈ (t+1)/(n·m)·UCR; "For RS(544,514), n = 544, k = 514, t = 15, m = 10"; "BER objective: 1E-13, equivalent to FLR 6.2E-11 for 64-byte frames"; "Pre-FEC BER: ~2.4E-4?" (question mark in the original); "To account for burst errors, multiply this BER by 2 for a = 0.75 with precoding" [Established]. Read from the full deck (Nov 2026): "this BER" is the "random error BER" equivalent to the required slicer SNR, and the table on slide 16 gives the detector error ratio (DER) and a BER that "include[s] the additional errors due to the bursts", multiplied by 1.11, 1.6 and 2 for a = 0.1, 0.375 and 0.5/0.6/0.75 (precoding on for a ≥ 0.6). So the factor 2 converts an error-event rate into a bit-error count; it is not by itself a tightening. To meet FLR 6.2E-11 (BER 1E-13 equivalent), the worst-case FEC-input BER falls from 3.08E-4 (2 codewords) or 2.93E-4 (4 codewords) at a = 0 to 1.78E-4–1.92E-4 at a = 0.75 across the bit- and symbol-multiplexing cases [Established].
- **KP4 and KR4 naming:** M. Gustlin, "802.3bj FEC overview and status", IEEE 802.3 100GSR study group, Jan 2020 (same slides in the IEEE P802.3db ad hoc, June 2020): 802.3bj "Added in KR4/KP4 RS FEC (Cls 91)… both RS(528,514) and RS(544,514)"; "RS(544,514) used for 50/100G per lane technology; RS(528,514) is used for 25G technology"; 802.3bs "RS FEC integrated into the PCS, always on/required"; "RS(544,514) aka 'KP4' FEC code". That RS(528,514) is the KR4 code follows by elimination and matches Clause 91 practice; the normative clause text was not read [Established].
- **Derived here:** at a random-error pre-FEC BER of 2.4×10⁻⁴, SER = 2.397×10⁻³, mean 1.304 wrong symbols per codeword, UCR = 8.152×10⁻¹³ (Poisson 9.839×10⁻¹³; a Gaussian approximation gives about 7×10⁻³⁶). At 3.8×10⁻⁴, UCR = 6.304×10⁻¹⁰, consistent with the FLR of 6.2×10⁻¹⁰ quoted above. The normative IEEE 802.3 clause text was not read.
- **Sources:** [Anslow, IEEE P802.3cd, July 2016](https://www.ieee802.org/3/cd/public/July16/anslow_3cd_01_0716.pdf); [Wang et al., IEEE P802.3df, Sept 2022](https://www.ieee802.org/3/df/public/22_10/22_0928/wang_3df_01b_220928.pdf); [Gustlin, Jan 2020](https://www.ieee802.org/3/100GSR/public/Jan20/gustlin_100GSR_01a_0120.pdf); [Gustlin, June 2020](https://grouper.ieee.org/groups/802/3/db/public/adhoc/presentations/gustlin_3db_adhoc_01_062520.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-068 · Process capability and outlier screening

- **AIAG defaults:** S. Steiner, B. Abraham and J. MacKay, "Understanding Process Capability Indices", University of Waterloo IIQP (1998), quoting AIAG's QS-9000 *Quality System Requirements* (1995): "For stable processes and normally distributed data, a Cpk value ≥ 1.33 should be achieved. • For chronically unstable processes with output meeting specification and a predictable pattern, a Ppk value ≥ 1.67 should be achieved"; the APQP manual (Ford Powertrain): "Significant Characteristics (SCs) must be in a state of statistical control with Ppk ≥ 1.67 and Cpk ≥ 1.33"; the PPAP manual (1995): "Pp and Ppk > 1.67 The process probably meets customer requirements … 1.33 ≤ Ppk ≤ 1.67 The process may not meet customer requirements … until an ongoing Cpk ≥ 1.33 is achieved." Current AIAG editions are paywalled and were not read [Established for the conventional thresholds].
- **AEC-Q100:** NASA NEPP, Lilani et al., "MIL-PRF-38535 vs. AEC-Q100" (2020), lists AEC-Q100 electrical distributions with "Where applicable, Cpk >1.67" [Established].
- **Cp and defect rates:** NIST/SEMATECH e-Handbook of Statistical Methods, §6.1.6: Cp 1.00, 1.33, 1.66, 2.00 ↔ rejects 0.27%, 64 ppm, 0.6 ppm, 2 ppb for a centered process; approximate confidence limits for Cpu and Cpl, $\hat C_{pu} \pm z\sqrt{1/(9n) + \hat C_{pu}^2/(2(n-1))}$ (normal approximation; Bissell 1990 cited). The chapter applies the same expression to $C_{pk} = \min(C_{pu}, C_{pl})$, as Bissell's 1990 paper does; the NIST page itself was not re-read in Nov 2026 (host unreachable from the audit session). Derived here: exactly ±4σ gives 63.34 ppm and ±5σ gives 0.5733 ppm [Established].
- **Part Average Testing:** US patent 10,118,200 B2, describing AEC-Q001 Rev-C (Jul 2003): reference data from at least 30 parts per lot (N ≥ 180); "The 'robust mean' is defined as the median"; "The 'robust sigma' is defined as the difference between [the quartiles], divided by 1.35"; "Calculate the upper and lower Static PAT limits as 'Robust Mean'±6*'Robust Sigma'". The current AEC-Q001 revision was not confirmed on the AEC site [Established].
- **Sources:** [Steiner et al. 1998](https://sas.uwaterloo.ca/~shsteine/papers/cap.pdf); [NASA NEPP 2020](https://nepp.nasa.gov/workshops/etw2020/talks/15-JUN-MON/1630-Lilani-Yarbrough-Harzstark-Cozzolino-NEPP-ETW-AEC_pt2.pdf); [NIST/SEMATECH e-Handbook §6.1.6](https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/pmc/section1/pmc16.htm); [US 10,118,200 B2](https://patents.google.com/patent/US10118200B2/en). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-069 · Weibull model and Arrhenius acceleration

- **Weibull:** NIST/SEMATECH e-Handbook of Statistical Methods (last updated 27 Apr 2022), §8.1.6.2: CDF $1 - e^{-(t/\alpha)^\gamma}$, with "α the scale parameter (the Characteristic Life), γ (gamma) the Shape Parameter"; "When γ = 1, the Weibull reduces to the Exponential Model"; "The Weibull model can be derived theoretically as a form of Extreme Value Distribution, governing the time to occurrence of the 'weakest link' of many competing failure processes"; shape 2 gives the Rayleigh distribution. The handbook warns that notation varies between authors [Established].
- **Arrhenius example:** JEDEC JESD47G (March 2009), Table 1 note (a): "with apparent activation energy of 0.7 eV, 125 °C stress temperature and 55 °C use temperature, the acceleration factor (Arrhenius equation) is 78.6. This means 1000h stress duration is equivalent to 9 years of use. This might be shorter than the application requirement." Derived here: with k = 8.617333×10⁻⁵ eV/K and kelvin = °C + 273.15 the factor is 77.65 (8.858 years); the note's 78.6 follows from k = 8.6×10⁻⁵ eV/K and an offset of 273 [Established; the rounding explanation is a derivation].
- **Sources:** [NIST/SEMATECH e-Handbook §8.1.6.2](https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/apr/section1/apr162.htm); [JESD47G (hosted copy)](https://designers-guide.org/forum/Attachments/JESD47G.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-070 · Thermal noise reference: kT0 and 290 K

- **Claim:** Keysight application note 5952-8255E, *Fundamentals of RF and Microwave Noise Figure Measurements* (published 15 May 2026): "Friis suggested a reference source temperature of 290 K (denoted by T0)"; "The power spectral density kT0, furthermore, is the even number 4.00 x 10-21 watts per hertz of bandwidth (–174 dBm/Hz). The IRE (forerunner of the IEEE) adopted 290 K as the standard temperature for determining noise figure" [Established].
- **Derived here:** with the exact SI value $k = 1.380649\times10^{-23}$ J/K, $kT_0 = 4.0039\times10^{-21}$ W/Hz $= -173.98$ dBm/Hz.
- **Source:** [Keysight 5952-8255E](https://www.keysight.com/content/dam/keysight/en/doc/gate/application-notes/5952-8255.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-071 · Op-amp noise specifications: 1/f and zero-drift

- **Bipolar low-noise op-amp:** Texas Instruments OPAx211 datasheet, SBOS377L (October 2006, revised January 2020): "Low voltage noise: 1.1 nV/√Hz at 1 kHz"; "Input voltage noise: 80 nVPP (0.1 to 10 Hz)"; noise density 1.4 nV/√Hz at 100 Hz (typical values) [Established for this part].
- **Zero-drift op-amp:** Texas Instruments OPAx189 datasheet, SBOS830I (September 2017, revised October 2021): input voltage noise 17 nVRMS and 0.1 µVPP for f = 0.1 Hz to 10 Hz; noise density 5.2 nV/√Hz at 10 Hz, 100 Hz, 1 kHz and 10 kHz; "zero-drift" architecture with "0.005 μV/°C" offset drift (typical values) [Established for this part].
- **Derived here:** a flat 5.2 nV/√Hz over 0.1–10 Hz integrates to $5.2\sqrt{9.9} = 16.36$ nV rms, consistent with the specified 17 nV rms, so the zero-drift part shows essentially no 1/f noise; its peak-to-peak-to-rms ratio is 0.1 µV/17 nV ≈ 5.9.
- **Sources:** [TI OPA211 datasheet](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/opa211.pdf); [TI OPA189 datasheet](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/opa189.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-072 · Lifetime distributions by failure mechanism

- **Electromigration is lognormal:** F. N. Najm, "Efficient Simulation of Electromigration Damage in Large Chip Power Grids Using Accurate Physical Models (Invited Paper)", IEEE International Reliability Physics Symposium (IRPS), 2019: "Due to random manufacturing variations, these TTF values are random samples, and they are known to follow a lognormal distribution", citing J. R. Lloyd and J. Kitchin, *J. Appl. Phys.* 69(4):2117–2127, 1991; Black's-model parameters "are extracted by regression from the measured TTFs using lognormal plots" [Established].
- **Oxide breakdown is Weibull:** U. M. S. Costa et al., "An Improved Description of the Dielectric Breakdown in Oxides Based on a Generalized Weibull distribution", *Physica A* (2006), doi 10.1016/j.physa.2005.07.017: charge- and time-to-breakdown "are usually assumed to be Weibull distributed" [Established].
- **Sources:** [Najm 2019](https://www.eecg.utoronto.ca/~najm/papers/irps19-najm.pdf); [Costa et al., arXiv cond-mat/0602515](https://arxiv.org/abs/cond-mat/0602515). Checked Nov 2026.

Checked Nov 2026.

## R-074 · MEMS inertial sensors: datasheet figures

- **Bosch BMI270** (datasheet BST-BMI270-DS000-08, revision 1.6, March 2026): "16 bit digital, triaxial accelerometer with ±2g/±4g/±8g/±16g range"; output noise density "0.16 mg/√Hz" at range 8 g, normal mode, 25 °C; "typ. 685 µA (in full ODR and aliasing free operation)"; accelerometer-only low-power mode at 25 Hz 10 µA; rms noise 1.51 mg at 80 Hz bandwidth [Established].
- **Bosch BMI323** (BST-BMI323-DS000-13, revision 1.7, 15 April 2026): noise density 180 µg/√Hz in high-performance mode at range 8 g (the unit glyph is garbled in text extraction and was read as µg/√Hz); "typ. 790 µA (in full ODR and aliasing free operation)" [Established].
- **Sources:** [BMI270 datasheet](https://www.bosch-sensortec.com/media/boschsensortec/downloads/datasheets/bst-bmi270-ds000.pdf); [BMI323 datasheet](https://www.bosch-sensortec.com/media/boschsensortec/downloads/datasheets/bst-bmi323-ds000.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-075 · 32.768 kHz timing: quartz crystals and MEMS oscillators

- **Quartz tuning fork, Micro Crystal CM7V-T1A** (datasheet version 8.0, 10.2016): "Turnover temperature T0 25 ±5 °C"; "Frequency vs. temperature ΔF/F0 –0.035 ppm/°C² (T - T0)2 ±10%"; motional capacitance C1 = 3.7 fF typical; series resistance 50 kΩ typical, 70 kΩ maximum; "operates under vacuum in a hermetically sealed ceramic package" [Established].
- **NDK NX2012SE** (document NDKX01-00015-001, DigiKey-hosted copy): "Turnover temperature +20 to +30 °C … Parabolic coefficient -0.04 ppm/°C2" (maximum) [Established].
- **SiTime SiT1532** MEMS 32.768 kHz oscillator (Rev 2.0, 6 April 2025): frequency tolerance 10 ppm at 25 °C; stability 75 ppm over −10 to +70 °C and 100 ppm over −40 to +85 °C; core current 0.90 µA [Established]. **SiTime SiT5356** (Rev 1.08, 1 January 2023): "a ±100 ppb precision MEMS Super-TCXO", −40 to +105 °C [Established].
- **MEMS resonator Q:** Melamud et al. (SiTime), Solid-State Sensors, Actuators and Microsystems Workshop, Hilton Head, 2012, pp. 66–69: "The typical resonator quality factor, Q, of 144,000" at 48 MHz [Established].
- **Derived here:** the CM7V-T1A's motional inductance $1/(\omega^2C_1)$ = 6376 H and Q = $1/(\omega R_SC_1)$ = 2.63×10⁴ at typical values; −126 ppm at 85 °C from the parabola.
- **Sources:** [Micro Crystal CM7V-T1A](https://www.microcrystal.com/fileadmin/Media/Products/32kHz/Datasheet/CM7V-T1A.pdf); [NDK NX2012SE](https://mm.digikey.com/Volume0/opasdata/d220001/medias/docus/8456/MU01260-32.768K.pdf); [SiT1532](https://www.sitime.com/datasheet/SiT1532); [SiT5356](https://www.sitime.com/datasheet/SiT5356); [Melamud et al. 2012](https://transducer-research-foundation.org/technical_digests/HiltonHead_2012/hh2012_0066.pdf). Checked Nov 2026.

The 2026 edition's growth rate implies a 2024 base of about $15.9 billion, not the $15.4 billion of the 2025 edition; the Yole pages themselves returned 403.
