## R-187 · IIR filter implementations

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from Arm's online documentation (the "latest" pages at the URLs below). Library statements are tagged [Established] as the library's documented behavior.*

- **Arm CMSIS-DSP, "Biquad Cascade IIR Filters Using Direct Form I Structure"**: "This set of functions implements arbitrary order recursive (IIR) filters. The filters are implemented as a cascade of second order Biquad sections. The functions support Q15, Q31 and floating-point data types." Each stage implements "y[n] = b0 * x[n] + b1 * x[n-1] + b2 * x[n-2] + a1 * y[n-1] + a2 * y[n-2]"; "A Direct Form I algorithm is used with 5 coefficients and 4 state variables per stage." "Pay careful attention to the sign of the feedback coefficients. Some design tools use the difference equation … - a1 * y[n-1] - a2 * y[n-2]. In this case the feedback coefficients a1 and a2 must be negated when used with the CMSIS DSP Library." An 8th-order filter uses numStages = 4; a 9th-order filter uses 5 stages with one configured as first order (b2 = 0, a2 = 0) [Established]. Used in 11-04. [Documentation](https://arm-software.github.io/CMSIS-DSP/latest/group__BiquadCascadeDF1.html).
- **Arm CMSIS-DSP, "Biquad Cascade IIR Filters Using a Direct Form II Transposed Structure"**: implements the same cascades "using a transposed direct form II structure"; "These functions provide a slight memory savings as compared to the direct form I Biquad filter functions"; two state values d1, d2 per stage; the same sign warning; and, on the numerical choice: "The advantage of the Direct Form I structure is that it is numerically more robust for fixed-point data types. That is why the Direct Form I structure supports Q15 and Q31 data types. The transposed Direct Form II structure, on the other hand, requires a wide dynamic range for the state variables d1 and d2. Because of this, the CMSIS library only has a floating-point version of the Direct Form II Biquad. The advantage of the Direct Form II Biquad is that it requires half the number of state variables, 2 rather than 4, per Biquad stage" [Established]. Used in 11-04. [Documentation](https://arm-software.github.io/CMSIS-DSP/latest/group__BiquadCascadeDF2T.html).
- **Arm CMSIS-DSP, "Finite Impulse Response (FIR) Filters"** (group page, read 20 Nov 2026): lists processing functions `arm_fir_f16`, `arm_fir_f32` and `arm_fir_f64` ("Processing function for floating-point FIR filter") and `arm_fir_q7`, `arm_fir_q15` and `arm_fir_q31` for fixed point [Established]. Used in 11-07. [FIR documentation](https://arm-software.github.io/CMSIS-DSP/latest/group__FIR.html).

## R-189 · SerDes reference equalizer

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the IEEE P802.3ck task force's public tools page: the COM 3.70 archive (`mellitz_3ck_adhoc_01_032322_COM3p70.zip`) was downloaded and its MATLAB source and configuration workbook opened. These are task-force tools and draft configurations, not the published standard; the values in IEEE Std 802.3ck-2022 itself were not checked (the standard was not accessible here).*

- **COM source, `com_ieee8023_93a_370.m`** [Established, as code]: function `FD_CTLE` computes `hctf = ( 10.^(kacdc_dB/20) + 1i*freq/f_z ) ./ ( (1+1i*freq/f_p1) .* (1+1i*freq/f_p2))`, i.e. $H(f) = (G + jf/f_z)/[(1 + jf/f_{p1})(1 + jf/f_{p2})]$ with $G = 10^{g_{DC}/20}$; the time-domain version maps the poles to $-2\pi f_{p1}$, $-2\pi f_{p2}$ and the zero to $-2\pi f_zG$. A second stage, `H_low = (10.^(g_DC_HP/20) + 1i*f/f_HP)./(1 + 1i*f/f_HP)`, adds a low-frequency pole–zero pair.
- **Configuration `config_com_ieee8023_93a=3ck_d3p1_KR_11_30_21.xlsx`** (draft 3.1, 30 Nov 2021; distributed with COM 3.70, March 2022) [Established, as a draft configuration]: `f_b` 53.125 GBd; `g_DC` [−20:1:0] dB; `f_z` 21.25 GHz; `f_p1` 21.25 GHz; `f_p2` 53.125 GHz; `g_DC_HP` [−6:1:0]; `f_HP_PZ` 0.6640625 GHz (= f_b/80).. The CR_CA and 120F_C2C configurations of the same draft set the same CTLE values; the C2M and MTF (host-to-module) configurations set `g_DC` [−13:1:0], `f_z` 12.58 GHz, `f_p1` 20 GHz, `f_p2` 28 GHz, `g_DC_HP` [−3:0.5:0] and `f_HP_PZ` 1.328125 GHz (re-read from the workbooks' cell XML, 20 Nov 2026). Used in 11-08 and 11-09.
- **Digital implementation** [Established, as code]: function `TD_CTLE` (comment: "Equation 93A-22 implemented in z-space") maps each pole and the zero with `(1+p/bilinear_fs)./(1-p/bilinear_fs)`, where `bilinear_fs = 2*fb*oversampling` (the bilinear transform with 2/T, T = 1/(f_b·M)), and builds the numerator as `poly([zd, -1])`, i.e. with an extra zero at z = −1; the call passes `param.samples_per_ui`, read as `xls_parameter(parameter, 'M', 32)`, and the KR configuration sets `M` = 32. Used in 11-09.
- **Source:** [IEEE P802.3ck tools page](https://www.ieee802.org/3/ck/public/tools/index.html), COM version 3.70 (R. Mellitz, posted March 2022). Checked Nov 2026.

## R-190 · Sampling and anti-aliasing in converter data sheets

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the PDFs at the URLs below, as downloaded (the same documents as R-184 to R-186, re-read for their sampling and anti-aliasing sections). Specifications are tagged [Established].*

- **TI ADS1291/ADS1292/ADS1292R** (SBAS502C, revised April 2020): §8.3.4 (PGA): "The PGA output is filtered by an RC filter before it goes to the ADC. The filter is formed by an internal resistor RS = 2 kΩ and an external capacitor CFILTER (4.7 nF, typical). This filter acts as an anti-aliasing filter with the –3-dB bandwidth of 8.4 kHz. The internal RS resistor is accurate to 15% so actual bandwidth will vary"; "A larger value CFILTER capacitor can be used for increased attenuation at higher frequencies for anti-aliasing purposes … The tradeoff is that a larger capacitor value gives degraded THD performance." §8.3.4.3: a "second-order modulator"; "the sampling clock has a typical value of 128 kHz"; the on-chip decimation filters "also provide antialias filtering". §8.3.5: "The sinc filter has notches (or zeroes) that occur at the output data rate and multiples thereof"; "the … passband repeats itself at every fMOD. The input R-C anti-aliasing filters in the system should be chosen such that any interference in frequencies around multiples of fMOD are attenuated sufficiently" [Established]. Figure 22 ("PGA Implementation") draws RS = 2 kΩ in each PGA output leg with CFILTER (4.7 nF) across PGA1P and PGA1N, and the electrical-characteristics footnote reads "CFILTER is the capacitor accross the PGA outputs"; so 8.4 kHz ≈ 1/(2π · 4 kΩ · 4.7 nF) = 8.47 kHz (re-read 20 Nov 2026 after the Course 11 audit). The §8.3.5 warning about interference around multiples of fMOD refers to "the input R-C anti-aliasing filters in the system", not to this PGA-output RC. Used in 11-10. [Data sheet](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/ads1292r.pdf).
- **TI TLV320AIC3204** (SLOS602E, revised September 2019): §9.3.3: "a stereo audio ADC, which uses a delta-sigma modulator with a programmable oversampling ratio, followed by a digital decimation filter. The ADC supports sampling rates from 8kHz to 192kHz"; Table 2 lists AOSR values 128 or 64 for decimation filter A, 64 for B and 32 for C; the ADC performance tables are specified at fs = 48 kHz with AOSR = 128 [Established]. Used in 11-10. [Data sheet](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/tlv320aic3204.pdf).

## R-196 · RF exposure limits (FCC and ICNIRP)

*Re-read on 20 Nov 2026 to extend R-083 with the full US maximum-permissible-exposure table; values tagged [Established] as regulation and published guidelines.*

- **47 CFR § 1.1310(e)(1), Table 1** (eCFR text read 20 Nov 2026), "(ii) Limits for General Population/Uncontrolled Exposure": "30-300 27.5 0.073 0.2 <30" (frequency MHz; electric field V/m; magnetic field A/m; power density mW/cm²; averaging time minutes), "300-1,500 f/1500 <30", "1,500-100,000 1.0 <30". § 1.1310(c): "a peak spatial-average SAR of 1.6 W/kg, averaged over any 1 gram of tissue (defined as a tissue volume in the shape of a cube)". [eCFR § 1.1310](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-A/part-1/subpart-I/section-1.1310)
- **ICNIRP 2020**, *Health Physics* 118(5):483–524, Table 2 (basic restrictions, averaging intervals ≥ 6 min), general public: 100 kHz to 6 GHz whole-body 0.08 W/kg, local head/torso 2 W/kg, local limb 4 W/kg; above 6 GHz to 300 GHz, absorbed power density 20 W/m²; "Local SAR is to be averaged over a 10-g cubic mass". [ICNIRP 2020 (PDF)](https://www.icnirp.org/cms/upload/publications/ICNIRPrfgdl2020.pdf)
- **Derived here (13-04):** the ratio 27.5 V/m ÷ 0.073 A/m = 376.7 Ω (the plane-wave impedance the table assumes); the rms field of a 10 W/m² plane wave, √(10 × 376.7) = 61.38 V/m; the far-field distance at which 1 W EIRP gives 10 W/m², 8.92 cm.

Fibre attributes: mode field diameter at 1310 nm, "Range of nominal values 8.6-9.2 μm", tolerance 0.4 μm; cladding diameter "Nominal 125.0 μm"; cable cut-off wavelength "Maximum 1260 nm"; chromatic dispersion at 1550 nm between 13.3 and 18.6 ps/(nm × km). ## R-200 · FCC 6 GHz unlicensed (U-NII) EIRP limits

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the eCFR text of 47 CFR § 15.407; tagged [Established] as regulation in force on that date.*

- **47 CFR § 15.407(a)(4):** "For a standard power access point and fixed client device operating in the 5.925-6.425 GHz and 6.525-6.875 GHz bands, the maximum power spectral density must not exceed 23 dBm e.i.r.p in any 1-megahertz band. In addition, the maximum e.i.r.p. over the frequency band of operation must not exceed 36 dBm."
- **47 CFR § 15.407(a)(5):** "For an indoor access point operating in the 5.925-7.125 GHz band, the maximum power spectral density must not exceed 5 dBm e.i.r.p. in any 1-megahertz band. In addition, the maximum e.i.r.p. over the frequency band of operation must not exceed 30 dBm." [eCFR § 15.407](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-A/part-15/subpart-E/section-15.407)
- **Context:** the 6 GHz band was opened to unlicensed use by FCC Report and Order 20-51 (adopted 23 Apr 2020; published in the Federal Register 26 May 2020). [Federal Register 2020-11236](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2020-05-26/pdf/2020-11236.pdf)
- **Derived here (13-11):** the PSD-limited EIRP for channel widths of 20, 80, 160 and 320 MHz (5 dBm/MHz + 10 log₁₀ of the width in MHz, capped at 30 dBm).

## R-201 · RISC-V unprivileged ISA manual, version 20260120

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from RISC-V International's ratified specifications library (primary); tagged [Established].*

- **Versions:** the manual set is versioned by date; 20260120 is current (earlier: 20250508, 20240411). The unprivileged preface lists "RV32I 2.1 Ratified … RV64I 2.1 Ratified"; the privileged volume lists Machine ISA 1.13 and Supervisor ISA 1.13. [Unprivileged preface](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/colophon.html) · [Privileged preface](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/priv/priv-preface.html)
- **RV32I chapter:** "RV32I contains 40 unique instructions"; "The RISC-V ISA keeps the source (rs1 and rs2) and destination (rd) registers at the same position in all formats to simplify decoding"; "the sign bit for all immediates is always in bit 31 of the instruction to speed sign-extension circuitry"; "The conditional branch range is ±4 KiB"; "Jumps can therefore target a ±1 MiB range"; "NOP is encoded as ADDI x0, x0, 0"; ADDI with rd = x0 (other than the NOP) is listed among the HINT encodings. [RV32I chapter](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/rv32.html)
- **Endianness (Introduction):** "RISC-V base ISAs have either little-endian or big-endian memory systems, with the privileged architecture further defining bi-endian operation. Instructions are stored in memory as a sequence of 16-bit little-endian parcels, regardless of memory system endianness." [Introduction](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/intro.html)
- **C extension:** "Typically, 50%-60% of the RISC-V instructions in a program can be replaced with RVC instructions, resulting in a 25%-30% code-size reduction." [C extension](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/c-st-ext.html)
- **Derived here (14-02):** every encoding, range and code size in the chapter is reproduced with LLVM 18's `llvm-mc` in verify_14_02.py.
- **Control-flow integrity (Chapter 34, "Control-flow Integrity (CFI)"; read 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 14 audit):** "To enforce forward-edge control-flow integrity, the Zicfilp extension introduces a landing pad (LPAD) instruction." "Compilers emit a landing pad instruction as the first instruction of an address-taken function, as well as at any indirect jump targets." "The Zicfiss extension introduces a shadow stack to enforce backward-edge control-flow integrity." "A shadow stack is a second stack used to store a shadow copy of the return address in the link register if it needs to be spilled." [Control-flow Integrity](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/unpriv-cfi.html)

## R-203 · RISC-V licensing and trademark model

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from RISC-V International's FAQ and About pages (primary); tagged [Established].*

- "The RISC-V ISA is free and open with a permissive license for use by anyone in all types of implementations. Designers are free to develop proprietary or open source implementations…"; "There is no fee to use the RISC-V ISA. … To create an implementation from the RISC-V ISA, it is necessary to procure or leverage additional IP outside of RISC-V which may carry a fee."; on publishing a core's source: "No, the source code can be completely closed."; "the use of the RISC-V trademark requires a license which is granted to members of RISC-V International for use with compliant implementations." [FAQ](https://riscv.org/about/faq/)

## R-204 · Arm licensing models and FY2026 revenue (Form 20-F)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from Arm Holdings plc's Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended 31 Mar 2026, filed with the SEC 26 May 2026 (primary); tagged [Established].*

- **Models:** "our customers can choose from several licensing models … These include CSS, Arm Total Access, Arm Flexible Access, TLAs and Architecture Licenses. Regardless of the license model a customer uses, we receive a per-unit royalty fee on substantially every chip shipped." "Under an ALA, the licensee is allowed to develop their own highly customized CPU designs that is compliant with the Arm instruction set architecture ('ISA') for a fixed architecture license fee." "Under a TLA, we license a single CPU design or other technology design to a customer in return for a fixed license fee." "Royalties are generally either set as a percentage of the licensee's average selling price per chip or as a fixed amount per chip. The royalty rates per chip typically reduce over time as the total volume of chips incorporating our products shipped increases…"
- **Revenue (US$ M):** FY2026 total 4,920 (FY2025 4,007); license and other 2,307 (1,839); royalty 2,613 (2,168). *Derived:* royalty ≈ 53% of FY2026 revenue. License and other revenue from related parties: 1,009 (418).
- **Litigation:** the filing describes the Arm v. Qualcomm/Nuvia architecture-licence dispute, including a 30 Sep 2025 judgment for Qualcomm and Arm's pending appeal (status as of the filing date).
- [Form 20-F (SEC EDGAR)](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1973239/000197323926000097/arm-20260331.htm)

## R-206 · x86 Ecosystem Advisory Group

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 (primary); tagged [Established].*

## R-208 · Energy per operation at 45 nm (Horowitz, ISSCC 2014)

*A re-check of [R-157](#r-157--arithmetic-energy-and-dsp-blocks), which first logged this table (read from the paper itself); the values agree.*

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from a third-party mirror of the published digest pages (secondary copy; the IEEE footer and page numbers match the digest), not from IEEE Xplore; tagged [Established] as the field's standard reference table, with the caveat below.*

- **Figure 1.1.9, "Rough energy costs for various operations in 45nm 0.9V":** integer add 8-bit 0.03 pJ, 32-bit 0.1 pJ; integer multiply 8-bit 0.2 pJ, 32-bit 3.1 pJ; FP add 16-bit 0.4 pJ, 32-bit 0.9 pJ; FP multiply 16-bit 1.1 pJ, 32-bit 3.7 pJ; 64-bit cache read 8 KB 10 pJ, 32 KB 20 pJ, 1 MB 100 pJ; DRAM 1.3–2.6 nJ.
- **Text:** "the programmable nature of a processor has high energy overhead, 70pJ/instruction, vs. a few pJ for an operation"; "energy cost of a DRAM access (1 to 2nJ) is a couple of orders-of-magnitude higher than the cost of an internal cache access or functional operation (10pJ)".
- **Caveat:** the paper's table (1.3–2.6 nJ) and text (1 to 2 nJ) disagree on DRAM; quote the one used and attribute it.
- M. Horowitz, "1.1 Computing's energy problem (and what we can do about it)", ISSCC 2014 Digest of Technical Papers, pp. 10–14, doi:10.1109/ISSCC.2014.6757323. Copy read: [mirror (PDF)](https://gwern.net/doc/cs/hardware/2014-horowitz-2.pdf)

## R-209 · RISC-V ELF psABI: calling convention, relocations and relaxation

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the psABI source files on the master branch of RISC-V International's riscv-elf-psabi-doc repository (primary); tagged [Established].*

- **Calling conventions (riscv-cc.adoc):** the integer register table (x0 zero; x1 ra, not preserved; x2 sp, preserved; x3 gp and x4 tp, unallocatable; x5–x7 t0–t2, not preserved; x8–x9 s0–s1, preserved; x10–x17 a0–a7, not preserved; x18–x27 s2–s11, preserved; x28–x31 t3–t6, not preserved); "The base integer calling convention provides eight argument registers, a0-a7, the first two of which are also used to return values."; "The stack grows downwards (towards lower addresses) and the stack pointer shall be aligned to a 128-bit boundary upon procedure entry."; "Registers s0-s11 shall be preserved across procedure calls."; the ILP32 type table (char 1, short 2, int 4, long 4, long long 8, void * 4, double 8, each aligned to its size); LP64 makes long and pointers 8 bytes. [riscv-cc.adoc](https://github.com/riscv-non-isa/riscv-elf-psabi-doc/blob/master/riscv-cc.adoc)
- **ELF object files (riscv-elf.adoc):** "R_RISCV_CALL and R_RISCV_CALL_PLT relocations are associated with pairs of instructions (AUIPC+JALR) generated by the CALL or TAIL pseudoinstructions"; "With linker relaxation enabled, the AUIPC instruction in the AUIPC+JALR pair has both a R_RISCV_CALL or R_RISCV_CALL_PLT relocation and an R_RISCV_RELAX relocation"; "Procedure call linker relaxation allows the AUIPC+JALR pair to be relaxed to the JAL instruction when the procedure or PLT entry is within (-1MiB to +1MiB-2) of the instruction pair." [riscv-elf.adoc](https://github.com/riscv-non-isa/riscv-elf-psabi-doc/blob/master/riscv-elf.adoc)
- **Derived here (14-03):** every listing, relocation, address and layout in the chapter is reproduced with LLVM 18 (llvm-mc, ld.lld, clang) in verify_14_03.py.

## R-210 · Number formats: IEEE 754-2019, bfloat16 and OCP MX (primary re-check)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026; primary sources, tagged [Established]. Re-checks and extends [R-056](#r-056--low-precision-floating-point-formats) and [R-143](#r-143--block-scaled-ai-number-formats), whose MX details had come from secondary documentation.*

- **IEEE 754-2019:** IEEE SA standard page: "Active Standard IEEE 754-2019 … Superseding 754-2008 … Board Approval 2019-06-13 … Published: 2019-07-22"; an active revision project, P754, is listed. Identical adoption: ISO/IEC/IEEE 60559:2020. [IEEE SA](https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/754/6210/)
- **bfloat16:** Google Cloud Blog (S. Wang and P. Kanwar, 23 Aug 2019): "Bfloat16 is a custom 16-bit floating point format for machine learning that's comprised of one sign bit, eight exponent bits, and seven mantissa bits"; "bfloat16 multipliers are about half the size in silicon of a typical FP16 multiplier, and they are eight times smaller than an FP32 multiplier!" Google Cloud TPU documentation (updated Nov 2026): "By default, TPUs perform matrix multiplication operations with bfloat16 values and accumulations with IEEE float32 values"; "the bfloat16 on Cloud TPU does not support subnormals, so all subnormals are flushed to zero during the conversion." RISC-V's ratified BF16 extensions note that BF16 "is not an IEEE-754 standard format" but a valid format under IEEE 754's rules. [Google blog](https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/bfloat16-the-secret-to-high-performance-on-cloud-tpus) · [Cloud TPU docs](https://cloud.google.com/tpu/docs/bfloat16) · [RISC-V BF16 chapter](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/bfloat16.html)
- **OCP MX v1.0 (Sep 2023), read directly:** contributors AMD, Arm, Intel, Meta, Microsoft, NVIDIA and Qualcomm; MXFP8 (E5M2/E4M3), MXFP6 (E3M2/E2M3), MXFP4 (E2M1) and MXINT8, each with k = 32 and an E8M0 scale; "each block of k elements can be encoded in (w + kd) bits"; E8M0 has no Inf and a single NaN; E2M1 has bias 1, maximum ±6.0, no Inf or NaN. §6.1–6.2: the dot product's "internal precision … and order of operations is implementation-defined". §6.3 conversion: "Set X to be the largest power-of-two less than or equal to max(|Vi|), divided by the largest power-of-two representable in the element data type"; elements are quantized with roundTiesToEven, and normal numbers beyond the element maximum are clamped. MXINT8 elements carry an implicit scale (one sign bit, one integer bit, six fractional bits). [OCP MX v1.0 (PDF)](https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-microscaling-formats-mx-v1-0-spec-final-pdf)
- **RISC-V M and F chapters (manual 20260120):** division by zero returns an all-ones quotient and the dividend as remainder; signed overflow returns the dividend and remainder 0; rounding modes RNE, RTZ, RDN, RUP, RMM; canonical NaN 0x7fc00000; tininess detected after rounding (see R-201 for the manual).
- **Derived here (14-04):** the p² multiplier-area model (bfloat16 about half of FP16 and a ninth of FP32), consistent with Google's statements; all arithmetic results are machine-checked in verify_14_04.py.

## R-212 · AMD Zen 5 core (Hot Chips 2024)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from AMD's slides on hotchips.org (primary vendor disclosure); tagged [Established] as the vendor's own figures.*

- B. Cohen and M. Subramony (with M. Clark), AMD, "Next Generation 'Zen 5' Core", Hot Chips 2024 (HC36), Aug 2024: "8-wide dispatch, rename, retire"; "ROB/retire queue 448/224 1T/2T entries"; "D-Cache: 48KB, 12-way; 4 mem ops/cycle"; "L2-Cache: 1MB, 16-way"; "6 integer ALU". Also listed: L1I 32 KB 8-way with 2 × 32 B fetch per cycle; op cache of 6K instructions, 2 × 6-wide; decode 2 pipes × 4-wide; 240 integer physical registers (64-bit) and 384 vector registers (512-bit); 4 AGUs; L1 BTB 16K entries; return stack 52 entries; full 512-bit AVX-512 datapath; 32 MB L3 per 8-core complex; "up to" +16% IPC over Zen 4 (AMD endnote). [Hot Chips 2024 slides (PDF)](https://www.hc2024.hotchips.org/assets/program/conference/day2/24_HC2024.AMD.Cohen.Subramony.final.pdf)

## R-213 · Spectre and Meltdown: disclosure and CVEs

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 (primary: the Project Zero post and the MITRE CVE records; NVD returned 403); tagged [Established].*

- J. Horn, Google Project Zero, "Reading privileged memory with a side-channel", 3 Jan 2018: "We reported this issue to Intel, AMD and ARM on 2017-06-01"; "Variant 1: bounds check bypass (CVE-2017-5753) Variant 2: branch target injection (CVE-2017-5715) Variant 3: rogue data cache load (CVE-2017-5754)". [Project Zero](https://googleprojectzero.blogspot.com/2018/01/reading-privileged-memory-with-side.html)
- CVE-2017-5753 description: "Systems with microprocessors utilizing speculative execution and branch prediction may allow unauthorized disclosure of information to an attacker with local user access via a side-channel analysis." Records reserved 2017-02-01, public 2018-01-03, published 2018-01-04 (CNA: Intel). [CVE-2017-5753](https://cveawg.mitre.org/api/cve/CVE-2017-5753) · [CVE-2017-5715](https://cveawg.mitre.org/api/cve/CVE-2017-5715) · [CVE-2017-5754](https://cveawg.mitre.org/api/cve/CVE-2017-5754)
- Papers: M. Lipp et al., "Meltdown: Reading Kernel Memory from User Space", USENIX Security 2018; P. Kocher et al., "Spectre Attacks: Exploiting Speculative Execution", IEEE S&P 2019.
- **Later variant, read 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 14 audit (primary, MITRE CVE records):** CVE-2022-29900 (published 2022-07-12): "Mis-trained branch predictions for return instructions may allow arbitrary speculative code execution under certain microarchitecture-dependent conditions." CVE-2022-29901 (published 2022-07-12): "Intel microprocessor generations 6 to 8 are affected by a new Spectre variant that is able to bypass their retpoline mitigation in the kernel to leak arbitrary data." (Both are known as Retbleed.) [CVE-2022-29900](https://cveawg.mitre.org/api/cve/CVE-2022-29900) · [CVE-2022-29901](https://cveawg.mitre.org/api/cve/CVE-2022-29901)
- **ISA support for constant-time code (primary, RISC-V unprivileged manual 20260120, scalar cryptography chapter, read 20 Nov 2026):** the Zkt subset "allows CPU implementers to indicate to cryptographic software developers that a subset of RISC-V instructions are guaranteed to be implemented such that their execution latency is independent of the data values they operate on." [Scalar cryptography](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/scalar-crypto.html)

## R-214 · HBM3, HBM3E and HBM4: bandwidth and capacity

*Read on 20 Nov 2026. JEDEC's own pages (jedec.org) returned Cloudflare 403s, so the two JEDEC releases were read as verbatim HPCwire reprints (secondary copies); the Micron and Samsung pages are primary vendor pages. Tagged [Established] for the standards' headline figures and for the vendors' own part specifications.*

- **HBM3, JESD238 (JEDEC release, Arlington, Va., 27 Jan 2022):** "defining data rates of up to 6.4 Gb/s, equivalent to 819 GB/s per device" (6.4 × 1024 / 8 = 819.2); 16 independent channels, each with 2 pseudo-channels; 4-, 8- and 12-high TSV stacks with provision for 16-high; 8–32 Gb per layer; on-die ECC; 0.4 V low-swing interface and 1.1 V core supply. [HPCwire reprint](https://www.hpcwire.com/off-the-wire/jedec-publishes-hbm3-updated-to-high-bandwidth-memory-hbm-standard/)
- **HBM3E (vendor parts; the 2022 JEDEC release does not use the name):** Micron: "HBM3E has 1024 IO pins and the HBM3E pin speed of greater than 9.2Gbps achieves a rate higher than 1.2TB/s"; 24 GB 8-high and 36 GB 12-high stacks. [Micron HBM3E](https://www.micron.com/products/memory/hbm/hbm3e). Samsung: "Its 12-layer stack offers up to 1,180GB/s bandwidth at 9.2Gbps" (9.2 × 1024 / 8 = 1177.6); its HBM3 page gives "processing speeds up to 6.4Gbps and bandwidth that reaches 819GB/s". [Samsung HBM3E](https://semiconductor.samsung.com/dram/hbm/hbm3e/) · [Samsung HBM3](https://semiconductor.samsung.com/dram/hbm/hbm3/)
- **HBM4, JESD270-4 (JEDEC release, 16 Apr 2025):** "With transfer speeds up to 8 Gb/s across a 2048-bit interface, HBM4 boosts total bandwidth up to 2 TB/s"; 32 channels with 2 pseudo-channels each; 4- to 16-high stacks of 24 Gb or 32 Gb dies, up to 64 GB; vendor-specific VDDQ of 0.7, 0.75, 0.8 or 0.9 V. [HPCwire reprint](https://www.hpcwire.com/off-the-wire/jedec-releases-hbm4-standard-to-advance-ai-and-hpc-memory/)
- **Not verified:** whether a revision JESD238A covers HBM3E speeds, and JEDEC's own name for HBM3E.

## R-215 · RISC-V privileged ISA: virtual memory and traps

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the privileged volume of the ratified manual set 20260120 (Supervisor ISA 1.13, Machine ISA 1.13; see R-201) on docs.riscv.org (primary); tagged [Established]. The Sv32 bit layouts come from the spec's SVG figures ("Sv32 virtual address", "Sv32 page table entry", satp for SXLEN = 32), whose labels were read directly.*

- **Sv32:** "Sv32 implementations support a 32-bit virtual address space, divided into pages"; "The 20-bit VPN is translated into a 22-bit physical page number (PPN), while the 12-bit page offset is untranslated"; translation uses "a two-level page table"; "Sv32 page tables consist of 2^10 page-table entries (PTEs), each of four bytes. A page table is exactly the size of a page and must always be aligned to a page boundary. The physical page number of the root page table is stored in the satp register." "Any level of PTE may be a leaf PTE, so in addition to 4 KiB pages, Sv32 supports 4 MiB megapages." Virtual address: VPN[1] bits 31–22, VPN[0] bits 21–12, page offset bits 11–0. PTE: PPN[1] bits 31–20 (12 bits), PPN[0] bits 19–10, RSW bits 9–8, then D, A, G, U, X, W, R, V in bits 7–0; "When all three are zero, the PTE is a pointer to the next level of the page table; otherwise, it is a leaf PTE." [Supervisor chapter](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/priv/supervisor.html)
- **Translation algorithm (§11.1.3.2):** start at satp.ppn × PAGESIZE with i = LEVELS − 1 (Sv32: PAGESIZE = 2^12, LEVELS = 2, PTESIZE = 4); read the PTE at a + va.vpn[i] × PTESIZE; fault if V = 0 or (R = 0 and W = 1); a PTE with R or X set is a leaf, otherwise descend; a leaf above level 0 with nonzero low PPN bits is a misaligned superpage (page fault); check U against the privilege mode, then R/W/X; set A (and D for stores) or fault (Svade); pa.pgoff = va.pgoff, and for a superpage the low PPN fields come from the VPN.
- **Larger modes:** Sv39 translates a 27-bit VPN to a 44-bit PPN "via a three-level page table" with 2 MiB megapages and 1 GiB gigapages; Sv48 uses four levels (adding 512 GiB terapages); Sv57 adds a fifth: "in addition to 4 KiB pages, Sv57 supports 2 MiB megapages, 1 GiB gigapages, 512 GiB terapages, and 256 TiB petapages" (read 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 14 audit, [Supervisor-level ISA](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/priv/supervisor.html)). The 4 KiB base page was chosen deliberately: "After much deliberation, we have settled on a conventional page size of 4 KiB for both RV32 and RV64"; "The TLB reach problem is ameliorated by transparent superpage support in modern operating systems."
- **satp and SFENCE.VMA:** satp (SXLEN = 32) holds MODE in bit 31, ASID in bits 30–22 and the root PPN in bits 21–0 ("We store the ASID and the page table base address in the same CSR to allow the pair to be changed atomically on a context switch"); SFENCE.VMA "is used to flush any local hardware caches related to address translation", and the remote-hart procedure (fence, interprocessor interrupt, remote SFENCE.VMA, acknowledgement) "is, of course, the RISC-V analog to a TLB shootdown".
- **Traps (machine chapter):** "When a trap is taken into M-mode, mepc is written with the virtual address of the instruction that was interrupted or that encountered the exception"; mtval holds exception-specific information or zero; in vectored mode, interrupts set pc to BASE + 4 × cause ("a machine-mode timer interrupt … causes the pc to be set to BASE+0x1c"). mcause exception codes: 8 environment call from U-mode, 9 from S-mode, 11 from M-mode, 12 instruction page fault, 13 load page fault, 15 store/AMO page fault; interrupt codes: 1/3 supervisor/machine software, 5/7 supervisor/machine timer, 9/11 supervisor/machine external. xRET restores the privilege mode from xPP and the interrupt enable from xPIE. [Machine chapter](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/priv/machine.html)

## R-216 · PCI Express generations 1.0–8.0

*Read on 20 Nov 2026. pcisig.com and businesswire.com returned Cloudflare/Akamai 403s, so PCI-SIG's releases for 6.0, 7.0 and 8.0 were read as verbatim trade-press reprints (secondary copies), and the history of 1.0–5.0 from an article by a PCI-SIG board member (secondary, expert). Tagged [Established] for rates and encodings.*

- **Generations 1.0–5.0** (D. Das Sharma, PCI-SIG Board Member and Intel Fellow, "What's the Difference Going from PCIe 3.0 to PCIe 6.0?", *Electronic Design*): "PCIe 2.0 specification doubled the data rate to 5.0 GT/s in 2006. The first two generations of PCIe technology used 8b/10b encoding, incurring a 25% encoding overhead"; for PCIe 3.0, "a strategic decision was made to increase the data rate to 8.0 GT/s instead of 10.0 GT/s. This was augmented with a new 128b/130b encoding mechanism"; "October 2017: PCIe 4.0 Specification at 16.0 GT/s"; "May 2019: PCIe 5.0 Specification at 32.0 GT/s". [Electronic Design](https://www.electronicdesign.com/industrial-automation/article/21136215/whats-the-difference-going-from-pcie-30-to-pcie-60)
- **PCIe 6.0** (PCI-SIG release, January 2022; reprint dated 14 Jan 2022): "64 GT/s raw data rate and up to 256 GB/s via x16 configuration; Pulse Amplitude Modulation with four levels (PAM4) signaling…; Lightweight Forward Error Correct (FEC) and Cyclic Redundancy Check (CRC) mitigate the bit error rate increase associated with PAM4 signaling; Flit (flow control unit) based encoding". The original release date (believed 11 Jan 2022) was not confirmed. [Reprint](https://www.signalintegrityjournal.com/articles/2390-pci-sig-releases-pcie-60-specification-delivering-record-performance-to-power-big-data-applications)
- **PCIe 7.0** (released to members 11 Jun 2025; reprint dated 12 Jun 2025): "reaching 128.0 GT/s"; up to 512 GB/s bidirectional on x16; PAM4 and Flit encoding; "continues our long-standing tradition of doubling the IO bandwidth every three years" (Al Yanes, PCI-SIG). [Reprint](https://www.signalintegrityjournal.com/articles/3984-pci-sig-releases-pcie-70-specification-to-support-the-bandwidth-demands-of-artificial-intelligence-at-1280-gt-s-transfer-rates)
- **PCIe 8.0** (announced Aug 2025): target 256.0 GT/s, up to 1 TB/s bidirectional on x16, "planned for release to members by 2028". [Reprint](https://www.cotsjournalonline.com/pci-sig-announces-pci-express-8-0-specification-to-reach-256-0-gt-s/)
- **Derived:** PCI-SIG's x16 figures are bidirectional totals of the raw rate: 64 GT/s × 16 lanes × 2 directions / 8 = 256 GB/s, before Flit and FEC overhead.

## R-217 · AMBA AXI Issue L and CXL 4.0

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from Arm's documentation service and Arm's AMBA pages, and from the CXL Consortium's press release and specification page (all primary). Only the CXL press release and web page were read, not the CXL specification, whose download page restricts processing by AI systems. Tagged [Established].*

- **AXI:** Arm, "AMBA AXI Protocol Specification", ARM IHI 0022, Issue L, "Date of issue 27 Aug 2025" (defines AXI5, AXI5-Lite, ACE5-Lite; Issue L added a credited transport option, Arm Compression Technology and other features). Issue J (1 Mar 2023): "AXI3, AXI4, AXI4-Lite, ACE, and ACE5 content removed"; Issue H (2020) adopted the Manager/Subordinate terminology; first release Issue A, Jun 2003. The five channels are AW, W, B, AR and R. Arm: "AMBA specifications are royalty free and platform independent, and can be used with any processor architecture." [AXI Issue L (PDF)](https://documentation-service.arm.com/static/68b03beb01ae952d9559f9eb) · [Arm AMBA](https://www.arm.com/architecture/system-architectures/amba)
- **CXL 4.0** (CXL Consortium release, Beaverton, Ore., 18 Nov 2025): "CXL 4.0 specification doubles bandwidth from 64GTs to 128GTs, adds support for bundled ports, and enhances memory RAS features"; keeps the 256-byte Flit of CXL 3.x, adds native x2 links and up to four retimers, and is backward compatible with CXL 3.x, 2.0, 1.1 and 1.0. As of 20 Nov 2026 the specification page still offers CXL 4.0. [Press release (PDF)](https://computeexpresslink.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/CXL_4.0-Specification-Release_FINAL_Website-Copy.pdf) · [Specification page](https://computeexpresslink.org/cxl-specification/)

## R-218 · RISC-V RVWMO, Ztso and atomics

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the unprivileged volume of the ratified manual set 20260120 on docs.riscv.org (primary; see R-201); tagged [Established].*

- **RVWMO (Chapter 17, "RVWMO Memory Consistency Model, Version 2.0"):** "Under RVWMO, code running on a single hart appears to execute in order from the perspective of other memory instructions in the same hart, but memory instructions from another hart may observe the memory instructions from the first hart being executed in a different order. Therefore, multithreaded code may require explicit synchronization to guarantee ordering between memory instructions from different harts. The base RISC-V ISA provides a FENCE instruction for this purpose"; "The RVWMO memory model is defined in terms of the global memory order, a total ordering of the memory operations produced by all harts." [RVWMO](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/rvwmo.html)
- **Ztso:** the manual's table of contents lists Chapter 18, "'Ztso' Extension for Total Store Ordering, Version 1.0".
- **Same-address ordering:** the explanatory chapter notes that RVWMO enforces "CoRR (Coherence for Read-Read pairs)", part of "sequential consistency per location". [Explanatory material](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/mm-eplan.html)
- **A extension:** "LR.W loads a word from the address in rs1, places the sign-extended value in rd, and registers a reservation set"; "SC.W conditionally writes a word in rs2 to the address in rs1: the SC.W succeeds only if the reservation is still valid and the reservation set contains the bytes being written. If the SC.W succeeds, the instruction writes the word in rs2 to memory, and it writes zero to rd. If the SC.W fails, the instruction does not write to memory, and it writes a nonzero value to rd." "The A extension comprises instructions provided by the Zaamo and Zalrsc extensions." §12.1.3, "Eventual Success of Store-Conditional Instructions": "The Zalrsc extension defines constrained LR/SC loops", which "must comprise at most 16 instructions placed sequentially in memory", with only base-I instructions (no loads, stores or backward jumps) between the LR and the SC. [A extension](https://docs.riscv.org/reference/isa/v20260120/unpriv/a-st-ext.html) The guarantee (read 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 14 audit): "If a hart H enters a constrained LR/SC loop, the execution environment must guarantee that one of the following events eventually occurs: H or some other hart executes a successful SC to the reservation set …; some other hart executes an unconditional store or AMO …; H executes a branch or jump that exits the constrained LR/SC loop; H traps." The commentary calls it "an architectural guarantee of livelock freedom" and notes "Earlier versions of this specification imposed a stronger starvation-freedom guarantee."

## R-219 · x86-TSO (Sewell et al., CACM 2010)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the authors' final version of the CACM Research Highlights paper (dated 17 May 2010) on the Cambridge weak-memory pages (primary, author copy); tagged [Established].*

- P. Sewell, S. Sarkar, S. Owens, F. Zappa Nardelli and M. O. Myreen, "x86-TSO: A Rigorous and Usable Programmer's Model for x86 Multiprocessors", *Communications of the ACM* 53(7), July 2010. [Author version (PDF)](https://www.cl.cam.ac.uk/~pes20/weakmemory/cacm.pdf)
- **SB:** for x86, with x and y initially 0, "if two processors respectively write 1 to x and y and then read from y and x …, it is possible for both to read 0 in the same execution … this result cannot arise from any interleaving"; "Allowed Final State: Proc 0:EAX=0 ∧ Proc 1:EBX=0"; explained "as a visible consequence of store buffering".
- **The model:** shared memory, "a global lock to indicate when a particular hardware thread has exclusive access to memory, and one store buffer per hardware thread"; "The store buffers are FIFO and a reading thread must read its most recent buffered write, if there is one, to that address; otherwise reads are satisfied from shared memory"; "An MFENCE instruction flushes the store buffer of that thread"; a LOCK'd instruction takes the global lock and flushes the buffer at its end.

## R-220 · NVIDIA H100 (Hopper) specifications

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 by this session's fact-finding pass (quotes recorded verbatim at the time): the product page and the Hopper blog are primary; the architecture whitepaper (V1.02) was read only as a third-party mirror and carries preliminary figures, so its performance numbers are not used. Tagged [Established] as vendor specifications.*

- **Product page, H100 SXM** ("Product Specifications"): FP64 34 TFLOPS; FP64 Tensor 67; FP32 67; TF32 Tensor 989*; BF16 and FP16 Tensor 1,979*; FP8 Tensor 3,958*; INT8 3,958 TOPS*; "GPU Memory 80GB"; "GPU Memory Bandwidth 3.35TB/s"; "Max Thermal Design Power (TDP) Up to 700W (configurable)"; NVLink 900 GB/s; PCIe Gen5 128 GB/s; footnote "* With sparsity". [NVIDIA H100](https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/h100/)
- **Hopper blog** (NVIDIA Technical Blog, "NVIDIA Hopper Architecture In-Depth", 22 Mar 2022): H100 SXM5 has "8 GPCs, 66 TPCs, 2 SMs/TPC, 132 SMs per GPU; 128 FP32 CUDA Cores per SM, 16896 FP32 CUDA Cores per GPU; 4 fourth-generation Tensor Cores per SM, 528 per GPU; 80 GB HBM3, 5 HBM3 stacks, 10 512-bit memory controllers; 50 MB L2 cache"; each SM has 256 KB of combined L1 cache and shared memory; GH100 is built on TSMC 4N with 80 billion transistors on 814 mm²; "The Sparsity feature exploits fine-grained structured sparsity in deep learning networks, doubling the performance of standard Tensor Core operations." [Hopper In-Depth](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-hopper-architecture-in-depth/)
- **Thread-block clusters (Hopper blog, read 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 14 audit):** "Clusters enable multiple thread blocks running concurrently across multiple SMs to synchronize and collaboratively fetch and exchange data." "With clusters, it is possible for all the threads to directly access other SM's shared memory with load, store, and atomic operations. This feature is called distributed shared memory (DSMEM)."
- **Derived (not stated by NVIDIA):** dense BF16/FP16 Tensor ≈ 989.5 TFLOPS (half the sparse figure); the FP32 rate implies a boost clock of about 67 × 10¹² / (16,896 × 2) ≈ 1.98 GHz. NVIDIA's product page states no clock.

## R-221 · NVIDIA Blackwell systems (GB200 NVL72, DGX B200)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 by this session's fact-finding pass from NVIDIA's product pages (primary); tagged [Established] as vendor specifications. No standalone per-GPU B200 power figure was found on these pages.*

- **GB200 NVL72** (specifications table): a GB200 superchip (1 Grace CPU, 2 Blackwell GPUs) lists NVFP4 Tensor 40 PFLOPS sparse / 20 dense, FP8/FP6 20 PFLOPS and FP16/BF16 10 PFLOPS (sparse; "Dense is one-half sparse spec shown"), and HBM3E "372 GB, 16 TB/s"; the 72-GPU rack has 13.4 TB of HBM3E at 576 TB/s. [GB200 NVL72](https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/gb200-nvl72/)
- **DGX B200** (8 Blackwell GPUs): 1,440 GB of HBM3e at 64 TB/s in total; FP4 Tensor 144 PFLOPS sparse / 72 dense; maximum system power about 14.3 kW. [DGX B200](https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/dgx-b200/)
- **Derived per GPU:** from the rack, 13.4 TB / 72 ≈ 186 GB and 576 / 72 = 8 TB/s; from DGX B200, 1,440 / 8 = 180 GB and 64 / 8 = 8 TB/s. The two capacity figures differ (186 against 180 GB), presumably by product configuration; the bandwidth agrees.

## R-222 · Google TPU v4 and later TPUs

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 by this session's fact-finding pass: the TPU v4 paper from the authors' arXiv version (primary, author copy) and Google Cloud's TPU documentation (primary). Tagged [Established] for the paper's figures and Google's published specifications.*

- **TPU v4** (N. P. Jouppi et al., "TPU v4: An Optically Reconfigurable Supercomputer for Machine Learning with Hardware Support for Embeddings", ISCA 2023, doi:10.1145/3579371.3589350; [arXiv 2304.01433](https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.01433)): "Each TC contains four 128x128 Matrix Multiply Units (MXUs) and a Vector Processing Unit (VPU) with 128 lanes (16 ALUs per lane) and a 16 MiB Vector Memory (VMEM)"; 2 TensorCores per chip; Table 4: "Peak TFLOPS 275 (bf16 or int8) | 123 (bf16)" for v4 against v3, "HBM2 capacity, BW 32 GiB, 1200 GB/s | 32 GiB, 900 GB/s"; clock 1050 MHz; 7 nm, under 600 mm², 22 billion transistors; 6 ICI links at 50 GB/s; systems of up to 4096 chips; first deployed 2020.
- **SparseCores (TPU v4 abstract, arXiv 2304.01433, read 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 14 audit):** "Each TPU v4 includes SparseCores, dataflow processors that accelerate models that rely on embeddings by 5x-7x yet use only 5% of die area and power."
- **Derived:** 2 × 4 × 128 × 128 MACs × 2 FLOP × 1.05 GHz = 275.25 TFLOPS, matching the paper's 275.
- **Later generations** (Google Cloud TPU documentation, per chip): v5p 459 TFLOPS BF16, 95 GiB HBM at 2765 GB/s, pods of 8960 chips; v6e (Trillium) 918 TFLOPS BF16, 32 GiB at 1638 GB/s, pods of 256; TPU7x (Ironwood) 2307 TFLOPS BF16 and 4614 FP8, 192 GiB HBM at 7380 GB/s, two TensorCores (built as two chiplets, each with one TensorCore, two SparseCores and 96 GiB HBM), pods of 9216 chips. [v6e](https://cloud.google.com/tpu/docs/v6e) · [TPU7x](https://cloud.google.com/tpu/docs/tpu7x)
- **Caution (not used):** the v6e page's statement of two 256 × 256 MXUs per TensorCore would imply a clock near 3.5 GHz at 918 TFLOPS, which is implausible; Google states no v6e clock there. No derivation in this curriculum uses that MXU count.

## R-223 · SPEC CPU 2017 and SPEC CPU 2026

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 by this session's fact-finding pass from spec.org (primary); tagged [Established].*

- **CPU 2017:** 43 benchmarks in four suites (SPECspeed 2017 Integer and Floating Point: time for one copy; SPECrate 2017 Integer and Floating Point: throughput of N concurrent copies). A benchmark's ratio is the reference machine's time over the system under test's time (multiplied by the number of copies for SPECrate); the overall metric is the geometric mean of the ratios; with three runs the median is used. Worked examples from the overview: "The reference machine ran 600.perlbench_s in 1775 seconds. … 1775/354.329738 = 5.009458"; "8*(1592/541.52471) = 23.518776". Base and peak: "The base metrics (such as SPECspeed2017_int_base) require that all modules of a given language in a suite must be compiled using the same flags, in the same order. All reported results must include the base metric. The optional peak metrics … allow greater flexibility. Different compiler options may be used for each benchmark, and feedback-directed optimization is allowed." [Overview](https://www.spec.org/cpu2017/Docs/overview.html)
- **Retirement and successor:** "With the release of SPEC CPU 2026, SPEC has retired SPEC CPU 2017 as of November 17, 2026." SPEC CPU 2026 was released on 5 May 2026; it "Includes 52 benchmarks, up from 43, with more than two times the lines of source code", from 38 applications; it keeps the SPECspeed/SPECrate and base/peak structure; SPECspeed Integer now has explicit parallelism and its memory requirement rose from 16 GB to 64 GB. [CPU 2017 page](https://www.spec.org/cpu2017/) · [Press release, 5 May 2026](https://www.spec.org/pressreleases/2026/20260505-spec-releases-cpu-2026-benchmark-suite/) · [CPU 2026 overview](https://www.spec.org/cpu2026/Docs/overview.html)

## R-224 · MLPerf divisions and scenarios

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 by this session's fact-finding pass from MLCommons' rules on GitHub (primary; inference rules at commit d3eba2f, training rules at commit 14bc89f). The rules change every round; cite the commit and date.*

- **Inference:** "The Closed division requires using pre-processing, post-processing, and model that is equivalent to the reference or alternative implementation. The closed division allows calibration for quantization and does not allow any retraining." "The Open division allows using arbitrary pre- or post-processing and model, including retraining. The qualified name 'MLPerf Open' must be used when referring to an Open Division suite result". Scenarios: single stream (90th-percentile latency), multistream (99th-percentile latency), server/interactive (Poisson arrivals; maximum throughput under a latency bound) and offline (throughput). [Inference rules](https://github.com/mlcommons/inference_policies/blob/master/inference_rules.adoc)
- **Training:** "The Closed division requires using the same preprocessing, model, training method, and quality target as the reference implementation"; the Open division allows arbitrary data, preprocessing, model or method. The current closed-division table (labelled v6.1) lists FLUX.1, GPT_OSS_20B, Llama31_8b, Llama2_70B_LoRA, deepseekv3_671b, Qwen3.5-397B-A17B and dlrm_v4_hstu. [Training rules](https://github.com/mlcommons/training_policies/blob/master/training_rules.adoc)

## R-225 · Geekbench 6 scoring and Geekbench 7

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 by this session's fact-finding pass from Primate Labs' documents (primary). Geekbench 7's internals could not be read (404/403), so its baseline and weights are not verified.*

- **Geekbench 6** ("Geekbench 6 Benchmark Internals", May 2024): "Geekbench 6 scores are calibrated against a baseline score of 2,500 (which is the score of a Dell Precision 3460 with a Core i7-12700 processor)"; the composite is "computed using a weighted arithmetic mean of the subsection scores. The subsection scores are computed using the geometric mean of the scores of the workloads contained in that subsection", with weights Integer 65% and Floating Point 35%; multi-core uses a "shared task" model ("each thread processes part of a larger shared task"), where Geekbench 5 ran separate tasks per thread. [Internals (PDF)](https://www.geekbench.com/doc/geekbench6-benchmark-internals.pdf)
- **Geekbench 7** (Primate Labs blog, 23 Jul 2026): "a major update that includes new media workloads, a smarter multi-core benchmark, and a refreshed GPU benchmark". [Announcement](https://www.primatelabs.com/blog/2026/07/geekbench-7/)

## R-226 · Google's first TPU (Jouppi et al., ISCA 2017)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 14 audit from the authors' arXiv version (primary, author copy); tagged [Established].*

- N. P. Jouppi et al., "In-Datacenter Performance Analysis of a Tensor Processing Unit", ISCA 2017; [arXiv 1704.04760](https://arxiv.org/abs/1704.04760).
- **Matrix unit:** "The matrix unit holds one 64 KiB tile of weights plus one for double-buffering (to hide the 256 cycles it takes to shift a tile in)." "Figure 4 shows that data flows in from the left, and the weights are loaded from the top." "The weights are preloaded, and take effect with the advancing wave alongside the first data of a new block."
- **Dataflow, compared with other work:** "the TPU stores the weight matrix tile while streaming the other input and the pre-activation partial sums" (that is, weight-stationary).
- **Sparsity:** "This unit is designed for dense matrices. Sparse architectural support was omitted for time-to-deploy reasons."

## R-227 · Semiconductor and IC package thermal metrics (TI SPRA953D)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the PDF on ti.com (primary); tagged [Established].*

- D. Edwards and H. Nguyen, "Semiconductor and IC Package Thermal Metrics," Texas Instruments application note SPRA953D, December 2003, revised March 2024. [PDF](https://www.ti.com/lit/an/spra953d/spra953d.pdf)
- "In light of the fact that RθJA is not a characteristic of the package by itself but of the package, PCB, and other environmental factors, it is best used as a comparison of package thermal performance between different companies."
- Measurement conditions are those of the EIA/JESD51 series (JESD51-1, -2, -5, -6, -7, -9 are cited).
- "In an attempt to provide the user community with a thermal metric to estimate in-use junction temperatures from measured case temperatures, a new thermal metric, ΨJT, has been adopted by the industry (JESD51-2). The metric is defined by the Greek character psi (Ψ) rather than theta (θ) because ΨJT is not a true thermal resistance." "ΨJT should not be used when application of a heat sink is intended."

## R-228 · Silicon thermal properties

- Ioffe Institute, *New Semiconductor Materials: Characteristics and Properties*, Si thermal properties: specific heat 0.7 J g⁻¹ °C⁻¹, thermal conductivity 1.3 W cm⁻¹ °C⁻¹, thermal diffusivity 0.8 cm²/s at 300 K. [Si thermal](https://www.ioffe.ru/SVA/NSM/Semicond/Si/thermal.html)
- Ioffe Si mechanical properties: density 2.329 g/cm³. [Si mechanical](https://www.ioffe.ru/SVA/NSM/Semicond/Si/mechanic.html)
- V. Palankovski, doctoral thesis, TU Wien, material parameters: κ₃₀₀ = 148 W/(m·K) for Si. [Thesis section](https://www.iue.tuwien.ac.at/phd/palankovski/node34.html)
- The spread (130 against 148 W/(m·K)) reflects sample purity and measurement method; the chapter quotes the range.

## R-230 · OPA627 settling-time specifications (TI SBOS165C)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the datasheet PDF on ti.com (primary); tagged [Established].*

- Texas Instruments, *OPA6x7 Precision, High-Speed JFET Operational Amplifiers*, SBOS165C, August 2000, revised January 2025. [PDF](https://www.ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/opa627.pdf)
- Front page: "Fast settling time: OPA627: 550ns to 0.01%; OPA637: 450ns to 0.01%". Packages: SOIC-8 (D) and TO-99 (LMC).
- Section 5.6, OPA627BU and OPA627AU (SOIC): gain-bandwidth 45 MHz typical (gain = 1 V/V, C_L = 30 pF); slew rate 150 V/µs typical (10 V step, gain = −1 V/V); settling time (10 V step, gain = −1 V/V, C_L = 30 pF) 120 ns to 0.01% and 110 ns to 0.1%, typical.
- Section 5.7, OPA627AM, BM and SM (TO-99): gain-bandwidth 16 MHz typical; slew rate 55 V/µs typical (40 V/µs minimum); settling time (10 V step, gain = −1 V/V) 550 ns to 0.01% and 450 ns to 0.1%, typical.

## R-231 · Linux power-allocator thermal governor (PID form and defaults)

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the kernel source tree, `Documentation/driver-api/thermal/power_allocator.rst` on the master branch of the Linux repository on GitHub (primary); tagged [Established].*

- "The power allocator governor implements a Proportional-Integral-Derivative controller (PID controller) with temperature as the control input and power as the controlled output: P_max = k_p * e + k_i * err_integral + k_d * diff_err + sustainable_power", with e = desired_temperature − current_temperature. [power_allocator.rst](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/master/Documentation/driver-api/thermal/power_allocator.rst)
- Two proportional constants: `k_po` during temperature overshoot (above the desired temperature) and `k_pu` during undershoot. Defaults: k_pu = 2 × sustainable_power/(desired_temperature − switch_on_temp); k_po = sustainable_power/(desired_temperature − switch_on_temp).
- "Typically `k_i` is set low (1 or 2) and `integral_cutoff` is 0"; for `k_d`, "It's recommended to leave it as the default: 0."
- "For reference, the sustainable power of a 4" phone is typically 2000mW, while on a 10" tablet is around 4500mW (may vary depending on screen size)."
- Perishable: kernel documentation changes between releases; recheck the current file before quoting defaults.

## R-232 · ACPI 6.5/6.6 passive-cooling equations

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from the UEFI Forum's HTML edition of the specification (primary); tagged [Established].*

- UEFI Forum, *Advanced Configuration and Power Interface (ACPI) Specification*, version 6.5, chapter 11 (Thermal Management). [Chapter 11](https://uefi.org/specs/ACPI/6.5/11_Thermal_Management.html)
- §11.1.5.1 (Processor Clock Throttling), Equation #1: ΔP [%] = _TC1 × (T_n − T_(n−1)) + _TC2 × (T_n − T_t), where T_n is the current temperature and T_t the target (_PSV); "The two coefficients _TC1 and _TC2 and the sampling period _TSP are hardware-dependent constants the OEM must supply to OSPM."
- Equation #2: P_n = P_(n−1) + HW[−ΔP], with Minimum % ≤ P_n ≤ 100%; values outside the range are truncated; Minimum% is the _MTL limit (or 0%); HW maps to the next available frequency setting.
- §11.4.22 (_TSP): the sampling period is returned "in tenths of seconds"; "The granularity of the sampling period is 0.1 seconds." §11.4.17 (_TFP) gives an optional fast sampling period in milliseconds.
- Re-read 20 Nov 2026 for the Course 15 audit: version 6.6 (uefi.org/specs/ACPI/6.6/11_Thermal_Management.html) carries the same Equations #1 and #2 in §11.1.5.1 and the same `_TSP` text in §11.4.22. §11.4.17 `_TFP`: "The granularity of the sampling period is 1 milliseconds"; "If both _TFP and _TSP are present in a Thermal Zone, _TFP overrides _TSP." [ACPI 6.6, chapter 11](https://uefi.org/specs/ACPI/6.6/11_Thermal_Management.html)
- Perishable: check for a newer ACPI version before citing section numbers.

## R-233 · Arm Helium (MVE): cores, MAC rates and uplift claims

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from Arm's newsroom, product pages, developer blogs and a March 2020 Arm white paper (primary, vendor); gathered by a research subagent; quotations as it recorded them. Tagged [Established] for architecture facts and dates; uplift figures are vendor claims.*

- **Architecture:** Helium is the M-Profile Vector Extension (MVE) of Armv8.1-M, announced 14 Feb 2019: "Arm Helium technology is a new M-Profile Vector Extension bringing enhanced compute capabilities to the Armv8.1-M architecture." The same release promises "up to 15x performance uplift for machine learning and up to 5x uplift to signal processing tasks" for future Cortex-M processors. [Armv8.1-M announcement](https://newsroom.arm.com/blog/next-generation-armv8-1-m-architecture)
- **Vector registers:** "In Helium, the vector size is 128-bit"; "Helium only has 8 vector registers", reusing the floating-point registers. [Arm white paper, Mar 2020 (PDF)](https://www.arm.com/-/media/files/pdf/white-paper/blending-dsp-and-ml-features-into-a-low-power-general-purpose-processor.pdf)
- **Cortex-M55 (announced 10 Feb 2020):** dual-beat implementation, 64 bits of compute per cycle; "two 32x32 MACs per cycle", four 16-bit and eight 8-bit MACs per cycle (developer blog and white paper). Claim: "up to a 15x uplift in ML performance and a 5x uplift in DSP performance ... compared to previous Cortex-M generations". [M55 announcement](https://newsroom.arm.com/news/new-ai-technology-from-arm-delivers-unprecedented-on-device-intelligence-for-iot)
- **Cortex-M85 (26 Apr 2022):** dual-beat Helium; "A 30% scalar performance uplift compared to the Cortex-M7". [M85 announcement](https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-expands-total-solutions-for-iot-portfolio-to-continue-delivering-transformative-innovation-to-ecosystem)
- **Cortex-M52 (22 Nov 2023):** "Optional single-beat Helium, supporting up to 1 x 32-bit MACs/cycle, 2 x 16-bit MACs/cycle, 4 x 8-bit MACs/cycle"; claim "up to 2.7x performance uplift for digital signal processing compared to previous Cortex-M generations". [M52 product page](https://www.arm.com/products/silicon-ip-cpu/cortex-m/cortex-m52), [M52 announcement](https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-cortex-m52)
- **Cortex-M42 (10 Nov 2026):** Armv8.1-M with Helium; "up to 3x the vector compute capability and 2x the vector power efficiency of Cortex-M33". Beat count and MACs per cycle not found. [M42 announcement](https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-cortex-m42-accelerate-edge-ai-innovation)
- **[Contested] baselines:** none of the pages read names the core or benchmark behind "15x/5x" or "2.7x" ("previous Cortex-M generations"). Treat them as vendor best cases.
- Perishable: Arm's own Helium technology page still listed only M55 and M85 when read.

## R-234 · IEEE P802.3dj: status and electrical signaling

*Read on 20 Nov 2026 from IEEE 802.3 task-force documents and IEEE SA governance agendas (primary; fetched with curl through the build proxy by a research subagent). Tagged [Established]; the final draft text (members only) was not read.*

- **Status:** the third SA recirculation ballot on Draft 3.3 (25 Sep – 10 Oct 2026) returned 133 approve (97%) with 8 comments, "All out of scope" ([Nov 2026 task-force report](https://www.ieee802.org/3/minutes/nov26/1126_3dj_open_report.pdf)). The 8 Dec 2026 RevCom agenda lists "P802.3dj/Draft 3.3 IEEE Draft Standard for Ethernet Amendment 13", and the SASB meets 9 Dec 2026 ([RevCom agenda](https://standards.ieee.org/wp-content/uploads/import/governance/revcom/revcom-agenda.pdf); rolling URL). Not yet an approved standard as of 20 Nov 2026. The PAR (approved 3 Dec 2022) was recommended for extension to 31 Dec 2027 on 21 Sep 2026.
- **Electrical signaling:** key motion (May 2023): "Adopt differential PAM4 signaling as the basis for all of the 200 Gbps/lane passive copper cable and backplane PMDs and adopt RS(544,514,10) as the only FEC" ([key motions](https://www.ieee802.org/3/dj/projdoc/KeyMotions_3dj_240516.pdf)). Baseline proposal (Jan 2024): "Nominal signaling rate 106.25 GBd, PAM4 modulation" for chip-to-chip; "106.25 GBd ± 50 ppm" for 800GBASE-KR4 and 1.6TBASE-KR8 ([baseline](https://www.ieee802.org/3/dj/public/24_01/ran_3dj_01a_2401.pdf)).
- **Arithmetic (ours):** 106.25 GBd × 2 bit = 212.5 Gb/s = 200 × (257/256) × (544/514); its Nyquist frequency is the 53.125 GHz of the loss objectives (R-141). Optical inner-FEC lanes run at 113.4375 GBd (Table 174-6, per a Draft 3.1 comment); do not confuse the two.
- Perishable: re-check approval after the 9 Dec 2026 SASB meeting.
