TI white paper SWRA349 (2010, secondary): "using 30mA peak current versus 15mA peak current only slightly reduce the effective capacity of a CR2032 (9% on average depending on vendor)", "Some vendors ... only achieve app. 50% of rated capacity", and a reservoir capacitor gave "a solid >40% improvement" for the worst cells ([SWRA349](https://www.ti.com/lit/wp/swra349/swra349.pdf)). No manufacturer figure was found. [Contested]
- **EEMBC ULPMark-CoreProfile** ([eembc.org/ulpmark](https://www.eembc.org/ulpmark/ulp-cp/scores.php)): a one-second duty cycle with the active part about 3% of the runtime; "The ULPMark-CP score is computed by first taking the inverse of the average power (in µW) ... and then multiplying by 1000"; examples: Microchip ATSAML10E16A 277 at 3.0 V (certified, 2019-03-01); onsemi RSL10 1090 at 3.0 V (certified, 2018-02-08); Renesas RL78 R7F102GGE2DFB 292 at 3.0 V (vendor score, not certified, 2026-10-17). [Established]

## R-312 · Hardware security: certification schemes, TrustZone-M, regulation, PUFs, attacks

*Read on 21 Nov 2026 by a research subagent with curl and pdftotext (primary unless marked). developer.arm.com, Nordic's notices and Springer pages blocked automated access; ISO/IEC 19790 is paywalled.*

- **PSA Certified** ([psacertified.org](https://www.psacertified.org/)): Level 1 is a questionnaire reviewed by a lab; Level 2 is "a laboratory evaluation of a PSA Root of Trust (PSA-RoT) to provide evidence that it can protect against scalable software attacks" (25 days); Level 3 "includes more attack types, more sophisticated side channel and perturbation attacks as well as physical attacks" (35 man-days); Level 4 iSE/SE uses "a higher attack potential (AP=25)"; Level 2 guidance: "Cryptographic keys stored in the PSA-RoT should be unique per device to prevent creation of a honeypot for physical attacks". Site counter in Nov 2026: "319 Certifications" from "124 Companies". GlobalPlatform assumed governance on 16 Sep 2025, when "more than 250" products were certified. [Established]
- **TrustZone for Armv8-M** (Arm white paper, 2015, and arm.com product pages): Secure and Non-secure states "orthogonal to the existing Thread and Handler modes"; the division "is memory map based" (SAU and IDAU) "without the need of a Secure Monitor exception handler"; entry into Secure code through an SG instruction in Non-Secure Callable memory, return with BXNS. Cores: Cortex-M23, M33, M35P (with physical-attack resilience), M55 and M85 (Armv8.1-M, adding PACBTI). Trusted Firmware-M implements the Secure Processing Environment. [Established]
- **SESIP and Common Criteria:** SESIP "has been adopted by CEN and CENELEC as a European Standard (EN 17927)" with five assurance levels based on Common Criteria (GlobalPlatform, primary); Common Criteria defines seven EALs from EAL1 "functionally tested" to EAL7 "formally verified design and tested", with vulnerability analysis rising from AVA_VAN.1 to AVA_VAN.5 (CC:2022 text); the CC portal lists 1753 certified products, 748 of them ICs and smart-card devices (Nov 2026); the EU's EUCC scheme has applied since 27 Feb 2025. [Established]
- **FIPS 140-3** (NIST CSRC): four security levels; approved 22 Mar 2019, effective 22 Sep 2019, based on ISO/IEC 19790:2012; FIPS 140-2 modules are accepted "through September 21, 2026" before moving to the Historical list (one NIST table says 22 Sep 2026; no completion notice found by 21 Nov 2026). [Established; transition wording inconsistent]
- **EU Cyber Resilience Act**, Regulation (EU) 2024/2847 ([Commission page](https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/cyber-resilience-act) and the legal text, Article 71): in force 10 Dec 2024; reporting obligations (Article 14) from 11 Sep 2026; notified-body provisions from 11 Jun 2026; main obligations from 11 Dec 2027. Annex III Class I lists "Microcontrollers with security-related functionalities"; actively exploited vulnerabilities require an early warning within 24 hours and a notification within 72 hours; support periods "no less than five years" unless the product's lifetime is shorter. **Radio Equipment Directive** cybersecurity: Delegated Regulation (EU) 2022/30 applies from 1 Aug 2025 (per Delegated Regulation 2023/2444), with EN 18031-1/-2/-3:2024 cited in the OJ "with restrictions" on 30 Jan 2025. [Established]
- **SRAM PUFs:** introduced by Guajardo, Kumar, Schrijen and Tuyls, CHES 2007 (LNCS, pp. 63–80); Schrijen and van der Leest, DATE 2012, measured fractional Hamming distances of about 3–5% between readouts at the enrollment temperature and up to 20.5% at temperature extremes, so error correction must be sized for the worst case. Vendor slides claim a −55 to 150 °C range and ">25 years lifetime" (vendor claims). [Established; vendor claims marked]
- **Classic attack papers:** Kocher, "Timing Attacks on Implementations of Diffie-Hellman, RSA, DSS, and Other Systems", CRYPTO '96; Kocher, Jaffe and Jun, "Differential Power Analysis", CRYPTO '99, LNCS pp. 388–397; Boneh, DeMillo and Lipton, "On the Importance of Checking Cryptographic Protocols for Faults", EUROCRYPT '97, LNCS pp. 37–51. (Crossref records and author pages.) [Established]
- **MCU attack cases:** Obermaier and Tatschner, "Shedding too much Light on a Microcontroller's Firmware Protection", USENIX WOOT 2017 (STM32F0 read-out protection); Kraken Security Labs, 31 Jan 2020: voltage glitching of the STM32F205 in Trezor wallets with "just 15 minutes of physical access"; Espressif advisory and NVD CVE-2019-17391 (ESP32 eFuse read protection bypassed by a glitch after reset; CVSS 3.1 4.6, physical vector); NVD CVE-2020-27211 (nRF52840 APPROTECT bypassed by a fault during boot); Schink, Wagner, Unterstein and Heyszl, "Security and Trust in Open Source Security Tokens", TCHES 2021. [Established]
- **Roots of trust:** NIST defines them as "Highly reliable hardware, firmware, and software components that perform specific, critical security functions"; NIST SP 800-193 (May 2018) frames platform firmware resiliency as protect, detect and recover. [Established]

## R-313 · Security regulation, Cortex-M52 and Bluetooth pairing (Course 18 audit)

*Read on 21 Nov 2026 by a research subagent from EUR-Lex, Arm, ISO and Bluetooth SIG documents (unece.org and the main ISO site blocked automated access; EU and ISO committee pages were used).*

- **CRA support period (Regulation (EU) 2024/2847, Art. 13(8), EUR-Lex):** "the support period shall be at least five years. Where the product with digital elements is expected to be in use for less than five years, the support period shall correspond to the expected use time." [Established]
- **CRA product classes:** Annex III Class I includes "Microprocessors with security-related functionalities" and "Microcontrollers with security-related functionalities" (items 13–14); Class II lists "Tamper-resistant microprocessors" and "Tamper-resistant microcontrollers" (items 3–4); Annex IV (critical) lists "Hardware Devices with Security Boxes", smart meter gateways, and "Smartcards or similar devices, including secure elements". [Established]
- **CRA conformity routes (Art. 32):** Class I may use internal control (module A) only where harmonised standards, common specifications or certification schemes (at least "substantial") are applied in full, otherwise module B+C or H; Class II needs B+C, H, or where available a European cybersecurity certification scheme at "substantial"; critical products need a European cybersecurity certificate where a delegated act under Art. 8(1) requires one, otherwise the Class II routes. [Established]
- **CRA reporting (Art. 14):** actively exploited vulnerabilities and severe incidents are notified "simultaneously to the CSIRT designated as coordinator … and to ENISA … via the single reporting platform"; early warning "without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours" of becoming aware, a notification within 72 hours, and a final report no later than 14 days after a corrective measure is available (one month after the notification for incidents). [Established]
- **CRA dates (Arts. 69 and 71):** the Regulation applies from 11 December 2027, Article 14 from 11 September 2026 and Chapter IV from 11 June 2026; under Art. 69(3) the Article 14 obligations also apply to products placed on the market before 11 December 2027. [Established]
- **Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2025/2392 of 28 November 2025 (OJ L, 1 Dec 2025):** the technical descriptions of the important and critical product categories; tamper-resistant MPUs and MCUs are designed for "protection of AVA_VAN level 2 or 3", secure elements "at least AVA_VAN.4", including TPMs and embedded UICCs. [Established]
- **Arm Cortex-M52:** announced 22 Nov 2023 (Arm Newsroom), extending "the Armv8.1-M Cortex-M line-up"; the product page lists "Optional support for Arm TrustZone technology", optional Helium and PACBTI. [Established]
- **Automotive cybersecurity:** UN Regulation No 155 (OJ L 82, 9 Mar 2021; in force 22 Jan 2021) applies to vehicle categories M and N (and O with an ECU); Regulation (EU) 2019/2144, Annex II (consolidated 7 Jul 2024), code B: refusal of new EU type-approvals from 6 July 2022 and of registration of non-compliant vehicles from 7 July 2024. ISO/SAE 21434:2021 "Road vehicles — Cybersecurity engineering", edition 1, published 2021-08 (SAE: 31 Aug 2021). [Established]
- **Bluetooth static passkeys:** Core Specification v5.4/v6.0, Vol 3 Part H §2.3.5.6.3: "The Passkey should be generated randomly during each pairing procedure and not be reused from a previous procedure. Static Passkeys should not be used since they can compromise the security of the link"; LE Secure Connections commits to the passkey's 20 bits one per round, and "A MITM attacker with only partial knowledge of the Passkey will only receive one incorrectly-guessed bit of the Passkey before the protocol fails." NIST SP 800-121 Rev. 2 (upd. 19 Jan 2022): "Static SSP passkeys facilitate MITM attacks." (That a static passkey falls to about 20 repeated pairings is an inference from the one-bit property, not a quoted figure.) [Established]

## R-314 · CMOS inverter practice: SKY130 devices, IRDS supply roadmap, near-threshold chips, FO4 delay, leading-node claims (Course 19)

*Read on 21 Nov 2026 by a research subagent with curl and pdftotext (tsmc.com, intel.com, businesswire.com, sec.gov and IEEE Xplore blocked automated access; TSMC's IEDM 2024 claim was read through the IEEE Electron Devices Society newsletter).*

- **SkyWater SKY130 open PDK** ([google/skywater-pdk README](https://github.com/google/skywater-pdk) and [the PDK documentation](https://skywater-pdk.readthedocs.io/en/main/)): "a mature 180nm-130nm hybrid technology"; "Support for internal 1.8V with 5.0V I/Os". 1.8 V devices `sky130_fd_pr__nfet_01v8` (and `_lvt`) and `sky130_fd_pr__pfet_01v8` (and `_lvt`, `_hvt`); models valid for $V_{DS}$ and $V_{GS}$ from 0 to 1.95 V. Device-details tables, typical-corner model (e-test nominal): NMOS threshold 0.538 V (0.541 V) at W/L = 7/8 and 0.645 V (0.700 V) at 7/0.15; PMOS −1.050 V (−1.050 V) at 7/8 and −0.781 V (−0.781 V) at 7/0.15; saturation currents at 7/0.15 of 3.512 mA (NMOS) and 1.347 mA (PMOS), bias not stated on the page. Rule poly.1a: minimum poly width 0.150 µm; minimum channel width 0.420 µm. Libraries sky130_fd_sc_hd, hdll, hs, ms, ls, lp and hvl; hd leakage "0.86 nA / kGate" at 1.80 V, 25 °C. "Inverter Gate Delays … FO = 1, 143 [stages]": 31.8 ps e-test nominal (24.7–44.1 ps); supply, temperature and method not stated. The docs say the open PDK "is not intended to be used for production settings at this current time". [Established]
- **IEEE IRDS 2024 More Moore** ([tables](https://irds.ieee.org/images/files/pdf/2024/2024IRDS_MM_Tables.xlsx) and [report](https://irds.ieee.org/images/files/pdf/2024/2024IRDS_MM.pdf); the latest More Moore edition as of Nov 2026): Table MM01 logic $V_{dd}$ 0.70 V in 2024 ("3nm"), 0.65 V in 2025 ("2nm") and 0.60 V from 2027 through 2039; high-performance subthreshold slope 82, 72 and 70 mV/decade over the same years. Long-term challenge: "Power scaling — no solutions left to reduce the supply voltage because of lack of steep-subthreshold devices"; near-term: GAA devices reduce the supply "but not in a sustained manner". The 2024 Executive Summary notes $V_{DD}$ was raised to 0.75 V in 2021 because of "higher than anticipated leakage of FinFET transistors". [Industry estimate] (roadmap) Same table, row "CV/I (ps) - FO3 load, HP [9]": 1.0611 ps (2024), 0.9575 (2025), 0.8733 (2027), 0.8317 (2029), 0.8625 (2031), 0.6905 (2033), 0.6675 (2035), 0.6437 (2037), 0.6241 (2039); note [9]: "Intrinsic transistor delay (τ) for NMOS devices at 25°C at FO3 loading. τ = (Cg,total´Vdd)/Id,sat" (re-read from the table file, 24 Nov 2026).
- **Swanson and Meindl**, "Ion-Implanted Complementary MOS Transistors in Low-Voltage Circuits", *IEEE JSSC* SC-7(2), pp. 146–153, Apr. 1972: "The smallest supply voltage at which these circuits will function is approximately 8kT/q"; in the body, the supply "must be at least 3–4 nkT/mq for the inverter to have sufficient gain for use in a digital circuit", with n/m near 2, "giving a minimum usable supply of about 8kT/q or 0.2 V at 27° C". [Established]
- **Intel "Claremont"** (S. Jain et al., ISSCC 2012, paper 3.6; [Intel Labs ISSCC 2012 press overview](https://download.intel.com/newsroom/kits/isscc/2012/pdfs/ISSCC-IL-Press-Overview.pdf)): IA-32 processor in 32 nm that "Operates from 280mV @3MHz to 1.2V @915MHz", "4.7x better energy efficiency in NTV mode", "2mW minimum power". The Hot Chips 2012 slides give different bench numbers and are not used here. [Established]
- **Ambiq SPOT**: 19 Jan 2015 release, SPOT "operates transistors at sub-threshold voltages (less than 0.5V)"; [5 Mar 2026 release](https://ambiq.com/news/ambiq-reveals-12nm-spot-platform-enabling-operation-down-to-300mv-for-atomiq/), a 12 nm SPOT platform on TSMC N12e with a ULP mode "engineered to operate as low as 300mV" for the upcoming Atomiq part. [Established] for 2015; [Industry estimate] (company claim, part not yet shipping) for 2026
- **FO4 delay rule of thumb**: Ho, Mai and Horowitz, "The Future of Wires", *Proc. IEEE* 89(4), pp. 490–504, Apr. 2001: FO4 delay about 360 ps × $L_{drawn}$ (µm) at typical and 500 ps × $L_{drawn}$ under worst-case conditions; "In a 0.18-µm technology, an FO4 is about 90 pS under worst case environmental conditions"; "a FO1 is one-third a FO4". Horowitz, DAC 2000 slides: "Approximately 0.36 nS/µm*Ldrawn at TT". Harris, Ho, Wei and Horowitz, "The Fanout-of-4 Inverter Delay Metric" (Stanford manuscript) proposes FO4 "to normalize process and operating condition variations". [Established]
- **Simulated FO4 by node**: Stillmaker and Baas, *Integration, the VLSI Journal* 58, pp. 74–81, 2017, Table 2 (predictive device models, not silicon): 77.2 ps at 180 nm and 1.8 V; 34.7 ps at 130 nm and 1.2 V; 10.9 ps at 45 nm and 1.1 V; 4.02 ps at 14 nm and 0.86 V; 2.47 ps at 7 nm and 0.8 V. No foundry-published measured FO4 for a 7, 5 or 3 nm node was found. [Industry estimate]
- **Leading-node claims**: Intel, 9 Oct 2025 Panther Lake release ([intc.com](https://www.intc.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/1752/intel-unveils-panther-lake-architecture-first-ai-pc)): Intel 18A delivers "up to 15% better performance per watt and 30% improved chip density compared to Intel 3" (footnote: Intel internal analysis as of February 2024). TSMC N2 (G. Yeap et al., IEDM 2024 paper 2.1, summarized in the [IEEE EDS newsletter, Apr 2025](https://www.ieee.org/ns/periodicals/EDS/EDS-APRIL-2025-HTML/InnerFiles/LandPage.html)): "a 15% speed gain (or 30% power reduction) at >1.15x chip density versus … N3", mass production scheduled for the second half of 2025. No nominal supply for N2 or 18A was found on a company page. [Industry estimate] (vendor claims)
- **SKY130 periphery rules, channel width in cells** ([periphery rules page](https://skywater-pdk.readthedocs.io/en/main/rules/periphery.html), read 24 Nov 2026): rule difftap.2b, "Minimum channel width (Diff And Poly) for FETs inside areaid.sc", 0.360 µm (against the general 0.420 µm). [Established]

## R-315 · Logic families in practice, SKY130 cells, logical effort, interconnect scaling and repeaters (Course 19)

*Read on 21 Nov 2026 by a research subagent with curl and pdftotext (tsmc.com, anandtech.com, dblp and the Semantic Scholar API blocked automated access; the TSMC A16 figures are from press reports only).*

- **Dynamic logic in processors:** Silberman et al., "A 1.0-GHz Single-Issue 64-Bit PowerPC Integer Processor", *IEEE JSSC*, Nov 1998 ([IBM Research page](https://research.ibm.com/publications/10-ghz-single-issue-64-bit-powerpc-integer-processor)): "a delayed reset dynamic circuit style enabling complex functions to be implemented in a few levels of logic". Nowka and Galambos, ICCD 1998: "highly-optimized and noise-characterized delayed-reset domino circuits were employed in the datapath elements". Sager, Hinton et al., ISSCC 2001 paper 20.6 (Pentium 4): "The fundamental logic family used in the ALUs is internally called 'Globally-Reset Self-timed Pre-charged Domino'"; the 2 GHz clock "is doubled locally to generate a 4GHz clock"; "The first six logic stages of the ALUs (3 domino and 3 static)". [Established]
- **Retreat from dynamic logic:** Stolt et al., "Design and Implementation of the POWER6 Microprocessor", *IEEE JSSC* 43(1), Jan 2008: "13 FO4 design … without the use of power-hungry dynamic circuits outside of register files and arrays"; register files "with more than 32 entries favored a dynamic circuit implementation". D. Harris, *CMOS VLSI Design* 4th ed. lecture 10 slides (2010): domino is "1.3 – 2x faster than static CMOS", "Widely used in high-performance microprocessors in 1990s when speed was king. Largely displaced by static CMOS now that power is the limiter. Still used in memories for area efficiency." No primary statement after 2015 was found. Intel patent US 7,362,621 (granted 2008): "an upsized keeper may prevent the timely discharge of current to ground when a data line has been selected." [Established]
- **SkyWater SKY130 `sky130_fd_sc_hd`** ([library repository](https://github.com/google/skywater-pdk-libs-sky130_fd_sc_hd), release 0.0.2, Nov 2020): 163 cell functions in 437 variants (count from the repository; the documentation's "approximate" table differs). `inv_1`: NMOS `nfet_01v8` W/L = 0.65/0.15 µm and PMOS `pfet_01v8_hvt` 1.0/0.15 µm (the documentation lists `pfet_01v8`). `nand2_1` and `nor2_1` use the same 0.65 and 1.0 µm devices as the inverter; higher drive strengths use parallel multiples (`m=2`, `m=4`). Drive strengths: inv _1, _2, _4, _6, _8, _12, _16; nand2 and nor2 _1, _2, _4, _8. From the netlists: `dfxtp_1` (flip-flop) and `mux4_1` (second level) use transmission gates; `mux2_1` and `dlxtp_1` do not. [Established]
- **Logical effort:** I. Sutherland, R. Sproull and D. Harris, *Logical Effort: Designing Fast CMOS Circuits*, Morgan Kaufmann, 1999 (ISBN 978-1-55860-557-2). The original paper, Sutherland and Sproull, "Logical Effort: Designing for Speed on the Back of an Envelope", appeared in *Advanced Research in VLSI*, 1991 (pages and publisher not confirmed). Harris, "Lecture 6: Logical Effort" slides (2020): NAND g = (n+2)/3, NOR g = (2n+1)/3, parasitic delay "In multiples of pinv (≈1)"; "For pinv = 1, solve numerically for ρ = 3.59"; "2.4 < ρ < 6 gives delay within 15% of optimal … I like ρ = 4"; "Delay of path is about log4F FO4 inverter delays". [Established]
- **Interconnect, IRDS 2024 More Moore** (Table MM01): node labels G48M24 (gate pitch 48 nm, M0 pitch 24 nm) in 2024 and G42M14 from 2035 (re-read from the table file, 24 Nov 2026); M0 pitch 24 nm (2024) to 14 nm (2035 on); Mx pitch 32 nm to 14 nm; tight-pitch Mx wire 300 Ω/µm (2024) and 475 Ω/µm (2025 on) with 270 aF/µm; 80 nm-pitch reference wire 13.3 Ω/µm and 198 aF/µm. Table MM-5: "Interconnect resistance has now entered an exponential increase regime because of non-ideal scaling of the barrier for Cu and increased scattering at the surface and grain-boundary interfaces." §4.5.1: Cu "expected to remain … the preferred solution … at least until 2029 while non-Cu solutions (e.g. Co and Ru) are expected to be used for the local interconnect (M0)." [Industry estimate] (roadmap)
- **Copper size effect:** D. Gall, *J. Appl. Phys.* 127, 050901 (2020): scattering "causes the resistivity of, for example, 10-nm-wide Cu interconnect lines to be approximately an order of magnitude larger than that of bulk Cu"; Cu ρ₀λ = 6.7 × 10⁻¹⁶ Ω·m², mean free path 39.9 nm (first principles). imec, 3 June 2025: "Ru lines at 16nm pitch with average resistance as low as 656 Ω /µm". [Established] (Gall); [Industry estimate] (imec)
- **Backside power:** Intel, 5 June 2023 (VLSI Symposium 2023 paper T1-1): PowerVia test chip with "standard cell utilization exceeding 90%" and "more than 5% frequency benefit in silicon due to reduced IR drop"; the press release adds "more than 30% platform voltage droop improvement". Intel 18A (9 Oct 2025 release) includes PowerVia. TSMC A16 "Super Power Rail" against N2P, from press reports (Tom's Hardware, 25 Apr 2024 and 22 Apr 2026): 8–10% speed, 15–20% lower power, 1.07–1.10× chip density, volume production now 2027. [Established] (Intel test chip); [Industry estimate] (TSMC, via press)
- **Wires and repeaters:** Ho, Mai and Horowitz, "The Future of Wires", *Proc. IEEE* 89(4), 2001: local wires "have delays that either track gate delays or grow slowly relative to gate delays"; global wire delay "will remain constant if repeaters are used, meaning that relative to gate delays, their delays scale upwards"; delay-optimal repeaters have "a driver fanout of 2.7", and a delay–power optimum makes segments "longer by 1.7×" with latency up "about 13%" and capacitance down "30%". Bakoglu and Meindl, "Optimal Interconnection Circuits for VLSI", *IEEE Trans. Electron Devices* ED-32(5), pp. 903–909, May 1985 (0–90% delays): k = √(R_int C_int/(2.3 R₀C₀)), h = √(R₀C_int/(R_int C₀)), T = 7.6√(R₀R_int C₀C_int). Harris, lecture 14 slides: about "40 ps/mm in 65 nm process" for an optimally repeated wire, and an energy premium of about 87% over an unrepeated wire. [Established]

## R-316 · Dennard scaling, leakage and DVFS, sequencing overhead and clock distribution, SRAM cells (Course 19)

*Read on 21 Nov 2026 by a research subagent with curl and pdftotext (st.com, semiwiki.com, fuse.wikichip.org and the ISSCC 2025 program were blocked or unreachable; TSMC N3/N3E/N2 SRAM figures are from trade press).*

- **Dennard scaling:** Dennard et al., "Design of ion-implanted MOSFET's with very small physical dimensions", *IEEE JSSC* 9(5), pp. 256–268, Oct 1974: under constant-field scaling "the power density remains constant. Thus, even if many more circuits are placed on a given integrated circuit chip, the cooling problem is essentially unchanged"; and already "the subthreshold behavior does not scale as desired". Horowitz et al., "Scaling, Power, and the Future of CMOS", IEDM 2005: "since kT/q does not scale and leakage currents are set by the transistor's threshold voltage, there is a limit to how low one can make a transistor's Vth … from the 130mn [sic] technology forward, Vdd has been scaling slowly, if at all"; the energy optimum "sets the leakage energy to be about 30% of the active power". Horowitz, ISSCC 2014 plenary: clock-frequency scaling "stopped in the early 2000s" because processors hit the air-cooling "power wall … around 100W" and threshold voltage could no longer scale "due to rising leakage currents". [Established]
- **DVFS operating points** (company-authored Linux device trees, mainline kernel, read Nov 2026): Rockchip RK3588 Cortex-A76 cluster 1200 MHz at 0.675 V to 2400 MHz at 1.000 V; Cortex-A55 cluster 1008 MHz at 0.675 V to 1800 MHz at 0.950 V; NXP i.MX 8M Mini Cortex-A53 1.2, 1.6 and 1.8 GHz at 0.85, 0.95 and 1.00 V. Upstream defaults, not datasheet guarantees. [Established]
- **Sequencing overhead:** Hrishikesh et al., "The Optimal Logic Depth Per Pipeline Stage is 6 to 8 FO4 Inverter Delays", ISCA 2002: latch overhead "36ps (1 FO4) at 100nm", skew 0.3 FO4 and jitter 0.5 FO4 (from Kurd et al. at 180 nm), total 1.8 FO4. Harris, *CMOS VLSI Design* slides: a buffered latch is "Rather slow (1.5 – 2 FO4 delays)"; "The typical FO4 inverter delay for a process of feature size f (in nm) is about 0.5f ps". [Established]
- **Clock distribution:** I. Young (Intel), Stanford EE380 lecture, 2005: Itanium (0.18 µm, 800 MHz) "Projected max skew without deskew mechanism = 110ps", "Max skew with deskew mechanism = 28ps"; Pentium 4 "Pre-compensation max skew ~ ±32ps", "Post-compensation max skew ±8ps", and "Deliberately skew clocks for performance – 200MHz frequency increase obtained". AMD Piledriver resonant clock mesh (ISSCC 2012): "reduce clock distribution power up to 24%"; JSSC 2013: "a peak 25% global clock power reduction at 75°C, which translates to a 4.5% reduction in average application core power". IBM POWER6 (*JSSC*, 2008): a grid fed by local H-trees; "On-chip clock tuning resulted in up to a 10% frequency improvement". [Established]
- **SKY130 flip-flop** (`sky130_fd_sc_hd__dfxtp_1`, typical corner tt_025C_1v80 Liberty, units ns and pF): clock-to-Q 0.2692 ns rise and 0.2652 ns fall at 0.01 ns clock slew and 0.0005 pF load; setup 0.0508 ns (D rising) and 0.1033 ns (D falling), hold −0.0285 and −0.0468 ns, at 0.01 ns clock and data slews; pre-layout models. [Established]
- **SRAM cells:** TSMC N5 "densest 0.021 um2 SRAM cells" (Yeap et al., IEDM 2019, title); TSMC N3E 0.021 µm² and N2 about 0.0175 µm² (38 Mb/mm²) per Tom's Hardware (31 Oct 2024, reporting TSMC's IEDM 2024 paper; its prose and table disagree on N3); Intel 18A 0.021 µm² (ISSCC 2025 paper title only). IRDS 2024: "Bitcell scaling is slowing down because of the slow-down of the device pitch and gate pitch." TSMC VLSI 2023: a 3 nm, 27.6 Mb/mm² SRAM with "0.48 - 1.2 V Wide Operating Range". SKY130 single-port bitcell `sram_sp_cell` is 1.200 × 1.580 µm; the PDK restricts its use to the provided IP. IBM POWER6 gave "voltage sensitive memory cells … a higher voltage supply (nominal at 1.25 V)" against 1.1 V logic. [Established] (N5, IRDS, SKY130, POWER6); [Industry estimate] (N3E, N2, 18A via press or titles)

## R-317 · SkyWater SKY130 standard-cell density and flip-flop area (Course 19)

*Read on 21 Nov 2026 by research subagents from the SkyWater PDK documentation and the `sky130_fd_sc_hd` library repository (curl; no blocking).*

- **Routed gate density:** "SkyWater Foundry Provided Standard Cell Libraries" page (<https://skywater-pdk.readthedocs.io/en/main/contents/libraries/foundry-provided.html>): the `sky130_fd_sc_hd` library uses "a 0.46 x 2.72um site, equivalent to 9 met1 tracks"; its section states "Routed Gate Density is 160 kGates/mm^2 or better" and leakage "@ttleak_1.80v_25C (no body bias) is 0.86 nA / kGate". The low-leakage `sky130_fd_sc_hdll` section states "Routed Gate Density is 120 kGates/mm^2", leakage "0.08 nA / kGate", and that "Compared to sky130_fd_sc_hd, this library enables 5-10X lower leakage power". **Correction (24 Nov 2026):** this entry first attributed the 120 kGates/mm² figure to `hd`; re-reading the page section by section shows it belongs to `hdll`. 19-09's Example 4 and Problem 6 were re-keyed with 160. The same page: the `hs`, `ms`, `ls` and `lp` libraries "are compatible in size, with a 0.48 x 3.33um site, equivalent to about 11 met1 tracks"; `hvl` uses "a 0.48 x 4.07um site, or 14 met1 tracks" (re-read 24 Nov 2026). [Established]
- **Flip-flop area:** `sky130_fd_sc_hd__dfxtp_1__tt_025C_1v80.lib.json` in the library repository: cell area 20.0192 µm², which is 16 sites of 0.46 × 2.72 µm (my arithmetic). [Established]

## R-318 · Synthesis and STA tools, Liberty and SDC, variation models, SKY130 corners and timing margins, AI-driven implementation claims (Course 19)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent and the main agent (curl and shallow git clones; api.github.com refused these repositories, so GitHub facts are pinned to commits; cadence.com and businesswire.com refused automated access and were not used directly).*

- **Yosys:** repository <https://github.com/YosysHQ/yosys>, highest tag `v0.71` ("Release version 0.71", commit dated 12 Nov 2026). README: "a framework for RTL synthesis tools"; "free software licensed under the ISC license". `abc` pass help: "This pass uses the ABC tool [1] for technology mapping of yosys's internal gate library to a target architecture"; `-liberty <file>`: "generate netlists for the specified cell library"; `-D <picoseconds>`: "set delay target". `dfflibmap` help: "Map internal flip-flop and latch cells to the flip-flop and latch cells in the technology library specified in the given liberty files." [Established]
- **ABC:** Berkeley starting page <https://people.eecs.berkeley.edu/~alanmi/abc/starting.htm>: "ABC combines scalable logic optimization based on And-Inverter Graphs (AIGs), optimal-delay DAG-based technology mapping for look-up tables and standard cells, and innovative algorithms for sequential synthesis and verification." [Established]
- **OpenSTA:** <https://github.com/The-OpenROAD-Project/OpenSTA> (commit `39f9dec`, 23 Nov 2026; upstream <https://github.com/parallaxsw/OpenSTA>). README: "a gate level static timing verifier"; reads "Verilog netlist", "Liberty library", "SDC timing constraints", "SDF delay annotation", "SPEF parasitics"; "dual licensed ... under GPL v3 ... and ... for commerical applications by Parallax Software". doc/Commands.md: `set_timing_derate`; on-chip variation "is the default analysis type". sdc/Variables.tcl: CRPR "The default value is 1". ChangeLog: "Release 3.2.0 2026/10/15"; 3.0.1 (2026/03/12): "Statistical timing (SSTA) with Liberty LVF (Liberty Variation Format) models is now supported"; 3.0.0 (2025/11/26) "adds multi-corner multi-mode (mcmm) support". Its example script uses "+/-10% min/max derating" (an example, not a norm). OpenSTA's documentation implements "SDC 2.1". No mention of AOCV was found. [Established]
- **SDC and Liberty governance:** Synopsys TAP-in page (<https://www.synopsys.com/community/interoperability-programs/tap-in.html>): SDC is "Used to describe the "design intent" and surrounding constraints for synthesis, clocking, timing, power, test and environmental and operating conditions", released under open-source licensing. Synopsys release, 30 Sep 2014 (<https://news.synopsys.com/2014-09-30-Synopsys-Announces-New-Additions-to-Liberty-to-Significantly-Speed-up-Timing-Closure>): "In May 2006, an industry-wide Liberty Technical Advisory Board was formed ... under the auspices of the IEEE Industry Standards and Technology Organization (IEEE-ISTO)"; AOCV, POCV and SOCV formats were consolidated into "the Liberty Variation Format (LVF) extensions", ratified "on August 1, 2014". Release of 27 Feb 2017: "three statistical moment-based extensions to the LVF standard consisting of mean shift, standard deviation and skewness". Si2 (<https://si2.org/?p=383>) lists "ECSM Timing and Power Specification v2.1.2 (contributed by Cadence Design Systems ...)". The latest Liberty Reference Manual version could not be confirmed (behind registration). [Established]
- **Variation models and corner counts:** A. B. Kahng, "New Game, New Goal Posts: A Recent History of Timing Closure," DAC 2015 (<https://vlsicad.ucsd.edu/Publications/Conferences/330/c330.pdf>): "Advanced on-chip variation (AOCV) delay derating tables have been mainstream since the 40nm foundry node"; POCV "improves on AOCV in that stage counts are no longer needed; rather, σ² terms are accumulated over a given path"; LVF provides "one number per load-slew combination per cell"; MCMM clock synthesis "in a regime where each of hundreds of scenarios has different clock insertion delay and timing constraints". A. Tetelbaum (Abelite Design Automation), "Corner-based Timing Signoff and What Is Next," 2014 (<https://anysilicon.com/wp-content/uploads/2014/10/Corner_based_Signoff_paper_Jan_2014a.pdf>): "design companies currently use 12-126 corners", with a prediction of 16–248 by 2020; the author's company sold signoff tools. No dated figure for 5/3 nm was found. [Industry estimate]
- **SKY130 corners and the open flow:** the `sky130_fd_sc_hd` repository's `timing/` directory holds 16 corner files: ff `100C_1v65`, `100C_1v95`, `n40C_1v56`, `n40C_1v65`, `n40C_1v76`, `n40C_1v95_ccsnoise`; ss `100C_1v40`, `100C_1v60`, `n40C_1v28`, `n40C_1v35`, `n40C_1v40`, `n40C_1v44`, `n40C_1v60_ccsnoise`, `n40C_1v76`; tt `025C_1v80`, `100C_1v80`. OpenROAD-flow-scripts (<https://github.com/The-OpenROAD-Project/OpenROAD-flow-scripts>, commit `b02bf4ae`, 23 Nov 2026) `flow/platforms/sky130hd/config.mk` loads only `sky130_fd_sc_hd__tt_025C_1v80.lib`, which declares `delay_model : "table_lookup"` (NLDM; no LVF or CCS groups found). The same assembled TT Liberty (<https://raw.githubusercontent.com/The-OpenROAD-Project/OpenROAD-flow-scripts/master/flow/platforms/sky130hd/lib/sky130_fd_sc_hd__tt_025C_1v80.lib>, read 24 Nov 2026) sets `input_threshold_pct_rise : 50`, `output_threshold_pct_rise : 50`, `slew_lower_threshold_pct_rise : 20`, `slew_upper_threshold_pct_rise : 80` (the same for fall) and `slew_derate_from_library : 1.0`. Reference-design clock periods (`flow/designs/sky130hd/*/constraint.sdc`): gcd 1.1 ns, aes 3.6 ns, jpeg 5 ns, riscv32i 6.0 ns, ibex 8.0 ns, each with I/O delays of 20% of the period; none of the five sets clock uncertainty or a timing derate. `sky130hd/microwatt` (15.0 ns) sets `set_clock_uncertainty 0.25` and derates of 0.95/1.05. Flow setup and hold repair margins default to 0. These are open-flow test settings, not foundry signoff recommendations. [Established]
- **SKY130 cell timing tables:** per-cell Liberty JSON files in <https://github.com/google/skywater-pdk-libs-sky130_fd_sc_hd/tree/main/cells> (read 24 Nov 2026 by the main agent): areas `inv_1`, `nand2_1`, `nor2_1`, `buf_1` 3.7536 µm²; `nand3_1`, `a21oi_1`, `o21ai_1` 5.0048; `nand4_1`, `and2_1` 6.256; `a22oi_1` 7.5072. NLDM tables (7 × 7, input transition 0.01–1.5 ns by load) at `tt_025C_1v80` for these cells and `inv_1` at `ss_100C_1v60` are reproduced unchanged in `courses/19-digital-vlsi-design/labs/19-10-lab/sky130_hd_subset.json` (Apache License 2.0). [Established]
- **Commercial tools:** Synopsys PrimeTime page: "As the industry's trusted golden signoff solution, PrimeTime provides comprehensive timing, signal integrity, power, and variation-aware analysis"; datasheet (06/10/26): "AOCV uses context-specific derate factors that consider location and logic depth of each path"; "POCV ... provides a lightweight statistical margining approach". Cadence Tempus launch, 20 May 2013 (EE Journal reprint <https://www.eejournal.com/industry_news/20130520-01>): a Cadence executive said "the time spent in timing closure and signoff is approaching 40 percent of the overall design implementation flow". Siemens markets Aprisa as "an RTL-to-GDS solution that offers complete synthesis and place-and-route functionality". [Vendor claim]
- **AI-driven implementation:** Synopsys release, 7 Feb 2023 (<https://news.synopsys.com/2023-02-07-AI-designed-Chips-Reach-Scale-with-First-100-Commercial-Tape-outs-Using-Synopsys-Technology>): "more than 3x productivity increases, up to 25% lower total power and significant reduction in die size"; DSO.ai "uses reinforcement learning to enhance PPA in very large solution spaces". Synopsys release, 20 Mar 2024: "a more than 10% boost in performance, power, area (PPA), up to 10X faster turn-around time ... when compared to optimization without the use of AI". Cadence Cerebrus launch, 22 Jul 2021 (design-reuse reprint <https://design-reuse.com/news/50354/cadence-ml-based-cerebrus.html>): "improve engineering productivity by up to 10X versus a manual approach while also realizing up to a 20% better power, performance and area (PPA)"; Samsung Foundry: "more than an 8% power reduction on some of our most critical blocks". Cadence release, 9 Jun 2022 (reprint <https://www.design-reuse.com/news/52118/cadence-cerebrus-intelligent-chip-explorer.html>): MediaTek "shrunk the die area by 5% and reduced power by more than 6%"; Renesas "a 75% improvement in total negative slack (TNS)". No independent benchmark was found. [Vendor claim]
- **OCV in plain terms:** EE Journal, "Less Pessimism, Please," 1 Aug 2011 (<https://www.eejournal.com/article/20110801-extremeda/>): "The single OCV derating factor reflects some "average" path length but is over-optimistic for short paths and over-pessimistic for longer paths." [Established]

## R-319 · SKY130 metal stack and limits, OpenROAD and open flows, open shuttles, routing and clock-tree citations (Course 19)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent (curl and shallow git clones pinned to commits; cadence.com, businesswire.com, IEEE Xplore, intel.com and DBLP refused or blocked automated access and were not used directly).*

- **SKY130 metal stack:** SkyWater PDK documentation (<https://skywater-pdk.readthedocs.io/en/main/rules/background.html>, google/skywater-pdk commit `7198cf6`): "5 levels of metal"; local interconnect li1 is separate. RCX table (<https://skywater-pdk.readthedocs.io/en/main/rules/rcx.html>), "Resistivity (mohms/sq)": "Local interconnect 12800", "Metal1 125", "Metal2 125", "Metal3 47", "Metal4 47", "Metal5 29"; vias "VIA 4500", "VIA2 3410", "VIA3 3410", "VIA4 380" (units for contacts not stated; MCON's 152000 conflicts with the open flow's about 9.25 Ω and is unresolved). [Established]
- **SKY130 tech LEF** (`sky130_fd_sc_hd.tlef` in OpenROAD-flow-scripts commit `b02bf4a`, "Copyright 2020 The SkyWater PDK Authors", Apache 2.0): met1 "PITCH 0.34", "WIDTH 0.14", "RESISTANCE RPERSQ 0.125", "DCCURRENTDENSITY AVERAGE 2.8 ; # mA/um Iavg_max at Tj = 90oC"; met2 pitch 0.46, width 0.14; met3 pitch 0.68, width 0.3, RPERSQ 0.047, DC EM 6.8 mA/µm; met4 pitch 0.92, width 0.3, 0.047, 6.8; met5 pitch 3.4, width 1.6, RPERSQ 0.0285, 10.17 mA/µm. Thicknesses 0.35/0.8/1.2 µm in the LEF and antenna table differ from the stack diagram's 0.36/0.845/1.26 µm. EM limits appear only as LEF comments, not in the readthedocs pages. DRC manual: minimum widths and spacings 0.14 µm (met1, met2), 0.30 (met3, met4), 1.6 (met5). Antenna table: "Met1 (met1 perimeter area/gate area) ... 400" without a diode. Density: "(m1.13),Max pattern density (PD) of met1,CU,0.77" in a 50 µm window (periphery rules); the LEF states MAXIMUMDENSITY 70 with a 700 µm window. [Established]
- **Open-flow wire estimates:** ORFS `flow/platforms/sky130hd/setRC.tcl` (commit `b02bf4a`): "set_layer_rc -layer met1 -capacitance 1.72375E-04 -resistance 1.20565E-03", "set_layer_rc -layer met3 -capacitance 2.14962E-04 -resistance 1.66281E-04", "set_wire_rc -clock -layer met3"; units follow the Liberty file (kΩ and pF per µm), i.e. 1.20565 Ω/µm and 0.172 fF/µm for met1. [Established]
- **OpenROAD:** README (commit `e5b41ea`): "Autonomous, No-Human-In-Loop (NHIL) flow, 24 hour turnaround from RTL-GDSII"; "BSD 3-Clause License"; "used for full physical implementation in over 600 tapeouts in SKY130 and GF180 through the Google-sponsored, Efabless MPW shuttle and ChipIgnite programs" (project claim). Modules: gpl "based on the open-source RePlAce tool ... Solves electrostatic force equations using Nesterov's method"; dpl "legalizes the result of global placement"; cts "based on TritonCTS 2.0" with sink clustering before "building H-tree"; grt "based on FastRoute" with an optional CUGR engine marked "not ready for production" (ORFS sky130hd nonetheless sets GLOBAL_ROUTE_USE_CUGR ?= 1); drt "based on ... TritonRoute"; rcx "based on the open-source OpenRCX". Ajayi et al., DAC 2019 (<https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10171025>): launched "in June 2018 as part of the DARPA IDEA program", with "24-hour turnaround time" as a deliverable. ePlace (Lu et al., ACM TODAES 2015): cells modeled as "positive charge", density coupled to potential by "Poisson's equation". [Established]
- **ORFS SKY130 reference designs** (commit `b02bf4a`): CORE_UTILIZATION gcd 38, aes 35, ibex 50, jpeg 50, riscv32i 45; platform PLACE_DENSITY 0.60; routing met1–met5, clocks from met3. In-repository QoR thresholds were removed on 22 Sep 2026; the last ones (commit `08f2bb7`) bounded setup slack at, e.g., ≥ −1.52 ns for gcd (regression bounds, not results). [Established]
- **OpenLane and LibreLane:** OpenLane 1 README: "OpenLane is currently in maintenance mode"; "not recommended for new projects or designs". OpenLane 2 stopped at 2.3.10; LibreLane (FOSSi Foundation) latest stable tag 3.0.17 (Nov 2026); its Classic flow lists Magic.DRC, KLayout.DRC, KLayout.XOR, Netgen.LVS, OpenROAD.CheckAntennas/RepairAntennas and Yosys.EQY among its steps. [Established]
- **Shuttles:** Google Open Source Blog, 8 Nov 2023: program launched November 2020; "eight shuttle runs on SKY130 and an initial test run on GF180MCU ... With 40 slots per shuttle, we've manufactured 360 designs out of over 600 submissions from 19 countries"; PDKs moving to CHIPS Alliance after GF180 MPW-1. Tiny Tapeout, 1 Mar 2025: "Efabless shuts down until further notice." ChipFoundry (<https://chipfoundry.io/>, read 24 Nov 2026): UmbraLogic "has acquired the assets of Efabless Corporation"; "$14,950 per tapeout"; "Up to 15mm² of die space". wafer.space (GF180MCU): "Submission Deadline ... 16 December 2026", 3.93 × 5.12 mm slot "Early Bird Price $7,000". Caravel README: user area "2.92mm x 3.52mm", "38" I/O pads; datasheet "10 mm2". [Established; prices as of Nov 2026]
- **Algorithm citations (Crossref):** Lee, IRE Trans. Electronic Computers EC-10(3), 1961, DOI 10.1109/tec.1961.5219222; GORDIAN, IEEE TCAD 10(3), 1991, DOI 10.1109/43.67789; Hwang, SIAM J. Appl. Math. 30(1), 1976, DOI 10.1137/0130013 ("l_s/l_m ≥ 2/3"); ePlace, ACM TODAES 20(2), 2015, DOI 10.1145/2699873; RePlAce, IEEE TCAD 38(9), 2019, DOI 10.1109/tcad.2018.2859220; Tsay, IEEE TCAD 12(2), 1993, DOI 10.1109/43.205004 (ICCAD 1991 original cited by Kahng and Tsao's Planar-DME paper as "Exact Zero Skew", pp. 336–339); Chao, Hsu, Ho, Boese and Kahng, IEEE TCAS-II 39(11), 1992, DOI 10.1109/82.204128. [Established]
- **Leading-edge metal stacks:** IRDS 2024 More Moore (<https://irds.ieee.org/images/files/pdf/2024/2024IRDS_MM.pdf>), an aside: "10−15 metal layers for logic IC's". TechInsights' analysis of Intel 4 (Semiconductor Digest, 1 Oct 2022): "Intel 4 18-layer metal stack" (secondary; the VLSI 2022 paper was not read). [Industry estimate]
- **Commercial tools:** Synopsys IC Compiler II page: "flat and hierarchical design planning ... congestion aware placement and optimization, clock tree synthesis, advanced node routing convergence ... and signoff closure". Cadence Innovus launch, 10 Mar 2015 (Design & Reuse reprint): "typically 10 to 20 percent better PPA and up to 10X full-flow speedup". [Vendor claim]

## R-320 · Test standards, defect-level model, DFT papers, compression claims, test cost, silent data corruption, automotive metrics, open DFT tools, March tests and scan overhead (Course 19)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent (curl, pdftotext and partial git clones; iso.org returned 403 and aecouncil.com was outside the session's allowed hosts, so ISO 26262 and AEC documents were not read directly).*

- **IEEE standards** (IEEE SA pages): "IEEE 1149.1-2013 / IEEE Standard for Test Access Port and Boundary-Scan Architecture", "Inactive-Reserved Standard", inactivated 2024-03-21; revision "P1149.1" PAR approved 2022-06-16 (<https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/1149.1/4484/>). "IEEE 1500-2022 / IEEE Standard Testability Method for Embedded Core-based Integrated Circuits", active, published 2022-10-12. "IEEE 1687-2014 / IEEE Standard for Access and Control of Instrumentation Embedded within a Semiconductor Device", inactive-reserved since 2025-03-27, revision P1687 (PAR 2022-03-24). "IEEE 1149.10-2017 / IEEE Standard for High-Speed Test Access Port and On-Chip Distribution Architecture", active. Inactive-reserved means "removed from active status through an administrative process for standards that have not undergone a revision process within 10 years". [Established]
- **Defect level:** T. W. Williams and N. C. Brown, "Defect Level as a Function of Fault Coverage," *IEEE Trans. Computers* C-30(12):987–988, Dec 1981, DOI 10.1109/TC.1981.1675742. Formula "D = 1 − Y^(1−F)" with "F = fault coverage", quoted in Nakamura, Savir and Fujiwara, *IEICE Trans. Inf. & Syst.* E88-D(6), 2005 (<https://archives.fujiwaralab.net/hideo/en/publication/J122_e_IEICE_2005_6.pdf>). [Established]
- **Classic papers (Crossref):** Roth, IBM J. R&D 10(4):278–291, 1966 (D-algorithm); Goel, IEEE Trans. Computers C-30(3):215–222, 1981 (PODEM); Fujiwara and Shimono, IEEE Trans. Computers C-32(12):1137–1144, 1983 (FAN); van de Goor, IEEE D&T 10(1):8–14, 1993 (March tests); Rajski, Tyszer, Kassab and Mukherjee, IEEE TCAD 23(5):776–792, 2004 (Embedded Deterministic Test); Eichelberger and Williams (LSSD) found only as the 1988 ACM reprint. [Established]
- **Compression:** Siemens TestKompress page (undated, read Nov 2026): "compressing scan patterns often 100X or more". Cadence Modus release, 2 Feb 2016 (EEJournal reprint <https://www.eejournal.com/industry_news/20160202-02/>): "up to 3X reduction in test time" and "compression ratios beyond 400X". Synopsys, 9 Sep 2013: DFTMAX Ultra "up to 3x higher compression" (relative to its previous compression). ITRS 2015 Test chapter (<https://www.semiconductors.org/wp-content/uploads/2018/06/0_2015-ITRS-2.0-Test-.pdf>): "factors of more than 100X compression are often achieved", and pattern compression "does little to help reduce test times and hence cost of testing". [Vendor claim; ITRS Industry estimate]
- **Test cost:** ITRS 2015: "lowered the typical cost of test as a percentage of IC revenue to less than 2-3%"; "Most SOC device are tested 2 to 16 at a time, and memory devices can have in excess of 1,000 devices tested at once"; "Improving quality of test has is much more beneficial economically than reducing cost of test." No IRDS or Heterogeneous Integration Roadmap figure found. [Industry estimate]
- **Silent data corruption:** Hochschild et al., "Cores that don't count," HotOS 2021 (<https://sigops.org/s/conferences/hotos/2021/papers/hotos21-s01-hochschild.pdf>): "on the order of a few mercurial cores per several thousand machines"; "often get worse with time"; "testing becomes part of the full lifecycle of a CPU". Dixit et al., arXiv 2102.11245 (2021): "hundreds of CPUs detected for these errors" across "hundreds of thousands of machines"; "these failures are reproducible and not transient". Dixit et al., arXiv 2203.08989 (2022): "the SDC occurrence rate of one in thousand silicon devices". [Established]
- **Automotive:** TI SLOA294A (rev. Apr 2024), "Table 2-1. Hardware Failure Metrics According to ISO 26262-5": ASIL B "≥90% ≥60%", ASIL C "≥97% ≥80%", ASIL D "≥99% ≥90%" (SPFM, LFM; in-field diagnostic coverage). TI SPRACI7A: "Device SRAMs are tested using March13n algorithm" at power-up "to detect latent faults". TI, WDSN 2007: "automotive devices set DPPM goals in single digits; even 'zero'". SemiEngineering, 8 Mar 2022: "While 10 Dppm used to be a solid benchmark, the bar is now 10 Dppb". AEC-Q100/AEC-Q004 and ISO 26262 texts not read. [Established via vendor reproduction; Industry estimate for DPPB]
- **Open-source DFT:** Fault (<https://github.com/AUCOHL/Fault>, commit `cf5509f`, version 0.9.4, Apache 2.0): "automatic test pattern generation (ATPG), scan stitching, static test vector compaction, and JTAG insertion"; "generates test vectors pseudo-randomly and performs fault simulations using the stuck-at-fault model". OpenROAD `openroad/dft/README.md` (commit `e5b41ea`): `scan_replace`, `execute_dft_plan` and chain reordering; no ATPG or compression. LibreLane (commit `8494d4f`): no scan-insertion step (inferred from the code). [Established]
- **March tests:** Microchip AVR8 SRAM March documentation: "March C-: {⇕(w0);⇑(r0,w1);⇑(r1,w0);⇓(r0,w1);⇓(r1,w0);⇕(r0)}", "The TL for March C- is 10*n", covering stuck-at, transition, address-decoder and coupling faults; "March B additionally covers some Linked Faults". Agrawal and Bushnell lecture 14alt: "MATS+ 5n ... MARCH C- 10n". [Established]
- **Scan overhead:** Agrawal and Bushnell lecture 23 (2001): "Gate overhead = [4 nsff/(ng+10nsff)] x 100% ... Example – ng = 100k gates, nsff = 2k flip-flops, overhead = 6.7%"; "Multiplexer delay added in combinational path; approx. two gate-delays"; s5378 full scan raises fault coverage from "70.0%" to "99.1%" at "15.66%" gate overhead. Teaching-era data; no modern-node figure found. [Established]

## R-321 · Functional verification study 2020–2026, SystemVerilog and UVM standards, emulation and prototyping claims, formal-verification citations, open verification tools, Pentium FDIV charge, mask-set cost, Zenbleed (Course 19)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent (curl, Crossref and shallow git clones pinned to commits; the full 2024 and 2026 Wilson PDFs are behind registration, accellera.org served a bot check, and intel.com and amd.com were not read; none was bypassed).*

- **2026 Wilson/Siemens study** [Industry estimate; the sponsor sells verification tools]: blog of 8 Sep 2026 (<https://blogs.sw.siemens.com/verificationhorizons/2026/09/08/the-2026-functional-verification-study/>): "Among IC/ASIC respondents, only 5% reported first-silicon success in 2026, compared with 14.4% in 2024"; "Logic and functional errors remain important and continue to be a major contributor to ASIC respins", with "reported contributions from firmware, safety, security, power, clocking, timing, and other system-level concerns". Verification Academy landing page: "functional verification accounts for an average of 49% of IC/ASIC project time and 51% of FPGA project time"; "Among 604 participants, 82.8% report use [of AI] beyond evaluation or pilot projects, yet only 9.0% describe AI as broadly integrated across the verification flow"; executive findings: "604 qualified participants: 320 working on IC/ASIC projects and 284 working on FPGA projects". Blog of 28 Oct 2026: "approximately three out of four IC/ASIC and FPGA projects are missing their original schedules". The sponsor notes a recruitment-methodology transition between the 2007–2022 and 2024–2026 studies and that outcomes "should not be read as independent causal claims".
- **Earlier studies:** 2022 (blog, 9 Jan 2023): "about 30% of all IC/ASIC projects" historically achieved first-silicon success, "only about 24 percent" in 2022; "logic or functional flaws" the leading cause. 2022 part 8: "about a one-to-one ratio in terms of mean peak number of verification and design engineers", "a 5-to-1 ratio" in processors; verification engineers grew at "a 6.2 percent CAGR" against 2.7 % for designers (2007–2022); designers spent "49 percent of their time in verification". 2022 part 10: "SystemVerilog Assertions (SVA) is the predominant assertion language in use today"; "the Accellera UVM is currently the predominant standard". 2020: "only about 32 percent". 2024 (Siemens abstract): "only 14 percent of projects achieving this milestone".
- **Standards** [Established]: IEEE SA, IEEE 1800-2023 "Active Standard", board approval 2023-12-06, published 2024-02-28, superseding 1800-2017 (<https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/1800/7743/>). IEEE 1800.2-2020 "Active Standard", published 2020-09-14, superseding 1800.2-2017 (<https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/1800.2/7567/>). Accellera `uvm-core` (GitHub, tag 2020.3.1, commit `78c0654`, 2024-09-03): "Accellera Universal Verification Methodology (UVM, IEEE 1800.2-2020)", "licensed under the Apache-2.0 license", "The implementation provided deviates from the 1800.2-2020 standard".
- **Emulation and prototyping** [Vendor claim]: Synopsys ZeBu Server 5 page: "Scalable Up To 30 Billion Gates"; ZeBu-200/HAPS-200 launch blog (13 Feb 2025): HAPS-200 "speeds of up to 10s of MHz", capacity "up to 10.8 BG", both on the AMD Versal Premium VP1902; Synopsys 2026 white paper: ZeBu-200 12-FPGA "up to 23 BG", HAPS-200 12-FPGA "up to 16.2 BG"; prototyping page customer quote: "The 50 MHz performance we have been able to achieve with HAPS-200". Cadence release of 18 Apr 2024 (design-reuse.com reprint): Palladium Z3 and Protium X3 "scale from job sizes of 16 million gates up to 48 billion gates", "a 1.5X performance increase", Protium X3 on the VP1902. Siemens Veloce CS release (20 Feb 2024): "from 40 million gates, up to designs integrating more than 40+ billion gates", Strato CS "up to 5x" over Strato, a purpose-built "Crystal" accelerator chip plus the VP1902. **NOT FOUND:** any primary vendor figure for emulator clock rate, or a comparable published figure for full-SoC RTL simulation speed; the chapter's 100 Hz and 1 MHz values are labelled illustrative (PROGRESS open issue).
- **Formal citations** (Crossref): Biere, Cimatti, Clarke and Zhu, "Symbolic Model Checking without BDDs," TACAS 1999, LNCS pp. 193–207, DOI 10.1007/3-540-49059-0_14. Bryant, "Graph-Based Algorithms for Boolean Function Manipulation," *IEEE Trans. Computers* C-35(8):677–691, 1986, DOI 10.1109/tc.1986.1676819. Sheeran, Singh and Stålmarck, "Checking Safety Properties Using Induction and a SAT-Solver," FMCAD 2000, LNCS pp. 127–144, DOI 10.1007/3-540-40922-x_8. Teaching slides consulted: University of Waterloo ECE725 BMC slides; TU Graz k-induction slides ("Property may be true, but not inductive").
- **Open tools** [Established]: Verilator (commit `9dee5c5`; `Changes`: "Verilator 5.054 2026-11-01"; README: "Compiles into multithreaded C++", and "may not be the best choice if you are expecting a full-featured replacement for a closed-source Verilog simulator"). cocotb 2.1.0 (PyPI, 2026-08-30, BSD-3-Clause): "a framework empowering users to write VHDL and Verilog testbenches in Python". SymbiYosys (tag v0.71, ISC): modes "``bmc``" and "``prove``"; "the proof may also yield an ``UNKNOWN`` result if an assertion can be made to fail during the induction step"; full SVA needs a commercial front end. EQY: "formal verification that two designs are equivalent, such as ensuring that a synthesis tool has not introduced functional changes".
- **Pentium FDIV charge** [Established, secondary sources]: wire report, 19 Jan 1995 (The Moscow Times archive): "set aside $475 million pre-tax for a replacement program", charged against fourth-quarter (1994) earnings; *Microprocessor Report* editorial, 6 Mar 1995: "the awesome $475 million price tag"; *Wall Street Journal*, 13 Apr 1995, reprint: "a $475 million charge to Intel's earnings". Intel's own annual report was not read.
- **Mask-set cost** [Industry estimate]: SemiAnalysis (24 Jul 2022): "With 7nm, the cost increases beyond $10M, and now, as we cross the 3nm barrier, mask sets will begin to push into the $40M range"; Siemens blog (3 Sep 2025): "At 3nm, a mask set costs tens of millions of dollars". No primary foundry price.
- **Zenbleed** [Established]: NVD record for CVE-2023-20593 (AMD PSIRT as CNA, published 2023-07-24): "An issue in "Zen 2" CPUs, under specific microarchitectural circumstances, may allow an attacker to potentially access sensitive information." Discoverer's summary in oss-security (24 Jul 2023): "a use-after-free in AMD Zen2 processors"; "The vector register file (RF) is a resource shared among all tasks on the same physical core"; workaround "set the chicken bit DE_CFG[9]", "the microcode update is preferred".
- **Coverage definitions:** Verilator documentation (line, branch, toggle, FSM state and arc, expression, covergroup coverage); Dobis et al., arXiv:2102.13460 (code vs functional coverage; bins, coverpoints, covergroups, crosses).

## R-322 · The gm/ID method and its tools, open-PDK device models, published RF CMOS f_T and self-gain, IRDS 2024 OSC, analog design automation, analog respin data (Course 20)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent with WebFetch and curl. IEEE Xplore, tsmc.com, cadence.com and the irds.ieee.org HTML pages refused automated access; IEEE abstracts were read through the OpenAlex and Crossref APIs (publisher abstracts), and the IRDS PDF downloaded directly. Verbatim IEEE quotes should be re-checked against Xplore when it is reachable.*

- **Origin of the method:** F. Silveira, D. Flandre, P. G. A. Jespers, "A gm/ID based methodology for the design of CMOS analog circuits and its application to the synthesis of a silicon-on-insulator micropower OTA," *IEEE JSSC* 31(9), pp. 1314–1319, Sept. 1996, DOI 10.1109/4.535416 (Crossref; author copy at people.engr.tamu.edu). Abstract: "The synthesis procedure is based on the relation between the ratio of the transconductance over dc drain current gm/ID and the normalized current ID/(W/L). The gm/ID indeed is a universal characteristic of all the transistors belonging to a same process." [Established]
- **Lookup-table book:** P. G. A. Jespers and B. Murmann, *Systematic Design of Analog CMOS Circuits: Using Pre-Computed Lookup Tables*, Cambridge University Press, 2017 (online 28 Sep, print 12 Oct 2017), DOI 10.1017/9781108125840. Free excerpt (assets.cambridge.org): the square-law error in $g_m$ "is of the order of 20–60%" in strong inversion; in moderate inversion it "may be in error by a factor of two or even more"; "moderate inversion represents a design 'sweet spot' for a variety of circuits in these technologies." Earlier monograph: P. G. A. Jespers, *The gm/ID Methodology*, Springer, 2010. [Established]
- **Open gm/ID tools** (GitHub, rendered commit pages): bmurmann/Book-on-gm-ID-design (AGPL-3.0; latest commit 6 Oct 2025; open-source starter files with lookup-table sweeps for `sky130/`, `ihp-sg13g2/` and `gf180mcuD/`, run in ngspice); dreoilin/pygmid (Apache-2.0; latest commit 26 Jan 2026; PyPI 1.2.12 of 28 Jul 2024; "a Python 3 version of the gm/ID starter kit"); medwatt/gmid "Mosplot" (Apache-2.0; latest commit 22 Sep 2026; "sizes complete circuits automatically across process corners"); ashwith/pyMOSChar (dormant since 30 Jun 2017); iic-jku/analog-circuit-design (Apache-2.0, gm/ID notebooks on IHP SG13G2, active 24 Nov 2026). Murmann's SKY130 README (edited 1 Jun 2025): "the pfet models look non-physical in moderate and weak inversion. These models should probably not be used for designs with VGS−Vt < 150 mV." [Established]
- **Open-PDK device models:** SKY130 `nfet_01v8`/`pfet_01v8` model cards (google/skywater-pdk-libs-sky130_fd_pr): "level = 54.0 / version = 4.5" (BSIM4), `lmin = 1.5e-07`, `toxe = 4.148e-09` (n) and `4.23e-09` (p), `epsrox = 3.9`; installed for ngspice by open_pdks. GF180MCU `nmos_3p3` (models/ngspice/sm141064.ngspice): BSIM4 4.5, `lmin = 2.8e-007`, `toxe = 8e-009`. [Established]
- **Published peak NMOS f_T / f_MAX** (abstracts via OpenAlex): C.-H. Jan et al., IEDM 2010: "The peak cut-off frequency (fT) doubles from 209 GHz in the 90 nm node to 445 GHz at the 32 nm node." M.-T. Yang et al., VLSI Technology 2011 (28 nm LP bulk): "peak fT/Fmax of 349/265GHz for NMOS". S. Ong et al., RFIC 2018 (22FDX): "The best performance for nFET/pFET fT is 347/275 GHz and for fMAX is 371/299 GHz." B. Sell et al., IEDM 2017 (Intel 22FFL): NMOS 230/284 GHz; H.-J. Lee et al., IEDM 2018: "ft and fmax of NMOS above 300 GHz and 450 GHz". S. Jagar et al., *IEEE TED* 65(1), 2018 (GF 14 nm FinFET): 314/180 GHz (single-side gate contact). A. Razavieh et al., *IEEE JEDS* 9, 2021 (12 nm): "FT/FMAX of 305GHz/ 315GHz". S. S. Parihar et al., *IEEE JEDS* 11, 2023 (5 nm): "a single gate contacted n-FinFET RF device exhibits a cutoff and maximum oscillation frequency of ~100 GHz and ~170 GHz". Foundry RF figures for 6/5/4/3 nm were NOT CONFIRMED. [Established] per data point; the "plateau" reading is the chapter's [Industry estimate].
- **IRDS 2024 Outside System Connectivity** (2024IRDS_OSC.pdf): no CMOS f_T/f_MAX table; "Other MOSFET high-frequency figures of merit, such as fMAX and NFMIN, which are strongly dependent on the device layout used in particular circuit blocks have been removed from the CMOS section of the OSC tables"; CMOS peak f_T is shown only in Fig. OSC-13 (TCAD, "calibrated on recent averaged measured data for the 28 nm, 22 nm, and 16 nm nodes"). [Established as a roadmap]
- **Intrinsic gain:** Jagar et al. (above): "a significantly higher self-gain (Gm/Gds) 40 and 34 for both NFinFET and PFinFET" (bias and length not in the abstract). E.-Y. Jeong et al., VLSI Technology 2017 (Samsung): "the optimized 14nm RF FinFET consumes 63% of DC power with 53% of device active area and 3.8 times higher intrinsic gain (gm/gds)" than its 28 nm planar counterpart. Annema et al., *IEEE JSSC* 40(1), 2005, and Subramanian et al., *IEEE TED* 53(12), 2006, give the qualitative trends. Textbook "intrinsic gain by node" numbers NOT CONFIRMED. [Established, single-company data]
- **Analog design automation:** Synopsys blog (30 Sep 2024): "Credo migrated the VCO design from 5nm to 7nm using Synopsys ASO.ai, reducing the migration efforts from multiple weeks to just a few hours, including setup offering up to 100X productivity improvement" [Vendor claim]. Cadence Virtuoso Studio launch (19 Apr 2023, via Design-Reuse): "more than a 3X improvement in design throughput for today's largest designs" [Vendor claim]. BAG2 (Chang et al., CICC 2018): one code base generated circuits in "TSMC 28nm, TSMC 16nm, GLOBALFOUNDRIES 45nm RF-SOI, ST 28nm FD-SOI, and GLOBALFOUNDRIES 22nm FDX". MAGICAL 1.0 (Chen et al., CICC 2021, accepted manuscript on NSF PAR): "it only takes around 90 seconds for layout synthesis"; "The measured SNDR difference is within 2dB compared to the manual design" (repository dormant since Sep 2021). ALIGN (Poojary et al., RFIC 2023): layouts "generated automatically in hours compared to weeks for a single manual layout" (repository active, Sep 2026). [Established] for the papers.
- **Analog respins** (Siemens EDA / Wilson Research Group, Verification Horizons blog): 2020 study (3 Feb 2021): "in 2020 we saw a huge increase in flaws attributed to tuning analog circuits (41 percent)"; 2022 study (9 Jan 2023): "the issue repeated itself in our 2022 study". The 2026 methodology paper notes a recruitment change between the 2007–2022 and 2024–2026 studies; the 2024 and 2026 analog shares are in gated reports, NOT CONFIRMED. [Industry estimate: vendor-sponsored survey, multiple answers allowed]
- **Analog share of SoC area or engineering cost:** no primary or analyst figure found for 2023–2026; NOT CONFIRMED.

## R-323 · Op-amp compensation, gain boosting, CMFB and loop-gain literature (Course 20)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent. IEEE Xplore refused automated access; IEEE abstracts were read through the Crossref and OpenAlex APIs (publisher abstracts) and, where available, author or university repository copies.*

- **Indirect compensation:** B. K. Ahuja, "An improved frequency compensation technique for CMOS operational amplifiers," *IEEE JSSC* SC-18(6), pp. 629–633, Dec. 1983, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.1983.1052012. Abstract: the RC-compensated two-stage op-amp has a limited capacitive-load range and its supply rejection "shows severe degradation above the open-loop pole frequency"; the new technique gives a much wider load range and better negative-supply rejection; measured 70 dB negative-supply rejection at 100 kHz and 58 nV/√Hz at 1 kHz (n-well CMOS). The abstract does not describe the circuit; the cascode/current-buffer description is the later literature's.
- **PSRR of Miller OTAs:** M. S. J. Steyaert and W. M. C. Sansen, "Power supply rejection ratio in operational transconductance amplifiers," *IEEE Trans. Circuits Syst.* 37(9), pp. 1077–1084, Sep. 1990, DOI 10.1109/31.57596: the single-stage OTA's PSRR is "one order of magnitude better than the commonly used Miller OTA". Supporting: TI SLOA011B (Karki), §5.11: datasheet PSRR is a DC parameter that "falls off as the frequency increases".
- **Gain boosting:** K. Bult and G. J. G. M. Geelen, "A fast-settling CMOS op amp for SC circuits with 90-dB DC gain," *IEEE JSSC* 25(6), pp. 1379–1384, 1990, DOI 10.1109/4.62165. Abstract: 1.6 µm CMOS; 90 dB DC gain and 116 MHz unity-gain frequency into 16 pF; single-pole settling at feedback factor 1/3 with 18 MHz closed-loop bandwidth into 35 pF and better than 0.03 % accuracy; 5 V supply, about 4.2 V swing; boosting costs 30 % area and 15 % power. Absolute power and settling time in ns are not in the abstract.
- **Nested Miller:** R. G. H. Eschauzier, L. P. T. Kerklaan and J. H. Huijsing, "A 100-MHz 100-dB operational amplifier with multipath nested Miller compensation structure," *IEEE JSSC* 27(12), pp. 1709–1717, Dec. 1992, DOI 10.1109/4.173096 (read from the TU Delft repository copy). A **bipolar** (all-npn) design: 100 dB, 100 MHz unity-gain bandwidth, 40° phase margin into 100 pF, 0.1 % settling in 60 ns for a 1 V step; plain NMC 60 MHz and 70 ns. Its eqs. (1)–(3) state the Butterworth design rule (stages numbered from the output); relabelled input-first it is C_m1 = 4g_m1C_L/g_m3, C_m2 = 2g_m2C_L/g_m3. Review: K. N. Leung and P. K. T. Mok, "Analysis of multistage amplifier-frequency compensation," *IEEE TCAS-I* 48(9), pp. 1041–1056, Sep. 2001, DOI 10.1109/81.948432 (metadata confirmed; equations not read).
- **Ring amplifiers:** B. Hershberg et al., "Ring amplifiers for switched capacitor circuits," *IEEE JSSC* 47(12), pp. 2928–2942, Dec. 2012, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2012.2217865: a 15-bit pipelined ADC in 0.18 µm CMOS with 76.8 dB SNDR and 95.4 dB SFDR at 20 MS/s, 5.1 mW, 45 fJ/conversion-step; a 10.5-bit ring-amplifier-only ADC at 61.5 dB SNDR. ISSCC 2012 version DOI 10.1109/ISSCC.2012.6177090.
- **Switched-capacitor CMFB:** D. Senderowicz et al., "A family of differential NMOS analog circuits for a PCM codec filter chip," *IEEE JSSC* SC-17(6), pp. 1014–1023, Dec. 1982 (abstract does not mention CMFB); US Patent 4,574,250 (Senderowicz, Intel; filed 13 Oct 1981): a switched-capacitor sampler that "serves as a common mode feedback"; R. Castello and P. R. Gray, "A high-performance micropower switched-capacitor filter," *IEEE JSSC* SC-20(6), pp. 1122–1132, Dec. 1985, whose abstract names "switched-capacitor common-mode feedback".
- **Loop-gain measurement:** R. D. Middlebrook, "Measurement of loop gain in feedback systems," *Int. J. Electronics* 38(4), pp. 485–512, Apr. 1975, DOI 10.1080/00207217508920421 (voltage and current injection without opening the loop, combined when neither alone suffices); M. Tian, V. Visvanathan, J. Hantgan and K. Kundert, "Striving for small-signal stability," *IEEE Circuits and Devices Magazine* 17(1), pp. 31–41, Jan. 2001, DOI 10.1109/101.900125 (a two-port loop method including reverse transmission, plus device-based gain nulling). NOT CONFIRMED and not used: the closed-form double-injection combination formula; which commercial simulators implement Tian's method.

## R-324 · Published figures of merit of multistage CMOS OTAs (Course 20)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent from the open-access accepted version of Grasso et al. (TCAS-II 2018), Table IV, and from the cited papers' abstracts (OpenAlex).*

- Many papers define FOM_S = GBW·C_L/Power (MHz·pF/mW) and FOM_L = SR·C_L/Power; current-normalized versions are often called IFOM_S/IFOM_L. The course computes $f_uC_L/I$ (MHz·pF/mA) from each paper's reported GBW, load and current.
- From Grasso, Marano, Palumbo and Pennisi, "High-performance three-stage single-Miller CMOS OTA with no upper limit of C_L," *IEEE TCAS-II* 65(11), pp. 1529–1533, Nov. 2018, DOI 10.1109/TCSII.2017.2756923, Table IV (GBW, C_L, current): Tan & Ki (JSSC Feb 2015) 3.37 MHz, 680 pF, 10.5 µA; Marano et al. (TCAS-I Sep 2016) 2.72 MHz, 10 nF, 24.49 µA; Qu et al. (JSSC Feb 2017) 1.18 MHz, 18 nF, 58 µA; Grasso et al. 0.91 MHz at 30 nF, 1.70 MHz at 10 nF, 0.43 MHz at 100 nF, 6.36 µA. Qin et al., "Dual AC boosting compensation scheme for multistage amplifiers," *IEEE TCAS-II* 64(8), pp. 882–886, Aug. 2017 (abstract): 0.52 MHz into 3200 pF, 60 µW from ±0.8 V (37.5 µA).
- Computed $f_uC_L/I$: 4.4 × 10⁴ (Qin) to 6.8 × 10⁶ MHz·pF/mA (Grasso, 100 nF). Caveats: Tan & Ki's own abstract gives 560 pF and 3.49 MHz, not the table's 680 pF and 3.37 MHz; Marano's reported FOMs differ by about 2 % from the table's own inputs.

## R-326 · Bandgap reference literature, recent CMOS references, ngspice BJT temperature parameters (Course 20)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent. ieeexplore.ieee.org and analog.com were unreachable; IEEE abstracts were read through Crossref and OpenAlex, Tsividis (1980) from a publicly posted copy (page images), and ngspice from the official SourceForge files.*

- **Foundations:** R. J. Widlar, "New developments in IC voltage regulators," *IEEE JSSC* SC-6(1), pp. 2–7, Feb. 1971 (a temperature-compensated reference "with numerous advantages over zener diodes"); K. E. Kuijk, "A precision reference voltage source," *IEEE JSSC* SC-8(3), pp. 222–226, June 1973 (output temperature-independent when the sum "equals the gap voltage of silicon"; a 10 V reference, −250 µV over ±10 K, 10 ppm/month); A. P. Brokaw, "A simple three-terminal IC bandgap reference," *IEEE JSSC* SC-9(6), pp. 388–393, Dec. 1974 (collector-current sensing, 2.5 V, 5 ppm/°C over "the military temperature range").
- **Tsividis 1980:** "Accurate analysis of temperature effects in I_C–V_BE characteristics with application to bandgap reference sources," *IEEE JSSC* SC-15(6), pp. 1076–1084, Dec. 1980, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.1980.1051519. Eq. (12) gives V_BE(T) with the nonlinear gap V_G(T) and η ≡ 4 − n; the extrapolated V_G0r = 1.20595 V (T_r ≥ 300 K); fitted η for five processes 2.330, 2.303, 2.286, 2.316, 2.405; fitting the linear-bandgap form (eq. 38) instead needs "fictitious" η = 3.429 and V_G0 = 1.1761 V (process V). Eq. (14): the zero-TC output is V_G0r + (η − θ_r)kT_r/q (θ_r = 1 for PTAT bias); the course evaluates it as 1.239–1.242 V at 300 K.
- **Sub-1 V:** H. Banba et al., "A CMOS bandgap reference circuit with sub-1-V operation," *IEEE JSSC* 34(5), pp. 670–674, May 1999, DOI 10.1109/4.760378: 0.4 µm flash process; conventional V_ref "about 1.25 V"; measured 518 ± 15 mV (3σ), 23 samples, 27–125 °C (the 1998 VLSI version says 515 mV). Minimum supply, TC and current NOT CONFIRMED (not in the abstract).
- **Recent CMOS references (abstracts):** G. Ge, C. Zhang, G. Hoogzaad, K. A. A. Makinwa, *IEEE JSSC* 46(11), pp. 2693–2701, Nov. 2011: ±0.15 % (3σ), −40 to 125 °C, single trim; residual error "mainly PTAT, and can be removed by a single room temperature trim"; 55 µA from 1.8 V, 0.16 µm. I. Lee, D. Sylvester, D. Blaauw, *IEEE JSSC* 52(5), pp. 1443–1449, May 2017: 35 pW from 1.4 V, ±1.0 % (3σ) 0–100 °C after one trim, 0.18 µm. Y. Ji et al., *IEEE JSSC* 54(12), pp. 3281–3291, Dec. 2019: 192 pW; untrimmed 0.53 %, 0.020 %/V and 33 ppm/°C over 45 chips. U. Kamath et al., *IEEE JSSC* 54(7), pp. 1830–1840, July 2019: 1 V bandgap in 7 nm FinFET, ±0.2 % and 6 ppm/°C from −45 to 125 °C. J.-H. Boo et al., *IEEE JSSC* 56(4), pp. 1197–1206, Apr. 2021: +0.02 %/−0.12 % (3σ), −40 to 125 °C, single trim, 17 µA from 1.8 V. K. Chen et al., *IEEE JSSC* 56(1), pp. 267–276, Jan. 2021: 1.16 V, ±8.2 mV across seven untrimmed samples (±0.71 %).
- **ngspice:** User's Manual v47 (Aug. 2026), §7.3.3: Gummel–Poon EG default 1.11 eV, XTI default 3, TNOM 27 °C; source bjttemp.c: IS(T) = IS (T/T_nom)^XTI exp[qEG(T − T_nom)/(kT T_nom)], the linear-bandgap form with EG as V_g0 and XTI as η.

## R-327 · Commercial voltage references and the box-method TC (Course 20)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent from Texas Instruments data sheets (ti.com/lit) and an Analog Applications Journal article. analog.com was unreachable, so no ADI parts were read.*

- **REF5025** (REF50xx, SBOS410O, rev. Oct 2025): ±0.05 % (I grade) / ±0.1 % (AI); 3 ppm/°C (I) / 8 ppm/°C (AI) max, −40 to 125 °C; 3 µVpp/V (0.1–10 Hz); 0.8 mA typ; long-term drift 22 ppm (0–1000 h) and 18 ppm (1000–2000 h), SOIC-8. REF5025E: ±0.025 %, 2.5 ppm/°C, 0.5 µVpp/V, 340 µA typ.
- **REF7025** (REF70, SNAS781G, rev. Sep 2023): ±0.025 %, 2 ppm/°C (−40 to 125 °C), 0.23 ppm p-p noise, 4 mA typ; "Every unit shipped is tested at multiple temperatures."
- **REF3425** (REF34xx, SBAS804G, rev. Apr 2026): ±0.05 %, 6 ppm/°C max (−40 to 125 °C), 5 µVpp/V (the front-page 3.8 µVpp/V applies to the 4.096 V and 5 V options), 72 µA typ, drift 25 ppm (0–1000 h).
- **REF35250** (REF35, SNAS809C, rev. Sep 2024): ±0.05 %, 12 ppm/°C (−40 to 105 °C, SOT-23), 0.65 µA typ, 3.8 ppm p-p noise for V_REF ≥ 2.5 V (front-page 3.3 ppm applies below 2.5 V), drift 40 ppm (0–1000 h).
- **Box method:** P. Miller and D. Moore, "Precision voltage references," TI *Analog Applications Journal*, SLYT183, Nov. 1999: TC = (V_MAX − V_MIN)/(V_nominal × (T_MAX − T_MIN)) × 10⁶; "the box method is most commonly used"; it "does not specify the exact shape and slope". The same article (1999): bandgap references "typically have an initial error of 0.5–1.0% and a TC of 25–50 ppm/°C" (trimmed status not stated).

## R-328 · LDO literature: classic, capacitor-less, digital and recent high-PSRR designs (Course 20)

*Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent. ieeexplore.ieee.org was not used; IEEE abstracts were read through OpenAlex and cross-checked against Crossref for volume, issue, pages and author spellings. Numbers are as stated in the abstracts.*

- **Rincón-Mora & Allen**, "A low-voltage, low quiescent current, low drop-out regulator," *IEEE JSSC* 33(1), pp. 36–44, Jan. 1998, DOI 10.1109/4.654935: operates down to 1 V input; 23 µA zero-load quiescent current; 18 mA at 1 V and 50 mA at 1.2 V input; exploits the load dependence of the frequency response to minimize quiescent current. Process, dropout and PSRR are not in the abstract.
- **Hazucha et al.**, "Area-efficient linear regulator with ultra-fast load regulation," *IEEE JSSC* 40(4), pp. 933–940, Apr. 2005, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2004.842831 (VLSI Symp. 2004 version, DOI 10.1109/VLSIC.2004.1346565): 90 nm; 1.2 V in, 0.9 V out; 0.54 ns response time at 94 % current efficiency; 90 mV peak-to-peak droop for a 100 mA step with 0.6 nF on-chip; 0.2 V minimum dropout at 100 mA; regulator 0.008 mm², capacitor 0.090 mm². Response time T_R = CΔV/ΔI (definition attributed to this paper by L. He et al., *Sensors* 22(23), 9367, 2022, DOI 10.3390/s22239367; 0.6 nF × 90 mV/100 mA = 0.54 ns matches exactly). Medium confidence for the definition. **UNVERIFIED:** the origin of the quiescent-current-weighted FoM T_R·I_Q/ΔI; FoM conventions differ between papers, so published FoMs are not recomputed here.
- **Milliken, Silva-Martínez, Sánchez-Sinencio**, "Full on-chip CMOS low-dropout voltage regulator," *IEEE TCAS-I* 54(9), pp. 1879–1890, Sep. 2007, DOI 10.1109/TCSI.2007.902615: no external capacitor; stable from 0 to 50 mA with up to 100 pF load; 2.8 V from 3 V; 0.35 µm; 65 µA ground current; 200 mV dropout.
- **Okuma et al.**, "0.5-V input digital LDO with 98.7% current efficiency and 2.7-µA quiescent current in 65nm CMOS," *Proc. IEEE CICC*, Sep. 2010, DOI 10.1109/CICC.2010.5617586: 0.5 V in, 0.45 V out; 98.7 % current efficiency and 2.7 µA quiescent current at 200 µA load; op-amp LDOs "fail to operate at 0.5V". Widely cited as the first digital LDO; not confirmed by an exhaustive survey (digitally controlled linear regulators predate it), so "first" is not claimed in the chapter.
- **Capacitor-less classics:** K. N. Leung & P. K. T. Mok, *IEEE JSSC* 38(10), pp. 1691–1702, 2003, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2003.817256 (1.5 V, 100 mA, 0.6 µm; PSRR −30 dB at 1 MHz). J. Guo & K. N. Leung, *IEEE JSSC* 45(9), pp. 1896–1905, 2010, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2010.2053859 (6 µW at 0.75 V, 90 nm; stable for 0–50 pF; 100 mA; <66 mV over/undershoot for full-load steps with 100 ns edges).
- **High PSRR:** J. Jiang, W. Shu, J. S. Chang, *IEEE JSSC* 53(8), pp. 2331–2342, 2018, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2018.2837044 (65 nm; >60 dB PSRR over 10 MHz and a 100 mA load range; feed-forward ripple cancellation; 80 mV dropout). Y. Lim et al., *IEEE JSSC* 53(9), pp. 2675–2685, 2018, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2018.2841984 (65 nm; capacitor-less, 240 pF; PSR better than −36 dB at all frequencies from 10 kHz to 1 GHz).
- **Recent fast capacitor-less / hybrid:** F. Chen, Y. Lu, P. K. T. Mok, *IEEE JSSC* 56(2), pp. 511–520, Feb. 2021, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2020.3015527 (65 nm; 500 mA; 250 pF on chip; 120 µA quiescent; 55 mV undershoot for a 450 mA step with 100 ns edge; 30 µV/mA load regulation). H. Park et al., *IEEE JSSC* 58(10), pp. 2696–2708, 2023, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2023.3279843 (180 nm; 0.1–300 mA with >60° PM; 100 pF; 48 mV undershoot for a 299 mA step). C. An et al., *IEEE JSSC* 61(5), pp. 2215–2226, May 2026, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2025.3602461 (28 nm; −53.7 dB PSRR at 10 kHz and 100 mA; 54 mV droop for a 99 mA step; 338.5 µA).

Jun 2023): 1 A; dropout 150 mV typ / 240 mV max at 1 A; ground current 15 mA typ at 0.1 mA load; PSRR 110 dB at 1 kHz (front page, headline conditions) and 51 dB at 1 MHz (EC table, 3.3 V out, 750 mA); noise 0.46 µVrms (10 Hz–100 kHz, 1.2 V out, 1 A); C_OUT 4.7–1000 µF.

Lee, "Understanding the terms and definitions of LDO voltage regulators," TI SLVA079, Oct. 1999 (dropout, I_q = I_i − I_o, efficiency I_oV_o/[(I_o + I_q)V_i], line and load regulation as steady-state parameters, PSRR "at all frequencies", a stable ESR window, e.g. 0.2–9 Ω). J. C. S. Pithadia, S. Lester, A. Verma, "LDO PSRR measurement simplified," TI SLAA414A, Jul 2009, rev. Aug 2017. TI SNVA020B (AN-1148), "Linear regulators: theory of operation and compensation," rev. *Read on 24 Nov 2026 by a research subagent. IEEE abstracts were read through OpenAlex and cross-checked against Crossref; Razavi's Solid-State Circuits Magazine columns were read in full from the author's UCLA pages. ieeexplore.ieee.org and analog.com were not used.*

- **StrongARM latch:** T. Kobayashi, K. Nogami, T. Shirotori, Y. Fujimoto, *IEEE JSSC* 28(4), pp. 523–527, Apr. 1993, DOI 10.1109/4.210039 (VLSI Symp. June 1992, pp. 28–29, "current-mode latch sense amplifier", DOI 10.1109/VLSIC.1992.229252; SRAM sense amplifier that stops sense current automatically). J. Montanaro et al., "A 160-MHz, 32-b, 0.5-W CMOS RISC microprocessor," *IEEE JSSC* 31(11), pp. 1703–1714, Nov. 1996, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.1996.542315: the abstract does not mention the latch; its use there is from Razavi (Medium). B. Razavi, "The StrongARM latch," *IEEE SSC Magazine* 7(2), pp. 12–17, Spring 2015, DOI 10.1109/MSSC.2015.2418155: zero static power, rail-to-rail outputs, offset mainly from one pair; amplification-phase gain ≈ g_m V_THN/I_CM; M3/M4 mismatch divided by about 4 and M5/M6 by about 10; the M1–M2 noise term "is typically four to eight times greater than" the precharge kT/C term; power ≈ f_CK(2C_PQ + C_XY)V_DD². B. Razavi, "The design of a comparator," *IEEE SSC Magazine* 12(4), pp. 8–14, Fall 2020, DOI 10.1109/MSSC.2020.3021865: 28 nm simulated example, τ_reg = 2.5 ps, noise ≈ 0.31 mV rms, offset 4.7 mV, ≈ 0.2 mW at 5 GHz; A_VTH ≈ 2.2 mV·µm.
- **Double-tail and dynamic-preamplifier comparators:** D. Schinkel et al., *ISSCC* 2007, paper 17.7, pp. 314–315, DOI 10.1109/ISSCC.2007.373420 (90 nm; σ offset 8 mV; 92 fJ/decision at 1.2 V; noise 1.5 mV; 18 ps setup+hold; 44 ps/decade delay sensitivity from an OCR'd full text, Medium). Tang et al., *IEEE JSSC* 55(4), pp. 1011–1022, Apr. 2020, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2019.2960485 (FIA; 46 µV at 1 pJ, 180 nm; >7× energy efficiency vs a StrongARM). H. Zhuang, Y. Cao, L. Tao, Q. Li, *IEEE JSSC* 60(3), pp. 1056–1069, Mar. 2025, DOI 10.1109/JSSC.2024.3446175 (38 µV at 0.48 pJ, 28 nm; noise-efficiency factor 0.69 nJ·µV², "23 times better than the classic StrongArm latch"). A.
